#10741. Magic: The Gathering is getting a Netflix animated series. For real this time.

Planeswalker is set to debut on Netflix in 2027.

Planeswalker is set to debut on Netflix in 2027.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

“It’s really happening. I promise. For real.” Those are the first words from Roy Graham, head of story for Magic: The Gathering, in a video posted yesterday announcing Planeswalker, the animated series heading to Netflix in 2027.

An animated Magic: The Gathering series was first teased back in 2019 with Joe and Anthony Russo at the helm. But that fell apart. In 2024, Netflix announced it still planned to make a series based on the game, but it decided to rework it from the ground up.

Now fans are finally getting their first glimpse of the show, which will be called Planeswalker. According to the sneak peek, because each plane exists in its own pocket universe, it will have a unique design, animation, and filmmaking style.

According to a synopsis of the show shared with IGN, Planeswalker will focus on Chandra Nalaar, a “volatile pyromancer hunted for her gifts, and haunted by her past.” Storyline details beyond that are vague, other than mentioning an “insidious force” that Chandra and other Planeswalkers will team up to confront “to prevent a cataclysmic war that threatens to consume their very existence.”

SpaceX's Elon Musk and Epic Games' Tim Sweeney are two of Huang's biggest cheerleaders in an attempt to get him his job back.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Just days after he left the company under a cloud of extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang might be on his way back to the company he founded. That's after a number of his allies rallied in support for Huang, including influential SpaceX CEO Elon Musk and outspoken Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney. The pair are just two of those demanding that Nvidia's board has a rethink, hoping that it will choose to "Bring Jensen Back" to the company. Huang is now discussing that possibility, according to a Financial Times report.

"Huang has discussed with confidantes the possibility of returning, according to multiple people familiar with the matter," the report claimed, adding that allies were putting together a "war room" of Silicon Valley figures to try and lobby for his return.

Huang's resignation from his Nvidia post came after he released a statement confirming his affair, one which could have repercussions far beyond Nvidia itself. U.S. intelligence was reported to believe that the woman involved in an affair with Jensen Huang, Alice Huang (no relation), is a spy working for the Chinese Ministry of State Security, or MSS. Ms. Huang was an executive at Megaspeed, an AI company accused of smuggling $2 billion worth of AI chips into China via a subsidiary called Speedmatrix. Since Jensen Huang admitted to his adultery, it was further alleged that he had a second affair, this time with an Nvidia executive who reported to him. That alone would potentially constitute a violation of Nvidia's own code of conduct. But the affair with an alleged Chinese spy is likely to be the most troublesome for the former-but-potentially-returning CEO.

Now, an open letter sent to Nvidia's board by Huang's allies has urged them to leave “all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang.” The open letter was penned by Altimeter Capital founder Brad Gerstner and Atreides Management boss Gavin Baker, both shareholders.

@yunta_tsai A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I’m signing it tooNovember 13, 2026

However, Nvidia has already started looking elsewhere. After placing chief financial officer Colette Kress in charge on an interim basis, it's believed the company has approached Arm chief executive Rene Haas as a potential Huang successor. Haas spent around seven years at Nvidia before leaving in 2013, but no official confirmation has been announced, nor is it likely to be until a decision is made.

It's possible that Huang can expect a vote of confidence from U.S. President Donald Trump, with the pair having a good relationship to date. But Trump recently called for former Apple CEO Tim Cook to take over at Nvidia.

Outside of the White House, Huang has other concerns to deal with. Congress has summoned him to appear before a Select Committee over his affair with the suspected Chinese spy, threatening to subpoena him if necessary.

#10737. What Actually Happens When a Kafka Region Fails?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most Kafka deployments in the industry plan to survive the loss of a single Kafka broker or an availability zone. But that's not enough for critical Tier-0 infrastructure where downtime or data loss of even a few seconds could have a massive business impact.

In such scenarios, the failure model should consider not just the loss of a broker or a zone, but the loss of an entire region. The business is expected to operate without interruption even when a whole region goes down, and it should be possible to also roll the cluster safely while operating in that state, to apply any break-glass changes.

But what actually happens inside Kafka when an entire region goes down? What happens to the replicas, producer writes, and KRaft quorum? And what happens when that region comes back?

Let's walk through these scenarios using a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka architecture.

For a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka cluster, we need three different regions with isolated failure domains and a minimum of two brokers in each region.

Brokers in each region should be configured with rack awareness to indicate their failure domains as follows:

For Tier-0 workloads, each topic's partitions should be configured to have a replication factor (RF) of 6. Kafka's rack-aware replica assignment will spread the replicas across the three regions. If the automatic replica assignment doesn't allocate two replicas per region, we have to explicitly reassign replicas immediately after topic creation to meet our availability guarantees.

This way, if one region goes down entirely, we still have four replicas surviving and the cluster would still continue to operate.

But just running brokers in multiple regions is not the solution here. In fact, that's the easy part. The hard question is, what guarantee remains when one of those regions disappears, and does it solve the business needs?

The two brokers in that region become unavailable, leaving four surviving replicas across Regions B and C.

For any partitions whose leaders were in Region A, Kafka needs to elect new leaders from the surviving in-sync replicas.

But having four surviving replicas alone does not determine whether Kafka will continue accepting writes.

The next step is to define what is considered a successful write in this Kafka setup. For this, we need two configs:

min.insync.replicas determines the minimum number of replicas that should be present in the in-sync replica set (ISR) of a partition for Kafka to accept writes when producers use acks=all.

acks=all is a producer-side config that ensures that the producer will get an acknowledgment only when the write has been successfully replicated to the required in-sync replicas.

These two settings work together to satisfy the durability guarantees we want.

When all three regions are healthy, ISR would include all six replicas. Let's say one region goes down, then the ISR would drop down to four.

Since minISR is three, writes will still continue to work.

There may be temporary interruptions while Kafka detects the failure, elects new leaders, and clients reconnect to them. But once the surviving leaders are available, the cluster can continue accepting writes as long as the ISR requirements are met.

The goal isn't availability at any cost, but rather the system should be available as long as the durability guarantees that we designed for can be maintained.

Now let's make the scenario more interesting.

Region A is still down. We have four replicas surviving across Regions B and C.

What if we need to roll the cluster one broker at a time, to apply any break-glass changes?

During that time, the ISR can go down from four to three. But since minISR is three, writes will still continue to work.

Let's look at how the cluster behaves under different failure scenarios.

Kafka will stop accepting writes only when the surviving ISR drops below three.

This is an important distinction for Tier-0 workloads.

It's not sufficient to design a cluster that survives a region failure. We also need to consider whether the cluster can continue operating safely while already in that degraded state.

So far, we focused on the data plane. Now switching gears, the KRaft controllers have a completely different availability model.

They form the metadata quorum, and a majority is needed to continue operation.

For our setup, five controllers could be distributed as 2 + 2 + 1 across the three regions, requiring a majority of three to maintain quorum.

If either of the regions containing two controllers goes down, three controllers remain. If the region containing one controller goes down, four remain.

So, the controller quorum survives the loss of any one region.

But, unlike brokers, we cannot necessarily roll the controllers at that point because taking one controller down could cause the quorum to drop below three.

With a static quorum, one way to solve this is to use more controllers, and a 3 + 3 + 3 distribution would provide this. Losing one region would leave six controllers with room to still roll the controllers, as we only need five to maintain quorum.

On the flip side, an increase in quorum size also increases the coordination overhead. The official Kafka documentation typically recommends three or five controllers, which tolerate one or two concurrent failures respectively.

Alternatively, with dynamic KRaft quorum (KIP-853), introduced in Kafka 3.9, we can add or remove controllers from the voting quorum without restarting the cluster.

For example, with our 2 + 2 + 1 setup, if a region containing two controllers goes down, the remaining three controllers can still maintain quorum. We can then remove the failed controllers from the voting membership, one at a time, reducing the quorum size to three. This allows us to roll one of the surviving controllers while still maintaining the new majority of two.

However, dynamic quorum reconfiguration is not automatic. The surviving controllers must be able to maintain quorum and successfully commit the membership changes before we can proceed with the controller restart.

You need to consider whether you want the same availability guarantee for the controllers as that of the data plane, and whether dynamic quorum reconfiguration during a regional outage is acceptable for your Tier-0 workloads.

So far, we assumed that a region either works or goes down completely.

But the WAN itself becomes a failure mode.

In this setup, a partly working WAN between two regions can be more dangerous than a link that's completely down.

Increased latency, packet loss, or intermittent connectivity issues between regions can cause replication to lag, ultimately resulting in replicas falling out of ISR.

The three regions might be independently healthy, but if the connectivity between them is not, then the availability of the cluster might be impacted.

Going back to the CAP theorem, to tolerate a network partition, we must choose between consistency and availability.

So, if a partition's ISR falls below the configured min.insync.replicas of three due to a network partition, Kafka stops accepting writes with acks=all rather than weakening those guarantees.

Similarly, if the KRaft controller quorum is lost, then no metadata changes, like leader elections, can happen in the cluster, even though existing data-plane traffic may continue for some time.

The availability guarantees we discussed also come with their own costs.

The first cost is client latency. With cross-region replication and acks=all, the network latency between these regions directly affects producer latency. Consumers can also pay the WAN latency cost when consuming from partition leaders located in a different region.

The second cost is replication overhead. With RF=6, there is additional storage, network traffic, and replication-related coordination cost in the brokers compared to a smaller number of replicas.

So, in an architecture like this, monitoring the health of the network paths between these regions is as critical as monitoring the Kafka brokers and controllers.

Metrics like round-trip latency (RTT) between regions, packet loss, TCP retransmissions, connection errors/timeouts, and network throughput/bandwidth utilization are a good start.

When one region goes down, all the partition leaders in that region would move to the two other surviving regions.

This implies that those two regions would have to handle more producer and consumer traffic, network throughput, and disk I/O.

Even if the cluster is designed to satisfy its ISR and controller quorum requirements, if it's not sized to handle these failures when one region goes down, the surviving regions would be overloaded, resulting in higher latencies, timeouts, and operational instability.

So, for Tier-0 infrastructure, capacity planning should always be done for failure states and not stop at normal operations.

Now let's say Region A comes back online.

The brokers in the recovered region might have to catch up on a significant amount of data before they become fully in sync again.

This can result in a sudden burst of cross-region network traffic and disk I/O across all regions.

Region recovery needs to be controlled so that it doesn't overwhelm the healthy part of the cluster.

This can be achieved by throttling the replica catch-up, with the trade-off of longer recovery time to minimize the impact on live production traffic.

Replication throttling needs a deeper discussion of its own, so we won't go into details here.

The important point is that bringing a failed region back online is not simply a matter of restarting its brokers. We need to make sure that the recovery process itself doesn't impact the business.

Before putting a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka cluster into production, validate these scenarios:

There is no shortage of ways to deploy Kafka across multiple regions. The challenge here is to pick the right architecture that addresses all our durability guarantees.

For Tier-0 workloads, this means designing the architecture around failures first.

What happens when a region goes down? Can we still roll the cluster? What continues to work, and at what point do we deliberately stop accepting writes?

Lastly, can we safely bring back the region that was down without impacting the business?

That is ultimately the difference between simply running Kafka across multiple regions versus actually supporting a mission-critical Tier-0 workload.

Time to re-install mid-quarter updates at Intel?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Intel has been sued by shareholders who accuse the company of misleading investors about the severity of the PC market downturn and its impact on the company's financial performance before its shares plunged by nearly 29% in late October, reports Reuters.

The proposed class action lawsuit led by the Southfield Fire and Police Retirement System pension fund was filed on Friday in a federal court in San Jose, California. The lawsuit alleges that Intel failed to inform investors in a timely manner about the extent to which slow demand for consumer processors and excessive inventories would affect its sales. In particular, the plaintiffs argue that the negative impact of the PC market downturn on Intel's business was greater than the benefits the company enjoyed from the unprecedented buildout of AI data center infrastructure and other AI-related opportunities.

"Intel made statements that were materially false and/or misleading when made because they failed to disclose the following adverse facts pertaining to Intel’s business," the lawsuit reads.

While Intel, like many other major technology companies, has invested heavily in its manufacturing capabilities, supply constraints have prevented the chipmaker from fully capitalizing on strong demand for data center processors. Meanwhile, a severe shortage of memory has driven up DRAM prices and, consequently, the cost of PCs, which has affected demand for consumer systems. As a result, Intel could not sell enough CPUs in the third quarter, which dramatically affected the company's stock price in October.

Intel chief executive Lip-Bu Tan expressed disappointment with the supply constraints, the extraordinary situation in the memory market, and the subsequent weakness of the PC business during the company's earnings call. Yet, the plaintiff believes that the company should have warned about the turmoil situation on the client PC market earlier. In fact, back in the early 2000s, when Intel was led by Craig Barrett, the company routinely issued mid-quarter business updates to inform investors about changes in its financial outlook. In fact, in September 2004, Intel used such an update to cut its third-quarter revenue and gross margin projections due to weaker-than-expected demand for its processors and inventory adjustments by its customers, which were very similar circumstances that the company faced in the third quarter of 2026.

A similar update could have alerted Intel's shareholders to the challenges the chipmaker faced well ahead of its quarterly earnings release. Unfortunately, Intel abandoned the practice of mid-quarter updates in January 2006 under Paul Otellini, though it occasionally revised its guidance between quarterly earnings releases thereafter, particularly during the global financial crisis of 2008 – 2010.

Intel's shareholders also allege that the company had a financial incentive to withhold information about the state of its business as it raised $23 billion through a stock offering at $95 per share in mid-August, roughly halfway through its third quarter. Intel secured the additional capital as it planned to expand its semiconductor production capacity.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Intel’s stock price dropped by 28.6% on October 30, erasing around $140 billion of the company's market capitalization, after the chipmaker issued Q4 revenue and earnings projections that fell short of expectations and warned that the PC market downturn would persist. Meanwhile, despite the massive sell-off, Intel's shares have gained 62% since the beginning of the year.

The lawsuit seeks unspecified damages on behalf of Intel investors who purchased the company's shares between July 23 and October 29, 2026. Intel CEO Lip-Bu Tan and CFO David Zinsner are also named as defendants.

#10733. Jailbroken PS4 runs 9-billion-parameter AI model at up to 43 tokens per second

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It needs no PC and no internet, just a console running GoldHEN.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Developer Leonardo Alegría’s open-source project Garlic LLM lets a jailbroken PS4 run the 9-billion-parameter Qwen3.5-9B AI model offline on the console’s 2013-era GPU. The app talks to the GPU directly, and the model is shrunk to about 4.5GiB to fit in the console’s memory, running at 31 to 39 tokens per second.

The project, named after the memory the GPU reads directly — its sibling memory type is “Onion” — relies on a PS4 running GoldHEN, which is a homebrew enabler for exploited consoles. Garlic LLM is a free, single 5.66GB download, available on Hugging Face and installable through the PS4’s Package Installer, with about 6GB free needed, or 12GB if the package is first copied to the console’s internal disk. The developer tested it on a base/Slim PS4 on firmware 11.52.

It’s capable of voice conversation, with spoken replies in seven languages through the Chatterbox speech model and the chat model running at about 22 tokens per second. A held controller button also lets it take dictation with Nvidia’s Parakeet TDT 0.6B v3 speech-to-text model, which supports 25 European languages. Parakeet models also power local transcription and dictation apps such as Meetily and Hex. The main model is Alibaba’s small open-weight Qwen3.5-9B, which, at the claimed 31 to 39 tokens per second in text chat — the prompt and test method are not given — is usable on the console.

The closest comparison is the PS5LM fork, which runs llama.cpp as a native app on the PS5 and reaches the console’s AMD GPU through PS5_Vulkan, a port of the RADV driver. The open-source llama.cpp is a widely used program for running language models locally, and RADV is the open-source driver that lets AMD GPUs run Vulkan, the graphics and compute API. On the PS4 there is no such driver layer for homebrew; Garlic LLM uses neither llama.cpp nor a Vulkan port, or so it would appear.

The model’s operations, such as matrix multiplies over 4-bit weights, are compute kernels written in OpenCL C and compiled with LLVM for AMD’s Graphics Core Next (GCN) architecture. The weights are kept in that “Garlic” memory, while the CPU side, in plain C, handles the tokenizer, the interface, text rendering, and audio. The pipeline runs from the prompt to the CPU tokenizer, then, for each token, a PM4 command buffer — the low-level commands a graphics driver would normally generate, which, in this case, the app builds itself — and the GCN kernels, before the token is displayed or spoken. The whole thing is built with the open-source OpenOrbis PS4 toolchain.

One immediate question is how you fit a 9-billion-parameter model in the console’s 8GB of memory, which the CPU and GPU share. At 8 bits per weight, you have one byte per parameter, meaning you’d need at least 9GB, with typically more than that required overall. Part of the answer is 4-bit (“Q4”) weights, which take half as much, although 3-bit (“Q3”) weights, at about 3.5 bits with scales, are used for the two largest matrices in each layer.

Using the combination gets you to about 4.5GiB, versus about 4.9GiB if it were all 4-bit. The attention, or KV, cache is also 4-bit, which lets a 16K-token context fit, in comparison to the model’s native 256K. The Parakeet dictation model adds 0.33GiB and the Chatterbox voice models 0.29GiB and 0.09GiB, for a grand total of 5.23GiB, or 5.62GB, which closely matches the 5.66GB package size.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The next question is performance. Qwen3.5 comes with a multi-token prediction (MTP) head, and Garlic LLM uses it to draft up to two tokens ahead, with the full model verifying. This matches what the model was trained for, and the vLLM instructions on Alibaba’s model card use two speculative tokens. The project credits this for its chat speed on 2013 hardware.

Code also comes out faster with this setup, at up to 43 tokens per second. Also detailed is the default temperature, or model variability, which is 0.7 — Alibaba recommends 0.7 for general tasks with thinking off and 1.0 with it on — although the demo video uses 0, so it is predictable. At 35 tokens per second, the middle of the reported performance range, a 300-token answer takes under nine seconds. As a comparison, the PS5 ran the three-times-larger Qwen3.8-27B at about 20 tokens per second and the smaller Gemma 4 E4B and Llama 3.2 3B at 61 and 124, respectively, which is potentially quicker.

The PS4 Pro is “not tested yet,” but the developer is asking users for reports on GitHub. The PS5 is not supported as the app is written specifically for the PS4’s GPU and system software. The app itself contains no exploit, but GoldHEN is installed at the user’s own risk.

Currently Amazon has 392 broadband satellites in orbit and hoped to move the service out of beta by the end of this year.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Amazon has suffered a major setback in its plans to offer a viable Starlink rival, after 40 satellites destined for low Earth orbit (LEO) were destroyed. Just ten minutes after the Vulcan vehicle launched from Space Launch Complex 41 in Cape Canaveral, Florida, on Sunday, there was an “anomaly,” meaning that the rocket “failed to deliver the Amazon mission to orbit.” The language seems quite vague, but the United Launch Alliance (ULA) says that data needs to be analyzed “to understand what occurred today.”

And LIFTOFF of the first Vulcan Amazon Leo mission, carrying 40 broadband satellites to low Earth orbit! https://t.co/dv1MSjJUMFNovember 15, 2026

Shortly after the triumphant takeoff clip was shared by the ULA, above, the space tech firm updated its X feed with a confirmation that its mission on behalf of Amazon failed.

Based on data received by our Launch Director, we confirm that the Vulcan vehicle launched from Space Launch Complex 41 suffered an anomaly 10 minutes into the flight and failed to deliver the Amazon mission to orbit. Every flight generates data, and every flight teaches us something. Since the anomaly just occurred, we don’t have any specific data yet to understand what occurred today. ULA exists to provide assured access to space for our nation and for the customers who depend on us. That purpose is why we take on some of the hardest missions in spaceflight, and why we hold ourselves to the standard of mission success. Today, we did not meet that standard. We will work closely with our customer and other appropriate organizations to find the cause, determine the appropriate corrective action, and return to flight as quickly as possible. ULA is committed to full transparency with our customers, and they will have visibility into the investigation process, which utilizes rigorous methodology and corrective action. We will provide further updates when new information becomes available.November 15, 2026

Sadly for Amazon, the ULA, and folks who want a viable substitute for Starlink in the satellite internet business, this failure will surely delay the full commercialization of this service. ISPReview notes that Amazon has successfully launched 392 Kuiper communications satellites, but needs at least 500 for basic global coverage. Moreover, Amazon Leo service’s transition from the current commercial beta phase to launching properly at the end of 2026 now looks uncertain.

Reports indicate Amazon has already manufactured 1,000 satellites, but ULA, which is a joint venture of Boeing and Lockheed Martin, will need to analyze and assure customers that Sunday’s failure won’t be repeated. ULA's Vulcan vehicle had already been out of action for nine months after technical investigations into issues with its solid rocket side boosters and core BE-4 engines (made by Amazon Blue Origin).

For some context, the ULA has (or had) a great track record. Sunday's incident was its first total mission failure in its 20-year history. Early telemetry from the organization is already pointing towards some suspected causes of the destruction of the 40 satellites. Likely fault candidates include the first-stage BE-4 engines burning longer than planned, and there may have been some altitude issues near staging.

The headline failure and essential careful analysis of the anomaly won’t hold up Amazon’s other launch partners, though. A week from today, the Amazon Leo constellation is set to grow by 36 more satellites. They will be sent into orbit aboard a European Ariane 64 Block 2 (LE-04) rocket.

Amazon has approval for an initial constellation of 3,236 LEO broadband satellites, with 392 now in orbit, plus 5,105 for 5G service. Satellite tracking sites suggest Starlink already has 11,200+ satellites in orbit.

#10727. How to Build an Accessible Gold Value Calculator in Vanilla JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Ask anyone selling an old chain or a broken ring what they want to know, and it's one thing: what is this worth? A small calculator can answer that in a second, but most of the ones you find online get the details wrong. They ignore troy ounces, treat 14K as 58.5% one day and 58.3% the next, or show a "value" that no buyer will ever pay.

In this tutorial, we'll build a gold value calculator that gets those details right. It runs on plain HTML and JavaScript, updates as the user types, works with a screen reader, keeps its state in a shareable URL and pulls a live spot price through a small Node.js endpoint. The calculation lives in one pure function, so we can test it in a few lines.

Every gold calculator comes down to three inputs: how much the item weighs, how much of that weight is actually gold, and what pure gold is trading for today.

Purity is measured in karats, out of 24. A 14K ring is 14 parts gold and 10 parts other metals, so it's 14 ÷ 24 = 58.3% gold. You'll also see 14K stamped as "585", which is the hallmark many manufacturers use for the same alloy. For a calculator, the karat fraction is the safer default:

The second detail most tutorials miss is the unit. Gold spot prices are quoted in USD per troy ounce, which is 31.1035 grams, not the 28.35-gram ounce on a kitchen scale. Jewelers also use pennyweight (dwt), which is 1.5552 grams. If your calculator multiplies grams by a per-ounce price without converting, every result is wrong by a factor of 31.

Put together, the formula looks like this:

For example, with gold at $2,400 per troy ounce, 10 grams of 14K gold contains 5.83 grams of pure gold, which is worth $449.85 at melt.

That $449.85 is the value of the gold content alone. Refiners and buyers pay a percentage of it, because refining, assaying and handling cost money. Depending on the buyer, the item and the market, the payout can sit well below melt value.

That gap is why good production tools separate the two numbers. A dedicated scrap jewelry gold calculator, like the one The Alloy Market offers, lets sellers estimate what their gold is worth from its weight and karat before they send anything in. Our calculator does the same job with a payout field: leave it at 100% to see melt value, or enter a buyer's rate to see a realistic offer.

Start with real form controls and a visible

A few choices here matter more than they look:

A little CSS gives touch-friendly controls. Keeping inputs at 16px (1rem) also stops iOS Safari from zooming in when a field gets focus.

The biggest problem with most calculator snippets is that the math is buried inside an event handler, mixed with getElementById calls. You can't test it, and you can't reuse it on a server. Put the calculation and validation in their own module instead:

Both functions take plain values and return plain values. They don't know a DOM exists, which means the same file can run in the browser, in Node.js and in your test runner.

Notice that we don't round inside the calculation. Rounding happens once, at display time. Rounding in the middle of a chain of multiplications is a classic way to end up a few cents off.

Now connect the form to the module. Listening for the input event on the form itself catches changes from every field, including the dropdowns, so there's no need to wire up each control separately.

With the markup, CSS and script in place, here's the finished calculator showing our 10 g, 14K example with an 80% buyer payout:

Asking users to type the spot price works, but prefilling it is friendlier. Don't call a metals price API straight from the browser, though. Your API key would be visible to anyone who opens DevTools, and every visitor would spend one of your API calls.

Instead, put a small endpoint in front of your price provider and cache the result. This example uses Express and the fetch built into Node.js 18 and later:

Three things keep this endpoint dependable:

On the page, the loadSpot() function from Step 3 fills the field and shows the time of the price. The field stays editable, so a seller who has a quote from a specific buyer can type that instead.

A seller comparing offers might want to send their numbers to a family member, or come back to them tomorrow. Because every input has a name, turning the form into a query string is one line with URLSearchParams.

The syncUrl() function in Step 3 runs on every change and uses history.replaceState instead of pushState, so typing doesn't fill the back button with dozens of entries. On load, restoreFromUrl() puts the values back. A link like this opens with the form already filled in:

This is also the progressive enhancement story. If JavaScript fails to load, the form still submits as a GET request. Your server can read the same parameters, import the same gold.js and render the result in HTML.

Because goldValue() is a pure function, testing it needs no browser and no mocks. Node.js ships with a built-in test runner, so there's nothing to install:

Run it with node --test. (Add "type": "module" to your package.json so Node treats the files as ES modules.) The troy ounce test is the one worth keeping forever: if anyone "simplifies" the conversion later, it fails immediately.

A quick checklist for putting this calculator on a real store:

A gold calculator looks like a ten-line script, but the details are what make it useful: converting troy ounces properly, separating melt value from payout, keeping the math testable and making the result accessible and shareable. The same pattern of a pure calculation module, a thin DOM layer and a cached server endpoint works for any calculator where the price depends on several inputs, from shipping estimates to currency conversion.

Mathematically, 14K is 14 ÷ 24 = 58.3%. Many manufacturers stamp 14K jewelry "585" and alloy it to that standard, so both numbers are common. Pick one, state it in the dropdown, and use it consistently.

Let the user choose. Most people weigh jewelry in grams, but spot prices are quoted per troy ounce, so the calculator should accept either and convert internally.

Yes. Drop Step 4 and let users type the spot price, or have a scheduled job write the current price into a static JSON file. Everything else runs entirely in the browser.

#10721. Overlocker uses vibecoded BIOS modification to target Intel's non-K overclocking restrictions

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In an attempt to resurrect ASRock's discontinued SkyOC overclocking feature, an enthusiast has vibe-coded a motherboard BIOS using AI, potentially bringing non-K CPU overclocking to modern Intel CPUs. According to a post on X, Hardware 360º used Chat-GPT Astra to decompile and compare two versions of an ASRock BIOS, one released with SkyOC and another with the feature disabled. The AI reportedly identified the code responsible for enabling the overclocking workaround and modified it to create a BIOS ready for implementation on a compatible motherboard. Unfortunately, it remains to be seen whether the modified firmware can restore SkyOC on modern Intel platforms, as the workaround is yet to be fully tested.

For context, Intel offers multiplier-based overclocking only on its unlocked K-series processors like the Core i9-14900K and the newer Core Ultra 270K Plus. Non-K models have locked multipliers, which restrict users from pushing CPU frequencies. However, there’s another way to raise the speed of a processor by pushing the base clock itself. The base clock, or BCLK, typically runs at 100 MHz and works alongside the CPU multiplier to determine its operating frequency. For example, raising the base clock speed of a CPU to 105 MHz increases the clock speed by 5%. The only downside is that BCLK adjustments can also affect the entire motherboard, including memory and PCIe-based components, thereby potentially causing system instability.

ASRock's SkyOC technology, which was introduced for Intel's Skylake processors in 2016, allowed users to overclock locked non-K CPUs through BCLK adjustments. Certain motherboards also featured independent clock generators, allowing CPU clock adjustments without affecting the rest of the system. Intel was not particularly pleased with the workaround, and ASRock eventually had to remove SkyOC from its motherboards via BIOS updates. Additionally, Intel introduced microcode changes to prevent non-K overclocking.

The current experiment revisits that workaround by identifying the original BIOS code and adapting it for modern motherboards. Hardware 360º claims Intel's restrictions include a mechanism that triggers a shutdown when BCLK exceeds 103 MHz, and that SkyOC originally worked around this by tricking the processor with a false report of the actual BCLK value.

Finding the original code for SkyOC does not necessarily mean it can be successfully ported to a modern motherboard BIOS. Newer Intel processors use different firmware, microcode, and clock-management mechanisms, which would make adapting the workaround considerably more complicated. Flashing modified motherboard firmware also comes with its own set of risks, including the possibility of rendering the board completely unusable. Nevertheless, it is interesting to see how AI-assisted reverse engineering can help gain access to BIOS modifications that would otherwise require a considerable amount of effort.

#10717. Anthropic says Claude Mythos Preview cut AMD MI355X onboarding from up to 12 months to around three days

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

If AI can write the low-level code that ties models to Nvidia, CUDA matters less.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anthropic told IPO investors that its Claude Mythos Preview model did the software work required to get its AI running on AMD’s GPUs in about three days, with “basically no human intervention,” a job it says used to take six to 12 months. The three days cover prompt processing for one Anthropic model on AMD MI355X GPUs “at a useful level of performance,” with Mythos porting or writing “almost 100% of the kernels.” That kind of low-level code, which ties an AI model to one company’s chips, has long been part of Nvidia’s CUDA moat, and an AI that can write it in days would suggest that moat is eroding.

Tom Brown, Anthropic’s co-founder and chief compute officer, made the time comparison, noting to investors that the original meant using a team of “dozens of engineers.” The company already runs its models on multiple kinds of chips — on GPUs from Nvidia and AMD, Google’s TPUs, and AWS Trainium — and its fleet is managed as “one fungible pool of compute.” Brown called the early multi-chip decision “an unconventional bet.” It may have been a wise one, given that Google’s TPUs can now natively run AI models built in PyTorch, and Meta uses them in production.

Anthropic's claim that the job used to take six to 12 months comes from past work, from its “historical experience bringing up new accelerators using traditional engineering approaches,” where tailoring the code to each hardware platform was a challenge. Removing that onboarding barrier could reduce things to a purchase decision, and Anthropic started working with AMD GPUs for the first time this year, with an agreement for up to 2 gigawatts now in place.

CUDA, Nvidia’s programming platform, is very mature at nearly two decades old. AMD has an equivalent, ROCm, with HIP as its programming layer. Code written for CUDA has to be transformed to work there, and AMD even built a tool called HIPify for the process. There is still a conversion cost to switching, and AMD’s vice president of AI software, Anush Elangovan, told EE Times in April that “Claude is better than HIPify because it has web search built in.”

AMD has been making the argument that AI can write this type of code since August of last year, presenting a research agent that fell short of human experts at the time. In September of that year, AMD CEO Lisa Su said on the Possible podcast that much of AI software is written in CUDA, which needs to be “translated to AMD,” and “a ton of that can now be done in software.”

Moving forward to July earlier this year, AMD’s senior vice president of AI, Vamsi Boppana, said its engineers were using AI models to generate GPU kernels, and Su said that when she asked whether her team was helping the Anthropic engineer working on the MI355X, she was told they didn’t need any help. Boppana said that in some cases, the resulting kernels were even “better than the most manually tuned versions.” Under its July agreement with Anthropic, AMD is also using Claude to optimize workloads for its Instinct GPUs and to speed up ROCm development.

At AMD’s Advancing AI event in July, OpenAI’s Philippe Tillet said that once OpenAI got its Helios racks, a single engineer confirmed everything worked within a few days, and that “agents are taking over our jobs, in a sense, as kernel engineers.” Before the event, AMD’s Andrew Dieckman, corporate vice president and general manager of the company’s data center GPU business, told reporters that he has had practically zero conversations with AMD’s customers about CUDA.

Published GEAK, an AI agent that writes GPU kernels; against 30 expert-written kernels it got 19 right, at 0.92 times the experts’ speed on average

Asked about AI coding tools, said much of the code written for CUDA can now be translated to AMD in software: “We’re using software to significantly optimize our customers’ code so that they can run on AMD”

Anush Elangovan, AMD vice president of AI software

Compared Claude with HIPify, AMD’s tool for converting CUDA code: “Claude is better than HIPify because it has web search built in”

“We use a ton of AI to create the kernels that we have”; “nobody knows our architecture better than we do”

Andrew Dieckman, AMD corporate vice president and general manager, data center GPU business

“I have almost zero conversations with our customers about CUDA at this point in time”

Vamsi Boppana, AMD senior vice president of AI

AI-assisted GPU programming will “remove any remaining barriers to broad adoption”

“agents are taking over our jobs, in a sense, as kernel engineers”

Tom Brown, Anthropic co-founder and chief compute officer

One engineer asked Claude to bring up an MI355X rack and left it running “over the weekend”

Tom Brown, Anthropic co-founder and chief compute officer

Mythos “got Claude running on AMD in just three days with basically no human intervention”; the slide footnote limits the three days to prompt processing for one model

Until this week, though, all of this came from AMD or its stage, not from Anthropic to its own investors. Anthropic buys the chips rather than selling them, and it’s heading into its IPO next week, so its multi-chip approach — now with at least some numbers to back it up — carries more weight than a chipmaker’s claims. Its prospectus says it in writing, too, though without a number: “We can onboard new chip platforms in a fraction of the time it once took.”

Not stated; left running “over the weekend”

Its “leading model” on an MI355X rack, with performance climbing

Mythos, with “basically no human intervention”

Getting “Claude running on AMD”; Claude “ported or wrote almost 100% of the kernels”

Work that “took dozens of engineers months”

IPO roadshow video, slide footnote, Nov. 10

Bringing up “prompt processing for one Anthropic model on AMD MI355X GPUs at a useful level of performance”

Six to 12 months (“~6-12-month estimate”), from “historical experience bringing up new accelerators”

Its engineering teams, “leveraging Claude”

Bringing “new chip platforms into production”; no chip named

What it calls “intense engineering effort over many months”

The prompt processing (prefill) part of the workload is the step where a model reads and processes what it’s been given before it starts replying, and Anthropic hasn’t said whether the replying step (decode) was included as well. Anthropic’s chart, with its axis labeled “Time to onboard new silicon,” shows both lines reaching the same “~100%” mark without saying 100% of what. The company also hasn’t said how fast the model ran on AMD’s GPUs versus Nvidia’s, and that’s the comparison we want to see to show whether AMD’s chips, running AI-written code, can match Nvidia silicon.

On the other hand, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said in an April interview with Dwarkesh Patel that Nvidia already uses AI on its own kernels and knows its chips best — “nobody knows our architecture better than we do” — and can get the most out of them. Asked why labs like Anthropic use other accelerators, he called Anthropic “a unique instance, not a trend.” Nvidia and AMD, among seven companies, are buying $26.0 billion of Anthropic’s stock at the IPO price, so Nvidia is investing in the very company Huang called the exception.

In late July, SemiAnalysis published an article, “Can AMD break the CUDA Moat?,” which essentially said that software creation is not the same moat it was even a year ago. That said, the catch for AMD is that with agentic coding, “each agent requires GPUs to test their code against,” which means that the AI writing the code still needs the hardware. It has found a constant lack of GPUs inside AMD for this type of development, which has held AMD back and may be a new bottleneck.

That leaves CUDA’s moat somewhere in between. Getting a model running on a new chip, once the expensive part, appears to be getting cheaper, and better AI may wear down Nvidia’s claim that it knows its hardware best. As for Anthropic’s fungible pool, the chips in it aren’t fully interchangeable — Google’s TPUs are increasingly specialized, for example — and getting the most out of any of them still takes the hardware to test on. The moat is narrowing, but is not gone.

Anthropic’s first AMD MI450 chips, in Helios racks, are expected in the first half of 2027. That’s a new generation and an opportunity to see if the three-day bring-up can be repeated. Anthropic’s IPO comes sooner, after which it will report as a public company.

#10715. Nintendo will soon discontinue Google and Apple sign-ins

Nintendo Switch users have until January 25 to change how they sign in to the Nintendo Switch app on their smartphones.

Nintendo is going all-in on passkeys in a scheduled update to the Nintendo Switch app and will be sunsetting 3rd party logins after January 25, 2027. The news was made available on an official Nintendo support page. That means you won’t be able to use your Google or Apple accounts to log in to your Nintendo account as of January 26. After January 26:

Instead of using 3rd-party account linking, Nintendo is recommending that users register a passkey for an exclusive, secure login experience through the Nintendo Switch app. If you forget to do this before January 26, you can always head to the Nintendo account page and run through the “forgot password” instructions. Nintendo recommends making sure you can log in to your account ASAP via non-Google or Apple links to ensure that you don’t run into problems before January.

Nintendo didn’t elaborate on the policy change, although some surmise it’s based on security-related measures. The advancement of AI has created bots that can hack into systems with unprecedented speed, so it’s possible that Nintendo is simply trying to shore up any possible area it cannot 100% control. Either way, if you use the Nintendo Switch app or have a Nintendo account of any kind, you should change how you log in immediately.

#10714. What Breaks When a Self-Hosted LLM Gateway Hits 1,000 Concurrent Streams

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

We sent 1,000 streaming calls at the same moment through three self-hosted LLM gateways: the Langfuse AI Gateway, the LiteLLM proxy and AcruxCore. Each gateway was limited to 2 CPUs. As shipped, Langfuse finished 261 of the 1,000 streams and LiteLLM finished 403. Neither failure came from the gateway's own code.

Langfuse's failures came from Linux's open-file limit, which Docker sets to 1,024 by default. LiteLLM had that problem too, plus a second one: an empty key cache sent hundreds of database lookups at once, and the ones that waited longer than 10 seconds were rejected. This article shows how each failure looked, how we traced it to its cause, and the settings that fixed it.

This benchmark measured the resource consumption of three LLM gateways under scaling concurrent streaming loads.

Full latency, memory, and feature results are available in the Langfuse vs LiteLLM vs AcruxCore benchmark.

First-token times are medians of the streams that succeeded. Up to 250 streams, every gateway succeeded. Then the errors started, and they did not look alike:

A 502 and a 503 point at different layers, so we expected two different causes. Two of the three turned out to share one cause.

Linux counts every network connection as an open file, and it caps how many open files one process may hold. The cap has two values. The soft limit is the cap that is in force now. The hard limit is the ceiling: a process may raise its own soft limit up to that value, and no further.

A streaming gateway holds two connections for each stream: one from the client, and one to the model provider. That makes about 1,000 open files at 500 streams, and about 2,000 at 1,000 streams. A gateway also needs a few more files for its database, its logs and its own libraries.

Docker on our machine gave every container a soft limit of 1,024 and a hard limit of 524,288. All three gateways received the same two numbers. What differed was how each program treats them when it starts:

Node.js raises its own soft limit to the hard limit at startup. The Rust and Python services keep 1,024, and neither project's Compose file sets a higher value. So the two gateways ran out of open files near 500 streams, which is the point where the errors started.

The out-of-files error appeared as a failed connection, not as a clear message. The Langfuse gateway's logs showed failed connections to OpenAI, and also failed connections to Langfuse's own web service, which the gateway asks to check each key. Both come from one cause: a process that cannot open another file cannot open another socket. Its client then receives a 502.

Read the limit the process actually has, not the one you think Docker gave it. Process 1 is usually the main process in a container:

If the gateway runs under a supervisor or several workers, check each worker's process ID instead of process 1. To see how close a process is to its limit under load, count its open file descriptors:

Raise the limit for the gateway service in its Compose file. We used 65,536 for all three gateways:

The same ulimits block works on the LiteLLM service. On Kubernetes or another platform, check /proc//limits inside a running container rather than assuming a value.

With the open-file limit raised, the Langfuse gateway finished all 1,000 streams. LiteLLM still returned 503 errors. To separate the two causes, we ran LiteLLM with one worker and only the open-file limit raised. All 500 streams succeeded in that ladder, but 595 of 1,000 failed with 503.

The 503 message names the authentication database, so we read LiteLLM's logs and its source. The log gave the same reason for all 840 failed streams in these runs, 595 in that ladder and 245 in the cold-cache runs below:

When 500 calls arrive while the key is not in the cache, each call looks the key up in Postgres. All of those lookups queue for 10 connections. The worker was also using its whole CPU allowance at this point, which slows every query it sends and receives. We infer that this CPU load lengthens the queue, but we did not measure it separately. Whatever the share of each, the lookups at the back of the queue miss the 10-second deadline.

If that explanation is right, the failures should depend on whether the key is already in the cache. So we ran 500 streams four more times. Three runs started after 75 seconds with no calls, which is longer than the 60-second cache. One run started right after a single warm-up call:

The warm run had no failures. The cold runs failed by different amounts, 161, 84 and 0, because the result depends on how many lookups miss the deadline, and that changes from run to run. This probably explains why the earlier ladder succeeded at 500 and failed at 1,000. A run is a race between the lookup queue and the deadline, so a single ladder does not show where the limit is.

The warm-up result is the one we measured. Send one call with each virtual key after LiteLLM starts, and avoid quiet periods longer than 60 seconds before a traffic spike. A steady flow of traffic keeps the key in the cache.

LiteLLM also exposes three settings that act on this path. We did not test them, so treat them as places to look rather than proven fixes:

We raised the open-file limit to 65,536 for all three gateways and gave LiteLLM 2 workers, one per CPU, as its production guide recommends for a single machine. Then we ran the full ladder again:

The open-file errors were gone for every gateway, and the Langfuse gateway gave the fastest first token of the three. LiteLLM still returned 120 × 503 at 500. That level started about 60 seconds after the first call of the ladder, so the cached key had probably just expired. All 1,000 succeeded at the next level, which started within 60 seconds of the 500 level.

Once nothing failed, CPU decided the first-token times. LiteLLM used its whole 2-CPU allowance from 100 streams on, and its median first token took 19.2 seconds at 1,000 streams. AcruxCore also used both CPUs from 100 streams on, and its first token at 1,000 streams was 3.8 s at the median and 9.7 s at p95. The Langfuse gateway peaked at 1.65 CPUs and 168 MiB, with a first token of 1.7 s at the median and 9.6 s at p95.

Each gateway also needs the rest of its stack, and that stack uses CPU too. While the Langfuse gateway served 500 streams, its whole stack used up to 3.1 CPUs, because the web service checked keys and the worker and ClickHouse stored usage. AcruxCore's whole stack peaked at 4.0 CPUs, because its API writes a trace for every call to Postgres. Size the stack around the gateway, not only the gateway container.

Talha Anwar is a senior AI and full-stack engineer and writes about building and running AI systems at talhaanwar.com.

#10712. Even Realities glasses are coming to a job site near you

Even Realities is making its first big push into the enterprise business world with a new Enterprise SDK release.

Even Realities’ G2 glasses have been a hit for the company, making up 11% of the AR glasses market as of September. Now that consumers are clearly interested in what they can do, Even Realities is branching out to the business world with the release of the company’s first Enterprise SDK (software development kit).

The SDK works well with Even Realities’ app-based software, which lets users browse a bespoke app store filled with games, utilities, and more, all of which are designed to be used on the Even Realities G2’s in-lens displays. In our Even Realities G2 review, reviewer Andrew Romero said the glasses are “for the tech minimalist who wants to stay connected to notifications, while keeping a healthy distance away.”

In the business world, that actually makes a lot of sense. XR glasses and other types of smart glasses with displays are used in fields where you might need more information in front of you but can’t be on a device like a phone. Even Realities glasses are also extremely useful for keeping up with notifications when they come through, something that would be very handy on a job site.

“We built Even G2 to help people access useful information while staying present in their everyday lives, and we believe that principle matters just as much at work. With the Enterprise SDK, we’re giving companies the flexibility to build around the way their teams already work, rather than asking them to change their workflows to fit the technology,”– Will Wang, Founder and CEO of Even Realities

The SDK will allow companies to build custom interfaces, directly integrate glasses’ inputs (microphone and motion data) into apps, and handle other traditional IT management needs like managing device connections and updates. Companies can sign up on the Even Realities enterprise page.

#10710. Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air-304 review: A quiet and compact Wildcat

Minisforum’s smallest and cheapest mini PC packing Intel's Wildcat Lake silicon is here. It is small, quiet, and easy to stow away, but this machine's performance is really only for light workloads.

Capable enough for home office and media consumption

Only old or lightweight games and emulators are playable

Not a cheap option unless you have spare parts and go barebones

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Minisforum’s Intel-based M2 line includes mini PCs with powerful processors such as the Core Ultra X7 Processor 358H (with 16C/16T CPU and B390 graphics), and the Core Ultra 7 356H (16C/16T). But there are more modest systems, too. The M2 Air-304 here is the most affordable option, based around the Intel Core 3 processor 304 with its 5C/5T CPU (1x Cougar Cove P-core and 4x Darkmont LP E-cores) and an iGPU with just a single Xe3 core.

No surprise, the little brother in the pack, both in terms of physical size and computing resources, is the most affordable. It currently starts at $263 for a barebones version. However, our test sample with 16GB DDR5-5600, a 500GB SSD, and Windows 11 Pro is listed at $559 - direct. That’s an introductory price, and Minisforum’s small text threatens that our configuration will bump up to $699 at some point in the future. Such a rise would put this product offering on very shaky ground, and we see B&H Photo has already raised the price at $729.

Overall, this Mini PC was pleasant to use, though. It ran quietly and only strained in gaming and productivity benchmarks which put it under loads that casual users aren’t going to be running on such a machine.

The Mini M2 Air looks stylish enough, and feels solid. Looks can be deceiving, though, and the top shell is a silver colored plastic, not a metallic alloy. Sometimes a metal shell can be used to help conduct away heat, but that seems less important with this actively cooled machine with a 15W processor. We note that there are thermal pads connecting the installed RAM and SSD to the bottom of this mini PC, which is metal.

A VESA mounting bracket is provided in the box if you prefer to hide the tiny device out of sight. It doesn’t have a remote power switch like the MSI Cubi NUC AI+ 3MG we reviewed earlier in the year, though, so you’ll have to consider reachability. A simple LED-lit power button is on the front of the device, alongside a couple of handy ports (more on the ports later). It also has a reset / clear CMOS hole on the front, and a Kensington lock slot on the rear.

Minisforum’s component choices mean this is a particularly small and light mini PC. The official M2 Mini specs state it is 112 x 112 x 37.15mm, which is roughly 4.4 x 4.4 x 1.5-inches. That’s a volume of 0.47 liters. It weighs in at just 440 grams, which is 0.97 pounds.

The power supply isn’t built-in. There’s a surprisingly bulky 65W power brick in the box, which connects to using a detachable figure-eight cable. However, this mini PC can be powered by USB-C, too, so you could pack a far more compact multipurpose power brick if portability is important.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Intel Core 3 304 (5C/5T) ‘Wildcat Lake,’ up to 4.3 GHz, 15W

Intel integrated graphics, 1x Xe3 core runs at up to 2.3 GHz, supports up to 3x displays

16GB in single SODIMM slot which can fit up to a 64GB DDR5-6400 module

1x M.2 2280 PCIe 4.0 x4 NVMe SSD slot (up to 2TB), microSD card expansion

Front: 2x USB-A 3.2 Gen 2, 3.5mm audio. Rear: 1x USB4 (40Gbps, DP Alt Mode, PD), 1x USB-C 10Gbps, 1x USB 2.0, 2x HDMI 2.0, 2x RJ-45 2.5GbE LAN, DC-IN. Side: microSD card slot.

Windows 11; barebone model available without OS

$559.00 limited-time price(16GB RAM, 500GB SSD, Windows 11 Pro)

On the front of the Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air-304 there are 2x USB-A 3.2 Gen 2 ports and a 3.5mm audio jack. Turn it to the side and you’ll see the microSD card slot. Then, around the back you have access to 1x USB4 (40Gbps, DP Alt Mode, PD), 1x USB-C 10Gbps, 1x USB 2.0, 2x HDMI 2.0, 2x RJ-45 2.5GbE LAN, and a DC-IN barrel jack.

Some might like to have at least one of the Type-C ports on the front, instead of two Type-As. But for its size the Mini M2 Air packs quite a few ports, including the very capable USB4, two HDMI ports, and two Ethernet ports. Realistically, a machine like this won’t be connected to oodles of wired peripherals, but if you found the port count lacking, USB4 allows for a capable and fast dock.

Wireless connectivity seems adequate, with an M.2 2230 card inside providing Wi-Fi 6E and Bluetooth 5.4. I had no problems with either during the test period. The wireless card is socketed. It's located under the SSD, making it easy to repair or upgrade down the line.

Accessing the components inside the Minisforum Mini M2 Air is straightforward, but you need to peel off all the four rubber feet to get to four long fastening screws. When they’re removed, just flip off the metal bottom to access the M.2 slots and SODIMM. Be aware there’s some adhesive force holding this metal plate in place, due to the sticky thermal pads on the RAM and storage.

There’s just a single M.2 2280 storage slot and one SODIMM slot, both populated. That means, there’s no way to upgrade the RAM than swapping the module you already have in place. Our sample came with a 16GB DDR5-5600 SODIMM, but the system supports up to DDR5-6400. This only non-barebones configuration also came with a 500GB SSD in the M.2 slot.

Intel Core Ultra X7 358H (16C/16T: 4P + 8E + 4 LPE), up to 4.8 GHz

Intel Arc B390 (Xe3, 12 cores, up to 2.5 GHz)

Intel Core Ultra 9 386H (16C/16T: 6P + 8E + 2LPE), up to 4.9 GHz

Intel Graphics (Xe3, 4 Cores, up to 2.5 GHz)

AMD Ryzen AI 9 HX 470 (12C / 24T:  4x Zen 5 , 8x Zen 5c), up to 5.2 GHz

Radeon 890M (RDNA 3.5, 16 CUs, up to 3.1 GHz)

AMD Ryzen AI 9 HX 370  (12C / 24T:  4x Zen 5 , 8x Zen 5c), up to 5.1 GHz

Radeon 890M (RDNA 3.5, 16 CUs, up to 2.9 GHz)

AMD Ryzen AI 9 HX 470 (12C / 24T:  4x Zen 5 , 8x Zen 5c), up to 5.2 GHz

Radeon 890M (RDNA 3.5, 16 CUs, up to 3.1 GHz)

Intel Core Ultra 9 185H (16C/22T: 6P + 8E + 2LPE), up to 5.1 GHz

Intel Arc graphics (Xe-LPG, 8 cores, up to 2.35 GHz)

AMD Ryzen AI Max+ 395 (16C/32T: 16x Zen 5), up to 5.1 GHz

Radeon 8060S (RDNA 3.5, 40 CUs, up to 2.9 GHz)

Intel Core 3 processor 304 (5C/5TL 1P + 4LPE), up to 4.3 GHz

Intel Graphics (Xe3, 1 Cores, up to 2.3 GHz)

We have Intel’s latest 18A silicon inside this tiny PC, but not that much of it. Our review unit came with an Intel Core 3 304 "Wildcat Lake" chip that runs up to 4.3 GHz. Previous mini PCs based on Intel 300 series mobile silicon like the previously MSI and the GMKtec Evo-T2 came with a 16C/16T CPU. However, here we have just 5C/5T, and that’s just one performance core supported by four low power efficiency (LPE) cores.

Still, single core performance continues to be important for system and app responsiveness. The proof of the pudding is in the testing, though. And while this system felt perfectly usable in Windows 11, internet browsers, and word processing — preparing and writing this review, for example — the benchmarks are somewhat tougher.

As we expected, the single core tests show Wildcat Lake has some bite. It easily beats its AMD-based rivals in the POV Ray single core rendering tests, for example. Moreover, in tests like Cinebench 2024 single core, the Mini M2 Air might trail the pack, but not by very much. It's roughly 107 points is actually within touching distance of the 120 points scored by ‘high performance’ mini PCs like the Beelink SER10 MAX (Gorgon Point) and MSI Cubi NUC AI+ 3MG (Panther Lake).

Multicore tests exposed obvious weaknesses of the 5C/5T CPU configuration with single-channel RAM. All the other mini PCs we’ve tested this year come with 12C/24T (AMD) or 16C/16T (Intel), so the productivity benchmarks we run which can take advantage of multithreading for demanding rendering or encoding tasks make the Minisforum here seem weak. However, it might still be acceptable for transcoding a short video or two, occasionally, as long as you aren’t in a rush. If you are coming from an older, less efficient desktop sporting something like an i7-7700K or i5-10400, this 15W Wildcat Lake chip is an improvement in both single and multicore performance.

During our demanding tests, the Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air-304 stayed cool and quiet thanks to its 15W processor and capable cooling.

For anything other than the most casual gaming or emulation of older consoles you should look at other devices. We’ve looked at Intel 300 silicon powered devices with iGPUs packing 12 Xe3 cores, and four Xe3 cores, but this just has one, and it is further hampered by sharing the single-channel system RAM.

Prepare to see some low frame rates / scores in our standard tests. I felt that some of these tests probably shouldn’t be run on this mini PC, but did it anyway, for completeness. However, some 3DMark benchmarks simply wouldn’t run, as Intel has pruned the features that work on this iGPU due to limited resources. For example, 3DMark Solar Bay doesn’t run on this system, and neither does Speed Way. Error messages about unsupported hardware pop up when you try these tests.

Steel Nomad ran, though, and the Minisforum and its solo Xe3 core achieved a score of 135. The aforementioned Evo-T2 with Intel Core Ultra X7 358H and (B390) iGPU with 12x Xe3 cores scored 1,624. That’s incredibly close to 12x faster than the Core 3 304’s ‘Intel Graphics.’ A score of 135 means Steel Nomad ran at just a little more than 1 FPS, which actually doesn’t hurt your eyes to watch as much as 10 FPS.

Using the multi-scene benchmarks built into Shadow of the Tomb Raider and Cyberpunk 2077 using our standard settings selections resulted in some unpleasant experiences. Performance of no better than 10 or 12 FPS was observed in these tests. To achieve 25 or even 30 FPS in these titles I found that reducing the game resolution to 720p and low/lowest settings would just about get there.

So, for some actual fun on the Mini M2 Air I decided to try the latest version of the Sony PSP emulator PPSSPP and play some Wipeout Pure. I managed to run this game on my 1440p monitor with a 6x render resolution, and it achieved a nice steady 60 FPS, delivering an enjoyable futuristic racing experience. It would also therefore likely be good for retro emulation of similar era and older consoles as well as home computers.

During my day-to-day use, the Minisforum system consumed around 9W when idling on the desktop. When under the heaviest loads I threw at it, the highest system power consumption I saw was around 31W.

I didn't find it surprising that this low-power system ran cool and quiet. System tools showed that during light activities like browsing the web the CPU temperature remained in the low 30s Celsius. Playing games or benchmarking for length periods, I never saw the reported CPU temperature rise beyond the low 70s Celsius.

Minisforum’s tiny mini PC was a quiet operator at idle and under load. My measurements from about half a foot in front of the device show noise levels under 30 dBA at idle, which is very quiet and actually said to be beneath the measurement threshold of my meter. Stressing the system with simultaneous CPU and GPU loads I managed to observe just a reading of a little over 37 dBA from the system.

The Mini M2 Air ships with Windows 11 Pro installed in the configuration we received. I found no pre-installed bloatware. The official specs don’t mention compatibility with other OSes like Linux.

Minisforum sells this mini PC with a two-year warranty and a 30-day money-back guarantee.

Minisforum sells only two Elite Mini M2 Air-304 configurations in the U.S. There’s a barebones model, or the configuration we tested with pre-installed RAM, SSD, and OS.

At the time of writing our 16GB / 500GB / Windows 11 Pro configuration is listed by the mini PC maker at $559 but the product page says the price is normally $699. The barebone model is currently $263, discounted from $329. We don’t know when the prices might get adjusted. However, we’ve noted U.S. retailers like B&H Photo are now selling the 16GB / 500GB model at $729.

Though the Mini M2 Air’s performance may lack in performance, it also probably the smallest cheapest, and least power-hungry mini PC we’ve tested all year. If these kinds of qualities are important to you, the Minisforum might be appealing.

It is admirable that this mini PC is so small but leaves RAM and storage upgrades on the table for later. Other positives are the sturdy build, despite the use of plastic for the top shell, and a good port selection considering its size. In addition to being easy to hide due to its small stature, and supplied VESA mount, you probably won’t hear it very much, either.

Of course, the performance is a weakness that can’t be ignored, especially in the context of our ever-growing tested mini PCs chart from 2026. It is great to see this latest-gen processor barebones at just over $260 direct from Minisforum. If you have a spare DDR5 SODIMM and SSD, perhaps from a laptop upgrade or wherever, this might help you get a new mini PC system up and running cheaply. However, the 16GB RAM and 500GB SSD (and Windows 11 Pro license) currently more than doubles the list price (it adds $296). That’s a bit painful.

Furthermore, if the Minisforum Elite Mini M2 Air ever rises to its apparent MSRP of $699, we’d definitely look elsewhere. Around this price, especially during sale periods, you can shop for mini PCs employing far more powerful previous-gen processors. One good alternative candidate might be the Geekom IT13 Max 2026 we reviewed in August at $799. Price trackers show this Core Ultra 185H machine with 24GB RAM has been available for under $600 with discount / coupon over the last few months. There are also a lot of capable older gen AMD mini PCs to ponder over at attractive prices.
