#10703. How to Use Developer Time Tracking Data to Improve Sprint Planning and Estimates

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint estimates usually fail because development teams heavily rely on memory and assumptions instead of evidence. They assume similar tasks will take the same amount of time. Based on these half-baked assumptions, most managers plan around ideal sprint working hours. The backlog then fills with commitments that look realistic on paper but often end up delayed.

Actual development rarely follows that plan. There are always unexpected bugs and task dependencies that consume more time than teams account for in their estimates. Even the buffer time set aside for unexpected work may not be enough. This causes tasks to take longer than expected and pushes sprint commitments behind schedule.

Developer time tracking helps teams make better estimates with historical data. It shows how long previous tasks actually took, which provides managers with an evidence-based reference when they are planning similar work.

Reviewing this data before committing to a sprint scope turns estimation from guesswork into an evidence-based process.

Historical time data offers deeper insights, depending on how you collect and analyze it. Most importantly, it explains why planned work took longer than expected.

Before sprint planning, the following records should be reviewed.

Completed task duration: Check the time spent on finished tasks. Managers should compare similar work to understand typical development effort.

Actual versus estimated hours: Management should observe and identify differences between planned and recorded hours. If there are repeated delays, to some extent, it indicates inaccurate estimates.

Carryover work: Review unfinished tasks from previous sprints. Determine whether poor estimates or external delays caused the spillover..

Interruptions: Measure time spent on urgent requests and production issues. These activities reduce the development capacity that you had planned earlier.

Meetings and ceremonies: Review the time being spent on planning sessions and sprint reviews.

Bug-fix time: Examine hours spent resolving defects. You can also compare it to the time for developing new features.

Blocked tasks: Managers should account for the tasks delayed by missing approvals or technical dependencies.

Capacity differences: Compare team availability across sprints. Include leave and staffing changes in that time as well.

These records provide the most value when reviewed together. For example, a task may finish three days late even if the development work stayed within its estimated hours. The actual issue could be an external dependency.

Teams should understand these differences before using historical data to estimate future work.

Historical records provide a useful starting point for estimating new tasks. However, teams must interpret the data rather than simply copy previous estimates.

The following approaches can improve estimation accuracy.

The first step is to consider completed tasks that resemble upcoming work. Managers should compare their requirements and technical complexity. Moreover, the development environment should be considered.

For example, suppose a team needs to estimate a new API integration. Three previous integrations required 12, 15, and 18 hours of active work.

The average is 15 hours. This gives developers a reference point for the new estimate.

However, the average doesn't tell the entire story. The new integration may involve unfamiliar authentication methods or more complicated testing.

Review the reasons behind differences in previous task durations. Check whether developers encountered unexpected bugs or unclear requirements.

As a project manager, I'd consider these details before estimating for the next project. In my experience, they provide more context than an average alone.

Inaccurate estimates can happen; it doesn’t mean that there is a definite planning problem. However, repeated overruns across similar tasks may reveal an underlying issue.

Research suggests the pattern is widespread. A review of software estimation surveys reports that 60–80% of software projects encounter effort overruns, with the average overrun falling between 30% and 40%.

Start by calculating the difference between estimated and actual effort. Here is the formula for it.

Estimation variance (%) = [(Actual hours − Estimated hours) ÷ Estimated hours] × 100

Suppose a task was estimated at 10 hours but needed 14 hours. The actual effort exceeded the estimate by 40%.

You can compare the results across similar tasks.

For example, database migrations may repeatedly require more time than expected. This means that the team might be overlooking data validation or additional testing. Once the cause is clear, adjust future estimates accordingly.

Avoid increasing every estimate by the same percentage. Different types of work involve different risks. Teams can also break large tasks into smaller parts. This makes hidden development activities easier to identify.

The aim is to correct recurring estimation errors without unnecessarily increasing the entire sprint backlog.

Task duration doesn't always reflect the hours spent developing a feature.

A study of developer workdays by Meyer and colleagues, which tracked 2,197 hours of activity across 20 developers, found that coding accounted for around 21% of the typical day. Collaborative work such as meetings and email took up roughly 24%.

Consider a developer who starts a feature on Monday and finishes it on Wednesday. The task took three days to complete. This is pretty straightforward, and the estimation could be based on it; however, the developer may have spent several hours in meetings. Other than that, urgent support requests could also have consumed additional time.

The actual development effort could be much lower than three full working days. This distinction matters during estimation.

Teams should record focused development time separately from interruptions. They should also track waiting periods caused by blocked tasks. A more appropriate approach would be to use active development hours to estimate similar future work. They should also account for recurring interruptions when calculating sprint capacity.

For example, a task may regularly wait for security approval. Adding more development hours to its estimate won't resolve that delay.

Instead, the team should recognize the dependency and consider its effect on delivery.

If managers are able to separate these activities, it will help create better estimates without confusing effort with elapsed time.

A precise estimate can create false confidence when a task involves substantial uncertainty.

Suppose similar tasks previously required between 12 and 20 hours. If you estimate the next task at exactly 14 hours, it will overlook important differences.

A range of 12–20 hours provides a clearer picture of the possible effort. Developers can then discuss what might push the task toward either end of the range.

For example, the lower estimate can be directly applied to integration, and the upper estimate might be considered for unfamiliar code or additional testing.

In such scenarios, if the requirements stay unclear, consider a short technical investigation before finalizing the estimate. This can help identify dependencies and reduce uncertainty.

However, estimate ranges should still support practical planning decisions. Teams need to determine how much capacity to reserve for the work.

Choose a planning assumption that reflects the task's risk and available capacity.

As developers learn more about the task, they can refine the estimate.

Historical time data should affect how much work a team accepts during sprint planning. Better estimates offer little value if teams continue making unrealistic commitments.

If several sprints end with unfinished tasks, it is best that you first review why the team missed its commitments in the first place. Once you have identified the causes, use those findings to reduce the next sprint's scope.

Teams should prioritize work that supports the sprint goal. Moreover, avoid adding extra tasks just because they appear manageable during planning.

Production incidents and urgent requests can disrupt planned development. To get more reliable estimations, review how much time these activities consumed in previous sprints. Then reserve an appropriate amount of capacity.

Historical estimates become less reliable in case unfamiliar technology or additional dependencies are involved. It is best to break complex tasks into smaller components where possible. This helps developers assess the work more accurately.

Nominal capacity represents scheduled working hours, whereas actual capacity reflects the time available after accounting for other commitments.

The Scrum Guide identifies previous performance and upcoming capacity as important inputs to sprint forecasting.

When you are selecting backlog items, consider both. Additionally, avoid committing every available hour or deducting the same interruption twice.

Historical time data is useful for sprint planning. Most development teams already use project management tools that include basic time tracking features.

However, these tools don't always break down how developers spend their working hours. For clearer time data, a feature-specific time tracker is more useful because it provides more details about tasks and workloads.

TimeBee is one such developer time tracking tool I've used. It tracks working hours and time spent on projects and tasks. It also provides attendance records and workforce activity reports.

With this data, teams can review how much time previous tasks required. For example, developers can record hours spent building a feature. They can also track debugging and revisions separately. Later, managers can compare these records with the original estimates. This helps them plan similar work more accurately.

TimeBee also tracks website and application activity. These reports can help teams understand how working hours are distributed.

However, not all activities reflect actual development effort. Sometimes a developer just spends time reviewing documentation or discussing technical problems. Such work may not always appear in TimeBee as active computer use.

Apart from that, attendance, leave, and overtime records from TimeBee can help managers calculate sprint capacity. They can review developer availability before deciding how much work to include in the next sprint.

One thing to remember is that developer time tracking should help teams understand workload patterns. It shouldn't be used to monitor every minute of a developer's day. Management should be careful that the aim is to improve estimates and make more realistic sprint commitments.

Sprint retrospectives should include a review of how the planning is going. If you have made a plan, it doesn’t mean you cannot make changes to it. During sprints, keep comparing original estimates with actual task hours. Track your unfinished work for recurring delays.

Ask developers what caused significant differences. Some tasks may have required additional testing. Others may have been delayed by dependencies. Select one specific improvement for the next sprint.

Not every estimation difference indicates a planning failure. Unexpected technical challenges are part of software development.

The objective is to improve forecasts over time rather than eliminate every variance.

Historical time data provides a more stable foundation for sprint planning. Developer time tracking helps teams understand what is actually happening within the sprint rather than creating unnecessary pressure for them.

Other than that, the data also reveals how interruptions affect available development hours.

However, time data should guide planning rather than dictate it.

1. What is the difference between sprint velocity and time-based capacity planning?

Sprint velocity measures how much work a team has completed during a sprint. It is usually measured in story points. Contrary to this, time-based capacity planning calculates the hours available for planned work. It accounts for meetings and other responsibilities.

Velocity helps predict future delivery based on past performance. Capacity planning shows whether the team has enough time to meet its commitments.

2. Which time tracking metrics are most useful for improving Agile sprint forecasts? The most useful metrics include estimated versus actual hours, task completion time, and unplanned work.

Bug-fix time, rework, and blocked tasks also provide valuable insights. Teams should also review available capacity across previous sprints.

These metrics help sort out recurring delays and estimation errors. They allow teams to forecast workloads more accurately and make realistic sprint commitments.

**3. How can development teams improve estimates when historical time tracking data is incomplete? **

Teams can start with available task records and completed sprint backlogs. Developer feedback can help fill information gaps. Similar tasks from previous projects may also provide useful estimates.

When data is limited, teams should use estimate ranges instead of fixed hours. Consistent time tracking in future sprints will gradually provide more reliable planning data.

4. How can time tracking data help balance workloads across developers during sprint planning?

Time tracking data shows how much time developers spend on assigned tasks and other responsibilities. Managers can review these records to identify uneven workloads. They can then distribute upcoming tasks based on available capacity.

Technical expertise and task complexity should also influence assignments. This helps prevent over commitment and supports a more balanced workload across the development team.

#10701. Intel bets on Classiq acquisition to turn Quantum efforts into commercially viable business

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Intel becomes a top-to-bottom quantum computing company.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After having spent more than a decade pursuing quantum computing research, Intel is making its first real push at commercializing its efforts. First, with the pending acquisition of quantum computing company Classiq, which has an existing list of enterprise customers. Intel says it'll be the only company that can produce qubit chips and cryogenic control electronics at scale; the deal would give it a complete top-to-bottom hardware-to-software stack, and Intel will also gain the chance to become a leading vendor of quantum computing products. Intel's acquisition of Classiq is expected to complete in the first half of 2027.

The key ingredient of that strategy is the pending acquisition of Classiq, which offers a hardware-agnostic quantum software development platform that enables engineers to design, synthesize, optimize, simulate, and execute quantum algorithms using high-level programming tools and AI-assisted automation, essentially untying software design from the complexity of quantum hardware. Meanwhile, Intel also plans to manufacture quantum processors using specialized versions of its 18A process technology, offer foundry services, and eventually sell complete quantum computers. Although the company expects its quantum business to become profitable, it acknowledges that commercial success will take time.

Intel has spent more than a decade developing quantum computing technologies, including silicon spin-qubit processors, cryogenic control electronics, and software for hybrid quantum-classical computing. However, the company has largely treated these activities as research and development projects rather than an actual business. The acquisition of Classiq, which Intel expects to complete in the first half of 2027, marks a shift toward commercialization as Classiq already has many clients and established revenue streams.

"Up until now […] our quantum computing group for the last ten years was […] purely a research and development project," said Pushkar Ranade, chief technology officer at Intel, during a media briefing. "It was not really viewed as a commercial activity."

Intel's quantum computing journey kicked off in 2015 with a research collaboration with QuTech — the quantum research institute of TU Delft and TNO — to develop quantum computing technologies, including silicon spin qubits. The idea was to leverage Intel's extensive expertise in silicon transistor development and its high-volume semiconductor manufacturing infrastructure to produce quantum processors in the same fabs that make conventional CPUs, overcoming the manufacturing scalability challenges alternative quantum computing architectures faced at the time. Intel subsequently developed Horse Ridge, a cryogenic control SoC manufactured using a 22nm process technology in 2020. Three years later, the company introduced Tunnel Falls, a 12-qubit silicon spin processor manufactured on a 300mm wafer.

Intel's quantum computing effort focuses on silicon spin qubits, which use spin states of individual electrons to store and manipulate quantum information. The company chose this approach partly because silicon spin qubits can be manufactured using equipment and processes similar to those employed for conventional semiconductor devices. This compatibility enables Intel to re-use most of its existing manufacturing infrastructure and expertise rather than build dedicated semiconductor fabrication facilities for quantum processors that yet have to become commercial products.

Despite the recent cuts at Intel, the company sustained its quantum computing project. Intel has developed two specialized versions of its 18A manufacturing technology for quantum computing: 18A-Q for silicon spin-qubit processors and 18A-CC for cryogenic control and readout electronics. To define/build 18A-Q, Intel has collaborated with Hitachi, AIST, and NEDO (three major supporters of quantum computing from Japan), and there appears to be a design kit intended to support large quantum computing systems. Without a doubt, 18A-Q and 18A-CC put Intel on the map of quantum computing chips in a big way. A couple of things should be kept in mind, though.

"The underlying device that we use, underlying process that we use to make the actual qubit device is a unique process, but it fits into those design rules," Ranade explained. "We can make these chips […] in the same fab that we use to make our CPUs."

Although Intel 18AQ uses BEOL interconnect geometry and design rules derived from Intel 18A, its underlying qubit devices require a different manufacturing process. According to Ranade, the technology requires a specialized thin-film stack and several additional manufacturing tools. Nevertheless, more than 90% of the manufacturing equipment is identical to that used for conventional semiconductor production, according to Intel.

"Intel 18A-CC, meanwhile, uses 18A-based geometry but requires additional characterization for operation at cryogenic temperatures," Ranade said. "Crucially, Intel can manufacture quantum processors alongside conventional CPUs in existing semiconductor fabs, which could reduce the capital expenditure required to scale production. […] More than 90% of the process equipment is exactly the same."

For conventional x86 architectures and IA-64 (while it was active), Intel has historically offered compilers that made software run better on its hardware (and sometimes on AMD hardware too). By contrast, with its quantum computing efforts, Intel has generally focused on hardware and while it has its own Quantum SDK that includes a compiler, this is not enough in the quantum computing realm as Classiq's offering operates at a higher abstraction level. With the acquisition of Classiq, the company intends to fill that gap.

Classiq develops a hardware-agnostic quantum software platform that applies electronic design automation (EDA) principles to quantum programming, which lets developers design, synthesize, and optimize quantum algorithms without manually constructing circuits from individual quantum gates. The company's software provides high-level modeling, automated algorithm synthesis, optimization, debugging, and execution capabilities across different quantum computing architectures, which somewhat resembles Intel's approach to its x86 compilers, albeit working at a different level.

Intel already has its Quantum SDK, which includes a C++-based compiler, runtime environment, and simulator for hybrid quantum-classical applications. Classiq's platform will sit above these tools and provide an application-development environment for programmers who lack extensive expertise in quantum computing.

Intel compares the potential significance of Classiq's software layer to Microsoft's MS-DOS, which helped establish the personal computer software ecosystem; Nvidia's CUDA, which enabled developers to exploit GPUs for general-purpose accelerated computing; and EDA tools, which abstracted high-level design descriptions from the complexities of transistor-level integrated circuit implementation and essentially set the stage for the multi-billion foundry industry today.

Furthermore, unlike Intel's quantum hardware business, Classiq already has commercial customers and generates revenue. The company employs approximately 130 people and serves more than 50 enterprise customers, including BMW, Comcast, Lufthansa, Rolls-Royce, and Toshiba. It also participates in more than 10 national quantum computing programs and supports over 50 quantum processors and simulators.

As existing quantum processors remain insufficient for many practical applications, companies can develop and optimize software ahead of hardware availability. As a result, these customers primarily use Classiq products to develop quantum algorithms, train engineers, and create intellectual property in preparation for commercially useful quantum computers.

Intel's CTO described Classiq's revenue and anticipated growth as healthy, but did not disclose actual financial figures.

"They have a very healthy revenue this year and a very healthy revenue CAGR over the coming years," Ranade said. "We hope that… we want that to continue."

It is necessary to note that Intel intends to preserve Classiq's hardware-agnostic approach, which means allowing the company to continue supporting quantum processors and simulators from competing vendors. This is essential to maintain Classiq's existing customer relationships and expand its revenue opportunities, as its clients can/will be able to develop quantum applications without committing to a particular hardware architecture. Obviously, Intel can generate software revenue regardless of which quantum computing technologies ultimately become commercially successful.

"We very much want to maintain that hardware-agnostic platform that they have developed to attract more and more customers," Ranade said.

When it comes to quantum computing, Intel's ambitions extend beyond manufacturing silicon spin-qubit processors. The company intends to become a supplier of complete quantum computing systems, which would combine quantum processors, cryogenic control electronics, classical computing resources, and software. For now, Intel has most of the building blocks required to develop complete quantum computing systems, but it still has to demonstrate that it can integrate them into commercially viable quantum computing at scale.

"We want to be in the business of building quantum foundry components, but also complete quantum systems," said Pushkar Ranade, chief technology officer at Intel.

Intel is also considering opportunities to provide quantum foundry services to other hardware developers. While its internal processor development will remain focused on silicon spin qubits, the company is open to manufacturing components for alternative quantum computing technologies, which somewhat resembles its approach to its x86 CPU and foundry businesses.

"We are committed to spin qubits," Ranade said. "But we are also open to providing foundry services to other modalities."

Intel believes that its capabilities in photonics, advanced packaging, and semiconductor manufacturing could become commercially relevant even to companies developing quantum processors based on different physical principles than silicon qubits.

Intel's decision to expand its quantum computing activities with the takeover of Classiq comes as chief executive Lip-Bu Tan emphasized profitability, financial discipline, and high-margin products across the company’s established businesses. Given that Intel's quantum computing initiative barely generates any revenue, let alone profits, the CEO stance raises questions about the viability of quantum computing for Intel, especially in a situation where the industry has yet to demonstrate widespread practical applications that justify the cost of large-scale quantum hardware.

When asked how Intel’s quantum computing strategy fits Tan's profitability requirements, Ranade explained that the company's immediate financial priorities primarily concern its established client and server processor businesses. Nevertheless, Intel must continue investing in emerging technologies that could shape future computing markets.

"This is really a bet on the future," Ranade said. "This is something we also have to do in addition to our mainstream businesses today."

The Intel CTO acknowledged that quantum computing is not set to become a high-margin business in the near future. Meanwhile, the acquisition of Classiq gives Intel an established commercial software operation and revenue stream here and now, as well as an opportunity to expand customer relationships well before its own quantum processors reach commercial maturity. Intel also hopes that combining its hardware and software capabilities (probably including Classiq) will attract customers interested in complete quantum computing solutions.

When asked whether vertical integration would make Intel's quantum computing operations profitable, Ranade acknowledged that he could not predict gross margins of the quantum computing business because the industry is still in its infancy. Nevertheless, he expressed confidence in the commercial prospects of Intel's quantum hardware and software.

"I expect it to become a profitable business very quickly on the hardware side, but too early to tell," Ranade said.

Even though quantum processors evolve at a rapid pace, the technology is unlikely to replace existing CPUs and GPUs for conventional computing any time soon. Instead, Intel expects quantum computing systems to operate alongside the usual processors in data centers and supercomputing environments.

Classiq's software platform will provide high-level quantum algorithm development capabilities, while Intel Quantum SDK will handle compilation, simulation, and hybrid quantum-classical execution. This approach differs from Nvidia's CUDA-Q, which is designed to integrate quantum computing workflows with existing accelerated computing infrastructure. According to Classiq chief executive Nir Minerbi, Classiq operates at a higher level of abstraction and can complement CUDA-Q rather than compete with it.

"CUDA Q is very much low-level; it is gate-level. It is a good platform, by the way, but it is very low-level," said Minerbi. "The reason companies like Rolls-Royce and BMW and others use the Classiq platform on top of CUDA Q is due to the level of abstraction. It is much, much easier to develop quantum programs with Classiq and then to simulate it with CUDA Q rather than only with CUDA Q."

Meanwhile, commercial success of quantum computing still depends on substantial advances in quantum hardware, including qubit scalability, error correction, control electronics, and system integration. For now, many Classiq customers are developing applications for quantum computers that have yet to reach the scale required for commercially valuable workloads.

Intel expects to complete its acquisition of Classiq in the first half of 2027, subject to regulatory approvals and customary closing conditions. The company has not disclosed the purchase price or other financial terms.

The transaction will bring Classiq's software development capabilities and established customer relationships into Intel's quantum computing organization, but will preserve the platform's compatibility with competing hardware technologies. For Intel, the acquisition represents an opportunity to turn more than a decade of quantum computing research into a commercial business unit as it will have all the building blocks required to create quantum systems. Furthermore, Intel's quantum computing business will be open to third parties in the sense that Intel will be able to build quantum processors for partners using its 18AQ and 18ACC process technologies.

Whether Intel's quantum computing business eventually delivers the high margins Intel seeks will depend not only on Intel's ability to build systems, produce quantum chips for others, or Classiq's software revenue, but also on the industry's ability to develop quantum processors and systems capable of solving real-world problems that people are willing to pay for.

Anton Shilov is a contributing writer at Tom’s Hardware. Over the past couple of decades, he has covered everything from CPUs and GPUs to supercomputers and from modern process technologies and latest fab tools to high-tech industry trends.

#10690. How to Improve Email Deliverability Rates

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You wrote a good email. You hit send. Then half your list never saw it, because it went straight to spam.

That is a deliverability problem, and it costs more than most teams think. Every email that misses the inbox is a lead you paid for and never reached.

The good part is that deliverability is not luck. Mailbox providers like Gmail, Yahoo and Outlook judge you on a short list of things: who you are, who you send to, and how people react. Fix those and your inbox placement goes up.

This guide covers 10 email deliverability best practices in the order you should fix them, starting with SPF and ending with sender reputation.

Email deliverability is the share of your emails that land in the inbox instead of the spam folder or nowhere at all.

People often mix it up with delivery rate. They are not the same thing.

You can have a 99% delivery rate and still have most of your emails sitting in spam. That is why your email tool can say "delivered" while your open rate keeps dropping.

The big mailbox providers stopped treating authentication as optional.

Microsoft followed on May 5, 2025. If you send more than 5,000 emails a day to Outlook.com, Hotmail or Live addresses, you need all three records in place or your mail goes to junk.

Send less than 5,000 a day? Follow the rules anyway. The same checks decide inbox placement for small senders too, just without the public warning.

SPF (Sender Policy Framework) is a TXT record in your DNS. It lists the servers allowed to send email from your domain.

When your email arrives, the receiving server checks that list. If the sending server is not on it, the email looks suspicious.

DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail) adds a digital signature to each email. The receiving server uses a public key in your DNS to check two things: the email came from you, and nobody changed it on the way.

Your email provider gives you the DKIM record. You paste it into your DNS and switch signing on.

DKIM also survives forwarding, which SPF does not. If you can only get one of the two perfect, get DKIM right.

DMARC ties SPF and DKIM together. It tells mailbox providers what to do when an email fails both checks, and it sends you reports on who is sending mail under your domain name.

p=none means "take no action, just send me reports." It is the minimum Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft accept from bulk senders.

Don't stop there. Read the reports for a few weeks, fix any real sender that is failing, then move up:

One more thing: DMARC needs alignment. The domain in your From address has to match the domain used in SPF or DKIM. Plenty of senders have all three records and still fail DMARC because their email tool signs with its own domain.

If you'd rather not handle this in-house, you can also hire a B2B cold email marketing agency such as IntentSignal, Martal or Belkins. A good agency will set up the domains, mailboxes, warm-up and SPF, DKIM and DMARC records for you, and keep an eye on deliverability while the campaigns run.

A new domain has no history. If it sends 10,000 emails on day one, it looks exactly like a spammer who just bought a domain.

Warm-up means starting small and growing a little each day, so mailbox providers can watch how people react to you.

Send to your most active contacts first. Their opens and replies are what build trust early. If bounces or complaints jump, hold at that volume until they settle.

And if you run cold outreach, do it from a separate domain or subdomain. That way a bad campaign can't hurt the domain your invoices and password resets come from.

A bigger list is not a better list. Dead addresses and people who stopped caring pull your numbers down.

Never buy a list. Bought lists are full of spam traps, which are addresses that exist only to catch senders who email people without permission. Hitting a few of them can get your domain blocklisted.

Yes, you lose some sign-ups. What you keep is real people with working inboxes who actually want your emails. That means fewer bounces, fewer complaints and better opens from day one.

It also keeps bots and fake addresses out of your forms, which matters a lot if your sign-up form is public.

Most email platforms add these for you. Check that yours does.

These two numbers tell you the most about where you stand.

The complaint figures come from Gmail's own rules. The bounce figures are the working limits most email platforms use.

A 0.3% complaint rate is only 3 complaints per 1,000 emails. It does not take much to cross that line.

If complaints are climbing, ask three questions:

Most complaint problems come from one of those three, not from the email itself.

Spam filters got smarter. A single word like "free" won't sink you anymore. Patterns will.

Replies help a lot. When someone replies, the mailbox provider sees a real conversation. Ask a question in your welcome email and you'll get some.

Sender reputation is the score mailbox providers give your domain and IP. Everything above feeds into it. A good score means inbox. A bad one means spam, no matter how good the email is.

You can't see the exact score, but these free tools get you close:

Check them weekly, and right after any big campaign.

A few habits protect your reputation over time:

Aim for 95% or higher inbox placement. Below 90% means something needs fixing, usually authentication or list quality.

The common causes are missing or broken SPF, DKIM or DMARC records, a high complaint rate, an old list with lots of dead addresses, or a new domain that sent too much too soon. Check authentication first because it is the fastest to fix.

DNS fixes like SPF and DKIM take effect within a day or two. Reputation is slower. Expect two to six weeks of steady, clean sending before inbox placement recovers.

Yes. The 5,000 mark is where Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft start enforcing openly. Small senders are still judged on the same signals, and Gmail asks every sender for at least SPF or DKIM.

Only if you send enough to keep it warm, roughly 100,000 emails a month or more. Below that, a good shared IP from a trusted provider usually performs better.

Sender reputation covers both your IP address and your domain. Domain reputation is the part tied to your domain name, and it follows you even if you switch email providers. Gmail leans on domain reputation more than IP.

If you only have an hour this week, spend it on SPF, DKIM and DMARC. Nothing else works until those pass.

After that, clean your list and keep an eye on complaints. Send emails people asked for, to people who still want them, from a domain that proves who it is. Do that for a few weeks and your emails will land in the inbox again.

#10689. How to replace the battery on Pixel Tag [Video]

The battery on the Pixel Tag is rated for about a year, but what happens when you need to replace it? Here’s how to replace it.

Due to the shape and design of the Pixel Tag, you might have a bit of difficulty getting the backplate off quickly to throw in a new battery when it’s time.

Having had a few problems myself (yes, a me problem most likely) due to the slick, smooth shape, I figured that, even though it might insult the intelligence of many, the process was at least documented so that anyone having issues has a reference to come to if they’re struggling to get that backplate off.

You will have difficulty if you try to do this one-handed. Instead, clasp the Pixel Tag with one thumb at the base or midframe, then with your other thumb twist anticlockwise until the backplate comes loose and the battery will drop out.

The first time you do this, it will be harder due to the tight factory seal. However, once it’s open, the process gets easier to replicate. Here’s a little demonstration of how to get it off without it slipping and being flung into the ether:

Be aware that you may need to open and reseat the battery if Find Hub does not recognize the Pixel Tag again when you’ve done this. Even with the factory-supplied battery, it can misalign if you don’t place it in the correct position and fully close the door when reassembling.

If you’re wondering what type of battery you need, CR2032 are the standard for watches and most Android trackers. They’re super cheap with a 10-pack of Amazon Basics batteries for just $10, Duracell offers a 4-pack for under $7 but you can even get unbranded 20-packs for around $9. All fairly cheap at various points in the quality spectrum.

It can’t hurt to have some on hand when you do eventually need to replace the battery in your Pixel Tag. You can get the tracker from Amazon and the Google Store right now.

Following reports that ousted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is plotting a return to the company, President Donald Trump has voiced his own support for such a move, Bloomberg reports. However, according to new insight from CNBC, Nvidia's board of directors is not considering the move and is instead focused on its ongoing investigation into Huang's extramarital affairs and a national security investigation, likely into possible allegations of chip smuggling into China.

Trump reportedly made his comments in support of Huang's return to reporters at the White House on Monday, ahead of his trip to Asia. "I would not mind that," he said. "A lot of people are asking Jensen; they think Jensen should go back. In the wake of reports that US officials were said to be concerned about his close relationship with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, it later transpired that Huang had indeed had an affair with Ms. Huang, and that there were suspicions she was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Perhaps more concerningly for Jensen Huang, it has been alleged that Ms. Huang and Megaspeed may have been involved in chip smuggling into China to the tune of more than $7 billion, with hundreds of thousands of H100 and H200 chips said to have found their way into the country at her behest, a factor analysts believe may be the true reason behind Huang's exit.

#10683. Google Voice appears to finally be working on RCS support

It might finally be happening. A new Google Voice update plants seeds of RCS support, though the exact details remain unclear.

About APK Insight: In this “APK Insight” post, we’ve decompiled the latest version of an application that Google uploaded to the Play Store. When we decompile these files (called APKs, in the case of Android apps), we’re able to see various lines of code within that hint at possible future features. Keep in mind that Google may or may not ever ship these features, and our interpretation of what they are may be imperfect. We’ll try to enable those that are closer to being finished, however, to show you how they’ll look in case that they do ship. With that in mind, read on.

Google Voice is an invaluable tool for accessing one phone number across a host of different devices, but to say it’s becoming less impactful without support for RCS is a bit of an understatement. Now that virtually every Android phone and iPhone supports the new messaging experience, Google Voice is less useful both for business and consumer users.

Google Voice for Android version 2026.11.01.1014278023 doesn’t enable RCS, but it does, for the first time, actually hint that the functionality might be on its way. In a teardown of the app, one simple string stands out:

That points to a new send button used for RCS messages rather than SMS. Google Messages similarly changes its send button to differentiate between the two standards.

This is great news. I can have both options now to text friends and businesses with RCS as a fallback option on Google Voice because I don't want to give out my Google messages number.

This update contains a host of other new strings around images, videos, and more, but none of them are directly tied to RCS support.

The folks over at Android Authority dug up some other tidbits about RCS including an “RCS chat preference” toggle and various RCS actions and messages. But, again, nothing is live just yet, and there’s no word on when it might arrive, if ever.

Google has been championing RCS support for over a decade now, so it’s long past due for Voice, a long-running Google product, to get on board. While the tidbits in this update don’t serve as any confirmation RCS if coming to Voice, it’s the most exciting update on the matter in years.

#10680. Nvidia announces Rubin HPC GPU with 'record number of CUDA cores' at Supercomputing 2026

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Rubin HPC puts "over 50,000" CUDA cores on a chip, implies four-die layout

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Supercomputing 2026 conference is under way, and despite continuing turmoil elsewhere in the company, Nvidia has lots to reveal at the show. Foremost among its announcements today, Nvidia took the wraps off a new member of the Rubin GPU family called Rubin HPC, a chip that the company says is meant to accelerate agentic workflows and simulation for scientists, engineers, and analysts working with large data sets.

To accelerate those most demanding workloads, Nvidia says Rubin HPC has the highest number of CUDA cores on any of its GPUs - "more than 50,000" - which might imply that this chip uses a configuration similar to the four-die Rubin Ultra GPU, which links those dies together using NVLink-C2C.

Today's Rubin GPU has 224 SMs and 28,672 CUDA cores, so scaling compute resources to the degree Rubin HPC does would necessarily require more dies on a package. Nvidia didn't reveal more details of the chip's physical layout alongside the announcement. (The representative image shared by the company wouldn't appear to take this into account.)

To keep data moving between Rubin HPC GPUs, Nvidia will use its NVLink 7 interconnect. The company didn't share any new details of the bandwidth or physical layer of this interconnect within the rack, but past disclosures about the tech have indicated that it retains the 3.6 TB/s of per-GPU bandwidth that NVLink 6 delivers while enlarging the size of its scale-up domain.

Nvidia didn't share any details of the Rubin HPC GPU's HBM capacity or bandwidth, and it also didn't share whether this part includes a rebalancing of compute resources towards native FP64, as AMD and Intel are touting at the conference with their own GPUs this week.

Nvidia instead touts the suitability of this chip for scientists and engineers using agentic workflows, for which the company expects faster simulation performance and therefore faster opportunities for discovery in those fields thanks to better agent performance in those domains.

In fields like climate research, genomic analysis, and financial simulation, Nvidia says the Rubin HPC GPU's "balanced design," with both higher compute resources and (presumably) faster data movement between nodes, will help feed the chip with the massive data sets typical of those workloads, minimizing idle compute resources and achieving better utilization.

Researchers, engineers, and analysts marshaling agent swarms to explore the problem spaces in their domains will also be able to take advantage of Nvidia's CUDA-X domain-specific libraries to boost performance on Rubin HPC, tightening the reasoning loop from hypothesis to simulation to refinement and hastening the pursuit of the most promising results.

Nvidia says Rubin HPC will arrive sometime in 2028.

As the Senior Analyst, Graphics at Tom's Hardware, Jeff Kampman covers everything that has to do with graphics cards, gaming performance, and more. From integrated graphics processors to discrete graphics cards to the hyperscale installations powering our AI future, if it's got a GPU in it, Jeff is on it.

#10677. How to Use IP Geolocation to Stop Bots and Fraud (Without Hurting UX)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Several methods can help you deal with online threats and strengthen security. All are effective, but each has a trade-off. For example, the harder you make it for bots to affect your website’s performance, the more likely you are to frustrate real customers.

Those of you who browse online daily know that credential stuffing, fake signups & payment fraud keep growing. To prevent fraud, businesses install CAPTCHAs & hard blocks that treat bots and legitimate users the same, diverting them to your competitors.

IP geolocation maps an IP address to a physical location & network details, letting you spot suspicious traffic before it reaches sensitive actions. Used well, it’s an invisible, low-friction risk signal. In this guide, you will learn how to flag high-risk activity and apply soft geo-fencing with step-up authentication.

Today, an IP lookup returns far more than a country & city. It can also reveal several other parameters, like

Each of these fields acts as a clue telling who is really behind a request.

This data powers anomaly detection. You can start by building a baseline for normal behavior for each user with aspects like

Then, you can watch for deviations from that pattern. An attacker won’t look like the account’s real owner, so you'll catch them.

Geolocation makes several common attack patterns easy to spot:

The first is traffic bursts from data center ASNs. These are bots, as real customers don’t browse from cloud servers

Many accounts created from one IP or subnet. This is a clear sign of fake signups

Impossible travel, such as logins from two distant locations within minutes

Timezone vs. IP mismatch. A device’s clock disagrees with its apparent location.

Note: Geolocation is just a signal, not a verdict. VPNs, travel, & mobile carriers can all create false alarms, so it works best when combined with other signals. That’s exactly why a proportional, soft response beats a hard block.

In short, IP geolocation turns a bare IP address into context, helping you separate real users from automated or fraudulent traffic.

Not every IP geolocation anomaly deserves the same response. The simplest way to stay consistent is to define a clear risk-signal framework.

That can be a short list of checks. Each check can have a weight that reflects how strongly it suggests fraud.

With risk scoring, you can turn these checks into a decision. You just have to assign each signal a numeric weight.

For example, medium can be 10 & 30 can be high.

Now, you need to add up every signal that comes up & map the total to thresholds like

A single medium signal passes quietly, but several can trigger action.

If you treat this as an automatic block, it would punish legitimate customers. Instead, let it raise the score & you take action only if it stacks with other signals, like a hosting IP or a new device.

Now that you know how to quantify the risk, here is how you can protect yourself without damaging UX.

Before soft, let us learn about hard blocking. This is where you completely deny or permanently ban traffic from specific geographic regions.

Now, hard blocking by country may look effective, but it can backfire quickly. Here is how.

In the worst case, determined bot operators simply rotate to IPs in allowed regions, so the only people you stop are your real customers.

This is where the balance between security and user experience becomes critical. As the digital strategist and Founder at LawRank, Mariano Rodriguez emphasizes, "Your website's security should be a silent guardian, not a roadblock. If your fraud prevention creates friction, you aren't just stopping bots—you're turning away real clients. True digital trust is built when backend protections are seamless and invisible to the legitimate user."

Now, with soft geo-fencing, you can respond to risk proportionally instead of making a simple allow-or-deny call. Instead of totally banning the users, you adjust the level of friction to match the risk score from the previous section:

**Note: **For soft geo-fencing, passkeys are safest because they are phishing-resistant. Only a legitimate user can take the biometric tap and pass.

Before we start, let’s understand Express.js middleware. An Express.js middleware for Geolocation Risk Scores is a custom or 3rd party function that intercepts the incoming HTTP requests, extracts the client's IP address, fetches a security risk profile based on their geographic location & attaches that data to the request object (req) before it reaches your route handlers.

Here is how you can use it for fraud prevention and bot mitigation:

Note: You can make it stronger by adding velocity checks (too many attempts from one IP or account) & device fingerprints.

From a broader angle, IP geolocation isn't the final verdict. It is just a signal. But with this signal, you can score each request, respond in proportion to the risk & save hard blocks for clear abuse. This increases the friction for bots and keeps the flow easy for legitimate users.

#10676. How Artificial Intelligence Can Help With Critical Thinking

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Knowledge workers such as scientists, teachers, lawyers, and business leaders are increasingly using generative artificial intelligence applications for tasks that demand imagination, creativity, and problem solving. Ironically, perhaps, recent research shows that people who interact with generative AI for work tasks can end up not thinking critically. They can surrender to AI’s quick and confident answers, which can lead to speedy but low-quality solutions.

Scholars refer to this increasingly prevalent problem as cognitive offloading. And they are showing that it can erode the very skills that make knowledge workers valuable.

My research at the intersection of artificial intelligence and knowledge work provides a different view, however. In one study, my colleagues and I interviewed 45 knowledge workers at a U.S. public university. They all were using generative AI applications in their core tasks: generating new ideas, solving complex problems, and designing novel solutions. We also analyzed the prompts and outputs from their iterative AI chats.

We found that when knowledge workers intentionally use AI to challenge their ideas—to generate friction—they can significantly improve their performance. Previous research on human-AI collaboration for loan evaluations had similar results.

Using AI to find information that contradicts your own can reveal unlikely perspectives that disrupt your familiar logic and spark new insights. This is particularly important because AI models quickly become familiar with how a user prompts the model and interprets its output, and the model presents information according to these patterns.

For example, a marketing professional in our study asked AI to create virtual customers with personas that would lead them to dislike a product under development, which was an accounting certificate program. The contrary feedback from the AI model revealed customer preferences that the accounting professional designing the program had not considered, such as using multiple cases from different industries to illustrate a principle, rather than cases from the same industry.

An attorney used AI to find obscure legal loopholes and to consider how companies might exploit them. That exposed surprising but realistic scenarios of hard-to-detect unethical behavior in employees.

Exploring unlikely and opposing ideas presented by AI can feel disorienting. To help, knowledge workers should lean on their own expertise to question surprising information that an AI system might provide.

An operations research scientist who works with supply chains described adapting unfamiliar computing programs from other fields suggested by AI, such as signal processing and wireless communication, which helped him create a stronger program of his own. Although the programs were new to him, his strong understanding of supply network modeling helped him see how he could apply them.

A study I was not involved with found that creative writers produce better copy when they use AI as a sounding board rather than to ghostwrite.

Knowledge workers can also use AI’s outputs to examine topics from multiple viewpoints, deepening their knowledge.

A research scholar in our study probed an AI system for journal papers that used a concept he was using, but in contrasting settings, which helped him understand the concept from different angles. A lawyer followed up on legal citations suggested by an AI model by asking for different types of illustrative cases. That helped her fully understand the original case and write a strong legal brief.

Leaders of organizations can encourage knowledge workers to develop friction-generating queries of AI models.

A straightforward approach is to use prompts that challenge rather than agree with a person’s thinking. People can also draw on their own knowledge, work experiences, and professional ethics to scrutinize AI outputs. Sometimes people realize that a response lacks originality, even if it is factually correct.

Organization leaders can also create programs for knowledge workers to share their experiences and insights. Regular sessions—led by cross-functional AI task forces or AI champions—can also help knowledge workers keep up with rapidly evolving AI tools.

Providing resources for knowledge workers is important, as well. Internal AI bootcamps and workshops led by AI experts or experienced users can help workers understand what AI tools can and cannot do.

A professor leading an initiative about AI use in teaching described how she developed internal competitions with prizes for professors who successfully completed AI bootcamps. Organizations can also offer AI sandboxes—safe, private environments where employees can experiment and learn how to use AI tools.

In these efforts to support knowledge workers, an important detail is pointing out that adding friction can appear to make an AI system work against you, but—as new research shows—it actually helps it work for you.

This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article.

Monideepa Tarafdar, Ph.D., is professor of information systems at UMass Amherst.

Sending reels on social media may seem frivolous or distracting, but it actually functions as a form of relationship maintenance.

How happy are you when you’re on the clock?

#10674. AMD grabs over 40% of new TOP500 list for supercomputers, up 14% since last year

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Lux is the first of two machines AMD is developing as part of a $1 billion deal with the DOE.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With the coming of SC26, also comes a fresh edition of the TOP500 list, which ranks the world's most powerful supercomputers. The newly released list illustrates that AMD powers 40% of the list, up 14% compared to 2025. AMD-powered systems now tally up to 202 on the list, up from 177 last year. New to the list is Lux, a “SI + HPC supercomputer” developed by AMD and the U.S. Department of Energy (DOE), which debuted at position 14.

AMD’s representation in the top 10 is familiar. After debuting in June, China’s LineShine remains in the top slot. El Capitan, powered by AMD chips, is in the second slot, followed by Frontier in third, HPC7 in sixth, and HPC6 in eighth, the same as the most recent TOP500 list.

Lux was announced a little over a year ago in a $1 billion deal between the DOE and AMD to develop two supercomputers. This is the first of the two, and the first time it’s been on the TOP500. The system was built by HPE and developed by AMD and DOE as part of the U.S. government’s Genesis Mission. The machine is housed in Oak Ridge National Laboratory’s Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility (OLCF), alongside machines like Frontier.

Lux relies on over 4,000 Instinct MI355X GPUs separated into over 500 nodes. For HPC workloads using FP64, the machine is capable of 317 PetaFLOPS. For AI training, the machine peaks at 20 ExaFLOPS of FP8, and for inference, 40 ExaFLOPS of FP4. With 288 GB of HBM3E per GPU, the entire system has access to 1.1 PB.

The machine uses AMD EPYC host CPUs — we weren’t able to find details on the specific generation— and AMD’s Pensando networking, with nodes built inside HPE’s ProLiant Compute XD685.

LingKun, LX2 304C 1.55GHz, LingQi, Kylin OS

HPE Cray EX255a, AMD 4th Gen EPYC 24C 1.8GHz, AMD Instinct MI300A, Slingshot-11, TOSS

HPE Cray EX235a, AMD Optimized 3rd Generation EPYC 64C 2GHz, AMD Instinct MI250X, Slingshot-11, HPE Cray OS

HPE Cray EX - Intel Exascale Compute Blade, Xeon CPU Max 9470 52C 2.4GHz, Intel Data Center GPU Max, Slingshot-11

BullSequana XH3000, GH Superchip 72C 3GHz, NVIDIA GH200 Superchip, Quad-Rail NVIDIA InfiniBand NDR200, RedHat Enterprise Linux

HPE Cray EX255a, AMD 4th Gen EPYC 24C 1.8GHz, AMD Instinct MI300A, Slingshot-11, RHEL 9

Microsoft NDv5, Xeon Platinum 8480C 48C 2GHz, NVIDIA H100, NVIDIA Infiniband NDR

HPE Cray EX235a, AMD Optimized 3rd Generation EPYC 64C 2GHz, AMD Instinct MI250X, Slingshot-11, RHEL 8.9

Supercomputer Fugaku, A64FX 48C 2.2GHz, Tofu interconnect D

HPE Cray EX254n, NVIDIA Grace 72C 3.1GHz, NVIDIA GH200 Superchip, Slingshot-11, HPE Cray OS

The machine has 3.5 million node-hours (one node running for one hour) per year, half of which are reserved for Genesis Mission research. Genesis Mission was established through an executive order in November 2025 with the stated goal of creating a “dedicated, coordinated national effort to unleash a new age of AI‑accelerated innovation and discovery that can solve the most challenging problems of this century.”

More specifically, the order lays out training scientific foundation models on Federal datasets, and creating “AI agents to test new hypotheses, automate research workflows, and accelerate scientific breakthroughs.” Lux is the first dedicated supercomputer for the Genesis Mission, but it joins existing machines that have been at least partially dedicated to research, including the Intel-powered Aurora at Argonne National Laboratory, which arrived fourth in the most recent TOP500.

Lux is built primarily for AI research, not as a successor to Frontier. Discovery is on track to debut as a next-gen flagship exascale supercomputer in 2028, also developed jointly by the DOE, AMD, and HPE.

#10670. Google Wallet rolling out digital Google Home keys with supported locks on Android

Google Wallet for Android is rolling out support for digital home keys if you have a compatible smart lock in Google Home.

After adding a home key to Google Wallet, you can Tap to Unlock with your phone. This leverages NFC and “works even when your phone screen is locked” by default. However, you can adjust verification settings in the Google Wallet app to require screen lock or biometric authentication. This requires Android 9+.

There’s also support for Unlock on Approach with an Aliro Ultra Wideband (UWB)-compatible Android phone (see below) and an Aliro UWB-compatible lock. Wear OS watches are not supported at launch.

Behind the scenes, the digital key is linked to the Google Home app rather than a single lock. As such, “each of your compatible Aliro locks will work with a single key in your Google Wallet.” Keys are added to Google Wallet using the Home app, which will prompt you to save after setup. You can also go to Home settings > Google Wallet key.

Crazy to me that Google's own smart lock with Yale doesn't have this functionality. Seems like a huge miss for them to not be forward looking with their own hardware here.

Afterward, your key “automatically syncs across compatible Android phones signed into your Google Wallet account.”

The card in Google Wallet features a simple Google Home logo with your home’s name in the top-right corner.

The following locks are supported at launch: Aqara u400 (UWB and NFC), Schlage Sense Pro (NFC at launch and UWB to follow), and Nuki Keypad 2 NFC. Support for more models is coming.

Additionally, support is rolling out first to the Google Home Public Preview before coming to all users “over the coming weeks.”

#10666. The Lenovo Legion Tab Gen 5 would be my perfect Steam tablet if it didn’t cost $900

For every Android-powered slate I’ve used, only Nvidia’s Shield Tablet really stands out in my memory. Part of the company’s occasional (and all-but-dead) dip into hardware products, the Shield Tablet — and its revitalized K1 follow-up, which I also owned through a recall program on the original — carried me through the back half of college, frequently keeping my Chromebook in my backpack in lieu of a mini Bluetooth keyboard or the Shield Tablet’s built-in stylus. Its front-firing speakers made it great for movie-watching, and while I’m not much of a mobile gamer, I did find it to be a great device for emulation, especially when paired with an external controller.

Even if Nvidia hadn’t abandoned the hardware game at the end of the last decade, it’s found new and much more destructive ways to make piles of cash at the expense of your easy access to RAM. And considering the market for Android tablets in general, it’s not all too surprising to see very few companies trying to make headway into offering a miniature, gaming-focused tablet that can easily be thrown in a backpack. Lenovo, however, has managed to fill its spot quite well. 9to5Google’s own Ben Schoon found himself smitten with the Gen 3 model nearly two years ago, and now, its Gen 5 successor has found its way into my nostalgic hands.

But expectations around mobile experiences have come a long way since 2016. Whether the Legion Tab Gen 5 can win over your heart really depends on your patience with some old-school Android jank — and whether your wallet can withstand an exceptionally-high asking price. Still, with recent advancements in PC emulation, it’s hard to say Lenovo isn’t onto something.

Considering the decade between their respective releases, I’m actually quite surprised just how much this tablet reminds me of the original Shield Tablet. Its 8.8-inch 16:10 LCD panel combined with a boxy matte-black design and a near-identical weight meant that I immediately felt at home holding this thing in my hands. You lose out on a couple of clutch features — most notably the built-in stylus — but largely speaking, this feels like the correct size and shape for a miniature gaming tablet.

Still, it’s not all perfect. Take, for example, the completely unnecessary camera bump that prevents this slate from properly laying flat on a desk. No one is buying this thing to use the camera, and judging by my very limited experience trying it out, I’m fairly confident whatever potato sensor is shoved in here would fit fine flush with the chassis. I’m pretty fed up with OEMs leaning on camera bumps to make any device look or feel more premium than it actually is, and that goes double quadruple for tablets like this.

Just like its predecessor, this thing still picks up smudges and fingerprints at an unreasonable level, its matte-black finish unable to hide anything. 360 grams is also a little heavier than I’d expect for a slate of this size, though to be fair, it’s much more noticeable in portrait than landscape. Holding the tablet with two hands to watch a movie or make my way through another round of Balatro made its top-heaviness much less noticeable.

This display is sharp and fast — with one minor caveat — and reasonably bright in all circumstances except direct sunlight thanks to its 600 nits of brightness (and 800 in HBM). Really, it’s the LCD of it all that gets me here; this thing is nearly $1,000, and the lack of OLED alone makes it difficult to recommend at this price. It’s not an outright bad panel, but I found its contrast levels to be pretty lacking, with most blacks only ever showing up as dark grays on-screen.

Oh, and that caveat: On two different occasions, I caught the tablet stuck in 60Hz mode, regardless of what refresh rate option I selected in settings. Switching back and forth between modes a few times eventually unstuck it, but it’s definitely a bug worth being aware of.

I think I’d feel a lot better about recommending this to someone looking for a content consumption device if this screen looked a little more premium. Your mileage may vary though — if you can get your hands on a demo unit before buying one, if only to see the display, I’d recommend it. For everyone else, this price tag might have you waiting for the OLED panel rumored to be arriving on next year’s successor.

At $900 — or potentially more, considering this tablet actually launched at $850 — the Legion Tablet’s hardware leaves something to be desired. Biometrics are the most obvious weak spot; camera-based face unlock is the only biometric option provided here, without a fingerprint scanner in sight. I wouldn’t blame some if they saw this as a dealbreaker, because even if you’re willing to put up with the security concerns that a basic front-facing camera brings to the table, I can promise you you’ll be typing the code in all the time. Whether you’re trying to unlock in a darker environment or it’s just been four hours since you last unlocked the slate, make sure you have your pin memorized.

If you’re primarily buying this as a content consumption machine, I think you’ll mostly have a great time. Lenovo built stereo sound into this slate, but did so in one of the smartest ways I’ve ever seen, ensuring all speakers are located to the right side along the bottom and top of the device. This might sound a little unbalanced in portrait mode, but in landscape, it guarantees you’re never actually covering up the speakers while holding the slate in your hands, something practically every other equivalent tablet is guilty of. No headphone jack here, unfortunately, something I would’ve liked to have seen on a gaming-focused device.

I find battery life genuinely difficult to judge on tablets, especially if you don’t pick it up every single hour of every single day, but I’ve been impressed overall with the Legion Tab’s longevity. Some weekend YouTube binge watching sessions at relatively high brightness settings left me with plenty of juice in the tank, and its standby time between sessions has been excellent. Obviously, if you’re really pushing your graphical limits on this thing — or if you’re emulating non-native software through something like DroidDeck — you’re going to see a bit of a dip, but combined with fast charging and two USB-C ports for both portrait and landscape access, battery life gets a big thumbs-up from me.

Lenovo’s packed a couple of accessories in the box with this thing, but I wouldn’t get too excited. While it’s nice to see a 68W charger included for those aforementioned fast top-offs, the included folio case is just awful. Not only does the triangle-shaped flap barely stick together to form a proper stand, but one of the corners of my case was loose enough to frequently flex back and forth. Holding the tablet while wrapped in its case just felt bad, in the way that so many bundled cases often feel, and a quick look at Amazon shows that basically no recognizable brand — or unrecognizable, for that matter — has stepped up to offer something better.

The Legion Tablet also includes a screen protector in the box, if that’s how you roll.

Unsurprisingly, the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 in here can handle effectively anything you dare to throw at it. While my mobile games might be limited to more casual, indie fare like Balatro — and, by the by, if you’re still enjoying Balatro a couple of years later, the size and shape of this device makes it perfect — graphically-intense games played fine. Asphalt Legends or the HoYoverse games might not be my cup of tea, but they perform excellently on this device, and compared to the 12-inch tablets companies like Samsung and Amazon have become obsessed with, the display size here actually feels perfect.

We definitely have to talk about emulation, though, because it’s increasingly becoming the main focus of mobile gamers everywhere, and this feels like a tablet perfectly designed for something like DroidDeck. Getting Steam emulation — complete with the Deck’s usual Big Picture mode UI — available as an application on the Legion Tab Gen 5 takes all of two minutes and a handful of taps on your display, and I have to say, I’m pretty shocked at just how impressive the overall experience is right now.

DroidDeck gave me the option to bring my mini gaming tablet experience full-circle. While my Steam library has no shortage of games I think could run without stressing this slate, I wanted to push the envelope a little bit. Persona 3 Reload felt like as good a candidate as any: a modern title that doesn’t necessarily require quick response times and fits virtual controls quite nicely. Plus, most of my gaming time on Nvidia’s tablet a decade ago was spent working through Persona 3 Portable; like poetry, it rhymes. I installed the game, ran it on the Legion Tab, and to my surprise and delight, it was absolutely in a playable state.

Now, there are some caveats here. I did drop the game’s graphics preset to low, and even still, there were some occasional hiccups while running around an early dungeon. The game’s initial intros and disclaimers actually gave the impression the title wouldn’t run at all, but I was able to sneak up on a shadow, use a slash attack to begin the battle — basically the only thing in P3R that remotely requires accurate timing — and issue commands to my player character and party mates.

Obviously, your experience emulating Steam titles on the Legion Tab will vary on what you want to play, and with such wide variables between experiences, I probably wouldn’t push most gamers to run out and pick one up right away. It might be early days for DroidDeck, but considering how we’ve already seen it progress, I wouldn’t be surprised to see some buyers picking this up as a Valve-esque take on a gaming tablet.

With all of that power under the hood, though, it’s Lenovo’s take on Android that leaves a lot to be desired. It’s not just that this feels like a rough-and-ready take on AOSP, an unrefined package that would feel more in line with devices from a decade ago, though, yes, that’s half the struggle here. But at its worst, the Legion Tab Gen 5 simply feels unoptimized in daily use.

Scrolling through web pages or various apps often felt sluggish and heavy, like the UI elements on screen never actually match the movement of my finger, and it’s unclear how much of that was due to the refresh rate bug I described above. Auto-rotate was both super finnicky and super sluggish, alternating back and forth between one state or the other, and the auto-rotate button that pops up when it’s disabled would frequently fail to appear. Occasionally, I’d run into app issues, such as choppy playback on a 1440p YouTube video that got so bad — regardless of what resolution I selected — that I had to fully force close the app in settings to fix it. Even getting the slate to reliably wake up (or not wake up) when picking it up was annoying, as it’s simultaneously sensitive enough to frequently turn on when I don’t want it to, or to stay asleep when I’m trying to unlock it. Inevitably, I turned that setting off.

You might think that shelling out $900 for an Android tablet would buy you an out from pre-installed bloatware; you’d be wrong. During setup, the Legion Tab attempts to install a whole host of apps and games, including Perplexity, TikTok, Temu, several match-three games that I’ve truly never heard of, and no less than two mahjong and solitaire titles each. In fact, there might actually be three solitaire titles depending on what “Jigsawcard Solitaire Puzzle” is, but I unchecked all of these apps during setup and didn’t deem the title worthy of bothering to check.

While Lenovo has been trying to improve its software update policies on some recent devices, this slate isn’t among that group. To my knowledge, there is no official update schedule for this device, but based on past performance, I’d expect somewhere around 2 OS upgrades and 4 years of security patches, give or take.

I would’ve loved nothing more than to recommend this year’s Legion Tab to small tablet fans and gamers everywhere, but when this thing is $200 more than Apple’s latest iPad Mini — which in and of itself is absolutely overpriced — and $100 more than the base OLED Steam Deck post-price hike, I just have a hard time telling anyone to buy it. Really, its Steam performance was one of the few areas that made me feel truly excited by this thing, but it’s simply too unpredictable an experience at this point (and price) for me to recommend.

Even setting aside the price, there’s simply too much here that feels unfinished or rough around the edges for my liking. A handful of bug fixes and a couple of additional optimization passes could fix a lot of my more minor quibbles, but despite what I just said, you can only ignore the $900 price tag for so long before it bites you in the ass.

We have seen a post-iPad Mini discount bringing this slate to an Apple-matching $700 ahead of the holidays, but even that price is $150 more than its predecessor was at launch without a whole lot to show for it. If you’re looking for a gamer-friendly tiny tablet, I do think Legion is a brand worth keeping your eye on, especially as DroidDeck and other Steam emulation apps continue to evolve. But at this point — and for this price — your best bet is waiting for whatever model follows this one. It might still be stupid expensive, but at least you’ll be getting OLED.

A decade ago, Nvidia’s Shield Tablet felt like a no-brainer addition to my everyday carry. But in this economy, gamers and tablet fans alike are simply better off dropping $900 elsewhere.
