#10665. Researchers ‘recognize that AI will never replace HPC,’ says AMD executive — high-precision workloads will ‘exist for a very long time’ even as AI usage expands

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

While AI continues to reshape industries across science and mathematics, traditional supercomputing has seemingly taken a back seat. However, AMD's Scott Tease told Tom's Hardware Premium that most HPC sites “recognize that AI will never replace HPC,” and suggests that building a heterogeneous compute stack is the best path forward to tackle bleeding-edge research, despite Nvidia pushing performance on lower-precision data types higher for AI workloads at the cost of native performance for the important double-precision FP64 format that’s critical for HPC workloads.

A heterogeneous compute stack brings together quantum, HPC, and AI under one roof, a fleet that AMD is helping pilot at Oak Ridge National Laboratory alongside IBM. Tease is AMD’s corporate vice president of HPC and sovereign AI, who joined the company earlier this year after more than a decade at Lenovo (and, before that, another 13 years at IBM). We spoke with Tease ahead of SC26 to get a read on the state of HPC, how it intersects with AI, and what’s actually driving the research community in a time when computer chips are driving headlines about international espionage plots.

FP64 is important for HPC workloads because of its precision. FP64 is a hot-button issue, largely due to how Nvidia’s GPUs have progressed over the past two generations. Or rather, how they haven't progressed. Nvidia’s latest Rubin GPU has 33 TFLOPs of FP64 compute, which is lower than the four-year-old H100’s 34 TFLOPs of FP64. For context, AMD’s year-old Instinct MI355X peaks at 78.6 TFLOPs of FP64 compute, while its upcoming HPC-angled MI430X peaks at 288 TFLOPs.

Nvidia has leaned on emulation to drive FP64 performance higher, a tactic that AMD is now pushing back on, but regardless, it’s easy to see why the HPC world could feel left behind in this new AI era. AI is defined by fuzzy, low-precision data formats, which is diametrically opposed to the accuracy of high-precision FP64. And, over the last generations of hardware from the big green giant that is Nvidia, FP64 performance has seemingly stalled in favor of driving low-precision data formats for AI workloads.

“Maybe a few years ago, people in HPC might have felt a little threatened by AI because it was taking some of the limelight,” Tease told Tom’s Hardware Premium. “But I think now they’ve come to realize that AI is an essential part of scientific discovery today. It’s just, how are you going to deploy it?”

That central question recontextualizes the apparent conflict between AI compute and HPC compute. Tease pointed to researchers spawning dozens of agents to explore viable paths for a given intent, while still leaving the complex simulations for a traditional FP64-driven HPC fleet. “...to try to do that with old approaches where you have islands of CPUs or islands of GPUs or something like that, and they're very different. They're not interconnected. It's just not going to be able to keep up,” Tease said.

One of the challenges right now is actually connecting those systems, which is an area AMD is exploring with its recent collaboration with IBM and Oak Ridge National Laboratory to deploy AMD’s “classical” computing stack in the Umbra HPC system alongside IBM’s 129-qubit Quantum Nighthawk quantum computer. The systems will connect to the Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility, allowing researchers to “explore how integrated quantum, AI, and scientific computing can accelerate scientific discoveries and national security.”

If the HPC crowd felt slighted by AI, you can imagine how quantum researchers probably feel.

Regardless, Tease says the future of scientific computing looks like a unified fleet that brings together AI, HPC, and quantum. “There [are] going to be a lot of problems where quantum will not be the be-all, end-all. There are some problems like human health, chemistry, where quantum’s going to allow us to do things we really can’t do with classical computing today. So having those things coexist, having them in a single workload, is where we want to get to.”

Scheduling workloads for HPC is a solved game, with open-source schedulers like Slurm driving much of the TOP500 to properly schedule workloads on supercomputers. Tease described something that goes deeper to connect these systems. “What we want to be able to do is not only do those kinds of ‘where should it go, here or there’ for HPC, we want to figure out when AI could be applied to it, when [GPU acceleration is] appropriate… and also when is it best to go in a quantum environment?”

The Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility is a test bed for figuring out how to hook these systems together properly. The press release explicitly states that it’s a “shared environment to evaluate the architectures, software, and workflows that future hybrid quantum systems will require.” The system is a co-design initiative with IBM, bringing together quantum and classical computing into one system.

With the system, researchers are exploring how to accelerate quantum error correction with classical systems, a critical step in quantum computing to weed out environmental influences. The goal is ultimately to accelerate quantum systems in the Quantum Genesis Q competition, an initiative launched by the U.S. Department of Energy that will provide up to $215 million in funding for quantum research.

By late 2028, the competition calls for quantum computers “with at least 100 logical qubits that are capable of performing hundreds of millions [of] fault-tolerant operations.” IBM describes the error correction research that will be performed at Oak Ridge as a “key component” in driving toward that goal. Separately, AMD announced last week an investment in quantum outfit PsiQuantum to provide CPUs, GPUs, and FPGAs to drive the company’s first fault-tolerant quantum system.

Jake Roach is the Senior CPU Analyst at Tom’s Hardware, writing reviews, news, and features about the latest consumer and workstation processors.

#10662. 5 Best AI Anime Video Generators in 2026, and Are AI Anime Videos Worth Making?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI video generators can now turn a prompt or a character image into a few seconds of animation, and anime is one of the most popular styles people ask for. But anime is also one of the hardest styles to get right: flat colors, thin linework and stylized motion expose the weaknesses of video models quickly. For developers and designers, the useful question is not which tool is “best”, but what each one actually offers, what it cannot do yet, and how to fit it into a real production workflow.

This article compares five generators on what their own pages state, covers the problems they all share, asks whether AI anime videos are worth making, and then explains how the models work and how designers and developers can build a practical workflow, including ffmpeg and API-integration examples. “Best” here depends on the job: the tools are listed in no ranking order, each is linked once to its official site for reference, and the comparison is based on public vendor pages reviewed in November 2026. Features, models and pricing change often, so verify current details before relying on any of them.

We reviewed each vendor’s own site and compared what it states on the same points. This is a documentation-based comparison, not a lab benchmark. Where a page does not state something, we say so instead of guessing.

AI anime video generator by Pollo AI sits within a general AI video platform. On its main site, Pollo describes generating video from text prompts or uploaded images and editing existing videos, such as adding, changing, removing or restyling content. It also offers AI image generation, so a character image and the animation can be made in one place.

Strengths for anime work: text-to-video and image-to-video in the same tool, plus video editing and restyling, which suits experimenting with looks.

Limitations and gaps: the pages we could read do not name the underlying models, list credit amounts or pricing, or describe anime-specific controls such as reference characters or first and last frame inputs. You will need to test consistency yourself.

Fits: creators who want image, video and editing tools in one platform and are comfortable testing results before committing.

Vidu lists text-to-video, image-to-video and reference-to-video modes. For image-to-video you can set first and last frames. Reference-to-video lets you upload several images of a character, object or scene, and saved references can be reused through a My References feature. The site highlights 2D animation and diverse artistic styles, mentions 1080p output and offers an API platform aimed at enterprise use.

Strengths for anime work: the clearest set of consistency tools among the five pages we reviewed, with first and last frames, multi-image references and reusable saved characters.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not specify clip durations beyond examples, detail its anime-specific templates, or list prices. Free credits are mentioned without an amount.

Fits: sequences built around a recurring character, and teams that want an API.

Kling AI presents Kling 4.0 for video, described as offering stable dynamic motion and an audio-visual experience, alongside an image model, a Motion Control tool and a node-based Canvas editor for working through scripts, storyboards, images and video. It also lists MCP and CLI support for batch generation and automated production, and an API platform with documentation.

Strengths for anime work: motion-focused tooling and a workflow environment for storyboards and multi-step production, plus automation options for developers.

Limitations and gaps: the page we reviewed does not detail image-to-video specifics, first and last frame controls, camera control, durations, video resolution or pricing, and it gives no anime-specific guidance.

Fits: teams that value motion control and scripted or batch pipelines.

Hailuo AI, from MiniMax, describes creating AI videos and images from text or photos. Its page shows a reference field and an Omni Reference option, a banner for a model that mentions multimodal generation and editing, and many photo-based templates such as photo-to-motion, transformation and style transfer.

Strengths for anime work: an accessible, template-driven way to animate a still image and try stylized effects quickly.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not explain how reference works, or state camera control, durations, resolution, API availability or pricing. Template-driven results may be harder to control precisely than prompt- and frame-driven workflows.

Fits: quick experiments and social clips made from a single image.

Dreamina describes itself as an all-in-one creative platform for images, videos and talking avatars. For video it lists text-to-video, image-to-video, sketch-to-video, cartoon video generation and reference-to-video, using several Seedance models, along with editing examples such as green-screen replacement. It also offers image generation and upscaling.

Strengths for anime work: a broad toolset, including cartoon-oriented generation, references and editing in one environment.

Limitations and gaps: no official maximum duration or video resolution is stated, and the page notes that displayed pricing is for reference only and that final pricing requires signing in. The breadth of features and models can make it harder to know which one to use for a given shot.

Fits: creators who want images, video and avatars under one roof.

“Not stated” means the vendor page we reviewed did not say, not that the feature does not exist. Treat the table as a checklist of questions to answer in your own tests.

It depends on the job and on what you compare them with. Against a full studio production they are limited. Against having no animation at all, they change what a small team or solo creator can attempt.

Treat the generator as a fast prototyping and shot-making assistant, not a replacement for writing, direction and design. Audience reaction also varies, and some viewers are openly critical of AI-made work, so quality and disclosure both matter.

Most current generators are diffusion models extended to video. As in image diffusion, the model starts from random noise and removes it step by step, guided by your inputs. The extension to video, described in research such as Video Diffusion Models, is that the model denoises a whole sequence of frames together, so that motion and appearance stay coherent over time. Many systems work in a compressed latent space rather than on raw pixels to keep generation affordable.

Create an original character sheet with front, side and three-quarter views, a fixed color palette and a clear silhouette. Keep it simple: fewer fine details means less flicker.

Break the sequence into short shots with one action and one camera move each. A four-shot, twenty-second sequence is more realistic than one long generation.

Start each shot from a clean keyframe of your character. Where a tool supports first and last frames or reference images, use them to define the pose and keep identity stable.

Describe subject, action, camera and mood, and add a style anchor. For example: “The girl slowly turns toward the camera, wind moves her hair, petals drift past, gentle push-in, 2D cel shading, clean linework.”

Scrub through each clip for warped hands, drifting faces and flickering lines. Discard or regenerate failures instead of hoping nobody notices.

Join the best takes, color-match them, add sound and captions, and add disclosure where a platform requires it.

Developers can make the pipeline repeatable. Two patterns help: scripted post-processing with ffmpeg, and a resilient job-based client for any generation API.

The script below extracts a last frame to seed the next shot, normalizes clips to a common size and frame rate so they can be joined, concatenates them, and optionally holds each frame twice for an “on twos” look. Adjust sizes and frame rates to your project.

Video generation is usually asynchronous: you submit a job, then poll or receive a callback. The sketch below shows the pattern with a timeout and backoff. The endpoints and fields are placeholders, so follow your provider’s documentation for real ones.

Most are diffusion models for video. They start from noise and remove it step by step across a sequence of frames, guided by a text prompt and often a starting image or reference images, until coherent motion appears.

Usually, for consistency. The image locks the character and style, while the prompt only needs to describe motion.

Thin lines and flat color regions make small frame-to-frame changes visible. Simpler designs, slower motion and post-processing reduce it.

Partly. Reference images, saved characters and shared keyframes help, but you should still review every shot for drift.

For short clips, concept previews and storyboards, often yes. For long, dialogue-driven stories, not yet.

It depends on the tool and plan. Read the current terms and make sure you have rights to any characters or assets you upload.

Run the same character image and prompt through each, then compare consistency, line stability, motion quality and cost per usable second.

AI anime video generators are useful, but uneven. The five tools reviewed here differ in input modes, consistency tools, developer access and how much their own pages say, and none removes the core problems of consistency, flicker and limited acting. The practical approach is to test several tools on the same shots, keep your workflow tool-agnostic, design characters that are simple and consistent, and review every clip before it ships. Used that way, they are a capable prototyping and shot-making aid, not a substitute for design and direction.

#10660. The iPhone Duo’s rocky launch is a reminder of how far Android foldables have come

Good morning. Today I’m finding open source alternatives to all of my apps, poring through Pitchfork’s list of the 100 best indie rock albums to find ones I haven’t listened to, and preparing my body for November 19th by pre-loading GTA VI (and replaying GTA IV in the meantime).

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

On Monday, I unboxed the iPhone Duo I pre-ordered back in October and experienced a disarming sense of déjà vu. It’s beautiful hardware married to a messy, often painful software onboarding experience atypical of Apple’s usual polish. It reminds me of the Galaxy Fold’s early years.

I was most looking forward to the combination of a foldable form factor and Apple’s superior app ecosystem, but even the apps that have been updated to support the new aspect ratio, on both the front and back, feel ungainly and buggy. In moving UI elements from the bottom to the right, even with the display open, the Duo forces a rebuilding of essential muscle memory that’s prevailed across mobile platforms, including Android foldables, for 20 years.

The front display, in particular, is a massive test of one’s patience: apps, even ones ostensibly optimized for its stout dimensions, often feel unusable, with UI elements that would be prominent on the iPhone 18 Pro hidden behind overflow menus on the Duo. It’s exasperating and, frankly, boggling.

But what’s interesting is the enthusiasm with which developers are approaching the new form factor. Reddit threads reveal dozens of Duo-optimized app recommendations that feel novel and, if not polished, then propulsive. There’s a fascinating delta between waiting for my bank, or Starbucks, or Kobo, to get its act together to support the Duo and seeking out net-new opportunities and experiences.

I spent most of the summer using the Galaxy Z Fold 8, and I didn’t expect going back to it to feel so stately and predictable: the showcase of a platform with fewer highs but none of the Duo’s current lows.

I’m not sure if I’m going to keep the Duo and wait out its colicky infancy. But I do know that until now I hadn’t given Samsung enough credit for the amount of work it did to make foldable software feel ordinary, and that’s the highest compliment I can pay.

This section has some of our favorite stuff from around the internet. Great videos, weird memes, fun reads, recommended apps, whatever. Send us your recommendations!

Andrew’s look at the Boox Picco is a nice reminder that a gadget can be interesting because it does less. The $109 e-reader has a 4-inch screen, physical page-turn buttons, and an included magnetic case that can attach to a compatible phone. It competes directly with XTEINK’s excellent X4 Pro, which features a rudimentary touchscreen and backlight for around $100, though its magnets are built in.

Both products are wonderful examples of hardware companies adapting to the growing size and complexity of smartphones and seeing an opening for single-purpose products that do one thing really well. Plus, it’s fun to stick a thing to the back of your phone and… read it.

For something you can explore without buying another device, Paolo Mainardi turned his personal website into a point-and-click adventure. Articles live in books, links sit on a notice board, and his about page is a letter in a lighthouse. The Context Island is an incredibly thoughtful and unique approach to “web” design, and it’s another example of how AI can really help bring crazy ideas to fruition.

We’ll likely find out today whether Jensen Huang will be removed as Nvidia’s CEO. According to the WSJ, the company’s board is preparing to oust the company’s singular chief executive, who co-founded it in 1993.

Momentum picked up after the New York Times reported that in addition to his three-year affair with Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese spy, Jensen had a relationship with one of his direct reports, who still works at the company.

#10659. New US rules aim to restrict Chinese firms’ remote access to AI servers and infrastructure

The Bureau of Industry and Security has released an interim final rule to ensure physical restrictions cannot be bypassed by using cloud-based infrastructure.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

New steps have been taken to ensure that countries and companies that are not on whitelists that would allow them to import advanced AI hardware cannot use cloud-based workarounds. The U.S. Commerce Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) has today unveiled an interim final rule, or IFR, in collaboration with the Department of Treasury and the National Security Council, aimed at backing up existing limitations on the export of hardware built by companies like Nvidia to non-whitelisted companies based in countries such as China. "The rule is designed to ensure that existing restrictions on access to sensitive American technology cannot be circumvented through remote computing arrangements" using data centers in third-party countries, a statement explains.

Nvidia, for example, is only allowed to export its high-performance AI chips to companies that are on a whitelist. But amid ongoing claims of black market GPU sales and a recent crackdown on overseas AI GPU rentals from China, it has become clear that these restrictions perhaps aren't as robust as they were intended to be. Nvidia Blackwell Ultra B300 GPUs were even recently discovered in a data center used by China Telecom.

But while blocking the sale of physical goods is one thing, the cloud-based workaround is another entirely. Nvidia itself told data center operators earlier this month that they must limit remote access to their services — 51% of their business must come from non-Chinese companies. But, again, it's unclear how effective that approach has been in ensuring access to powerful AI capabilities doesn't fall into the wrong hands, even if they're accessed remotely rather than in person.

The new BIS rule means that overseas data centers will be required to do more to limit access to their services where U.S-based technology is being used. Restrictions will also be placed on data centers that supply remote access to companies whose headquarters are deemed to be in higher-risk countries, such as China, Russia, and Iran. A license will also be required for anyone trying to export advanced chips to companies with headquarters in Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, or Brazil. The new requirements will officially go into effect in 90 days.

This latest rule comes against a backdrop of ongoing uncertainty over America's AI crown jewel, Nvidia. Jensen Huang, the company's CEO, resigned after confirming an affair, but is now potentially in line for a quick-fire comeback, despite the fact that the woman involved in the affair is an alleged Chinese spy.

#10655. Google launches Intelligent Eyewear glasses: $395+, pre-order today, ships Dec. 10

After years of development, Google is launching its second foray into smart glasses with Intelligent Eyewear that supports Android and iOS. Pre-orders start today at Gentle Monster and Warby Parker, with the audio glasses shipping on December 10.

Hands-on: Google and Samsung’s vision for Android XR glasses is 20/20

Intelligent Eyewear is meant to be worn all day rather than the sunglasses-heavy approach taken by others. The frames are 40-44 grams with the weight balanced evenly.

There is a 12-megapixel ultra-wide camera for photos and videos (3K 30FPS or 1080p). You get image stabilization and HDR processing, while four microphones work to provide spatial audio capture. Google is also quite proud of its noise cancellation work.

There are two open-ear speakers for balanced audio and clear vocals. Control is done by a touchpad that accepts taps and swipes, or via voice commands, with Google debuting a new “Hey Gemini” hotword.

Another way to control Intelligent Eyewear is through your Pixel Watch or Galaxy Watch, with Fitbit Air support coming next year:

Notably, Gemini commands that invoke the camera — like visual search or translation — will not save images or videos to your Gemini History. These visuals won’t be used to train AI models or for ad targeting. Additionally, the glasses will “automatically redact bystanders’ faces from these visuals before Gemini processes them.”

Battery life (245mAh) is rated at nine hours on a single charge, with a fully topped-up charging case (Gentle Monster’s shown below) offering seven full charges. Power is transferred via contact pins located on the underside of the nose bridge.

Android XR runs on the Qualcomm Snapdragon AR1 Gen 1 with 2GB of RAM and 64GB of storage. Other specifications include Bluetooth 5.4, Wi-Fi 6E, and IPX4 water resistance. Samsung is responsible for engineering the hardware, while there are two eyewear brands at launch.

Warby Parker Intelligent Eyewear is available in three styles and a total of 14 variations. Alden, Reid, and Wren are inspired by the brand’s “most beloved silhouettes.” Pricing starts at $395 for optical and sun styles in Pine, Saddle, and Jet Black.

The Gentle Monster Intelligent Eyewear collection features six frames in bold colors like dark red, pink, gray, and yellow, with 16 total variations. They can be paired with tinted, clear, blue, or brown lenses, with prescription options available for most designs. Pricing starts at $449 and will be available in the US and South Korea.

Intelligent Eyewear can be used for contextual visual searches, asking Gemini about your day, and having it accomplish tasks.

Google Maps offers turn-by-turn directions, while there’s real-time Google Translate in over 70 languages.

Like on phones, Gemini Guided Vision “offers conversational audio descriptions to people with vision impairments.” This can be used to read fine print, identify color differences, find items, and explore unfamiliar places.

These glasses also serve as always-available headphones with support for YouTube Music, Spotify, Apple Music, and Audible. The Glasses companion app supports:

#10653. China retains fastest supercomputer in the world crown as AI data centers soak up new hardware

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

As hyperscalers want more AI, traditional supercomputers take a back seat.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

China's LineShine remains the world's highest-performing supercomputer, according to the 68th edition of the Top500 list published at SC26 in Chicago on Monday. For the first time in decades, the supercomputer ranking saw exceptionally few additions despite unprecedented demand for high-performance computing hardware, as the vast amounts of compute resources deployed these days are destined for artificial intelligence (AI) data centers rather than traditional supercomputers.

China's LineShine, installed at the National Supercomputer Center in Shenzhen, offers sustained FP64 compute performance of 2.198 FP64 ExaFLOPS in the High Performance Linpack (HPL) benchmark at 42.22 MW. The machine relies on the homegrown LingKun platform with 304-core Armx9-based LX2 processors clocked at 1.55 GHz (13,789,440 cores in total) and the proprietary LingQi interconnect. Unlike the other four exascale-class supercomputers, LineShine does not use any compute accelerators and exclusively relies on CPUs, which is largely why it only gains 3.6X performance when moving from FP64 to mixed-precision data, which is lower than systems that use low-precision accelerators.

America's most powerful El Capitan supercomputer at the Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory relies on AMD's Instinct MI300A accelerated processing units (APUs), and the HPE Cray EX255a platform delivers 1.809 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 29.69 MW, which makes it significantly more power-efficient than its rival from China. The Frontier supercomputer at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory, based on AMD's 3rd Generation EPYC processors and Instinct MI250X compute GPUs, achieves 1.353 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 24.61 MW. Meanwhile, Aurora at the Argonne National Laboratory, which uses Intel's Xeon CPU Max 9470 processors and Data Center GPU Max accelerators, offers 1.012 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 38.70 MW.

All three American exascale supercomputers use HPE’s Cray EX platforms and Slingshot-11 interconnect.

Germany’s Jupiter Booster at the Jülich Supercomputing Center, which employs Nvidia’s GH200 Grace Hopper Superchips, the BullSequana XH3000 platform, and Nvidia’s quad-rail InfiniBand NDR200 interconnect, delivers 1.0 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 15.79 MW and remains the most energy-efficient exascale supercomputer on the list.

Remarkably, neither the 10 highest-performing supercomputers nor the 10 most energy-efficient machines have changed their positions since June.

Only 27 new supercomputers (5.4% of all machines on the list) made it to the November edition of the Top 500, the lowest number since the project was founded in 1993. The most powerful newcomer is South Korea's Hanggang at the KISTI Supercomputing Center, which occupies the 12th position with 342.6 FP64 PFLOPS. The machine uses Nvidia's GH200 Grace Hopper Superchips and HPE's Cray EX254n platform. Hanggang also ranks eighth in the HPCG benchmark with 4.29 HPCG PFLOPS.

The aggregate HPL performance of the world's 500 highest-performing supercomputers increased by a modest 4.4% over the past six months to 19.57 FP64 ExaFLOPS. Nonetheless, it now takes a machine with 2.857 PFLOPS of sustained FP64 performance to qualify for the TOP500, up 7.3% from 2.662 PFLOPS in June. The performance of the systems ranked 50th and 100th rose from 49.85 PFLOPS to 55.81 PFLOPS (12%) and from 21.85 PFLOPS to 22.74 PFLOPS, respectively. Also, there are now 27 machines that deliver at least 100 PFLOPS of FP64 performance, up from 25 in June, whereas the number of supercomputers equipped with compute accelerators increased from 275 to 291 (58.2%).

The historically low number of new entrants to the Top 500 is particularly noteworthy given the unprecedented scale of investments in advanced computing infrastructure these days. According to estimates by Epoch AI cited by the Top 500 authors, the amount of AI compute capacity added globally every quarter has increased by 2.5X per year. Essentially, the industry's priorities have shifted from construction of machines capable of solving exceptionally complex scientific problems to deployment of vast amounts of compute capacity for commercial AI workloads.

In fact, the world's installed AI compute capacity already exceeds the equivalent of 15 million Nvidia H100 GPUs, according to Epoch AI. Furthermore, as of the fourth quarter of 2025, five major American hyperscalers accounted for an estimated 71% of the world's AI compute resources, much of which was deployed in massive clusters. While demand for advanced computing hardware is at an all-time high, it appears that the vast majority of new hardware never makes it to the world's most known supercomputer ranking.

#10651. Hands-on: Google and Samsung’s vision for Android XR glasses is 20/20

While I acknowledge Meta’s role in bringing smart glasses to a mainstream audience, it has proven to be an irresponsible steward. Its “growth at all costs” mindset has actively poisoned people’s perception of the form factor. Under Meta’s watch, glasses have gained a creepy connotation before their utility has been fully realized.

Google’s second take on smart glasses is the exact opposite of what Meta has done. Every single aspect has been considered, and the result is worthy of being most people’s introduction to the form factor.

Officially, Android XR glasses are known as Gentle Monster and Warby Parker Intelligent Eyewear. The branding is a mouthful, and I’m not quite sure “Intelligent Eyewear” will take off. Something more colloquial, like “Google glasses” — glasses.google is the landing page — or “Samsung glasses,” will probably stick in the public consciousness.

That critique aside, the name reflects how Google wants Intelligent Eyewear to actually replace your existing glasses. Unlike the Ray-Ban trap that I frankly think Meta did not consider, Google’s offerings are not situational or activity-based devices like sunglasses. These are a “smart” upgrade to the assistive device some people have to wear.

As a wearable, smart glasses are a bit like smartwatches in that the platonic design is pretty well established and indistinguishable from traditional eyewear. Unlike watches, glasses are so visible on people’s faces that form overrides everything else.

Fortunately, the Samsung hardware I wore for an hour last week looks and feels like glasses, almost unremarkably so. I’d venture to say that audio-only glasses without a display are a solved design challenge. Google emphasized that, at 41 grams, this is the lightest pair of smart glasses on the market, and it worked to make sure the weight is distributed evenly.

The sides of the stems that face your head feature translucent plastic that lets you see the internal components. I liked that small design choice. You can control the glasses using voice commands, with Google debuting a new “Hey Gemini” hotword for the form factor. While “Hey Google” and “OK Google” remain available, the new one is meant to reflect how big a role Gemini plays on smart glasses.

There is also a touchpad on the right stem that accepts taps and swipes, while a button up top lets you take pictures and record video. Another way you’ll be able to control the glasses is with your Pixel Watch and Galaxy Watch. You can play/pause audio and enable Gemini using double-pinch and wrist gestures. Notably, gesture support is also coming to the Fitbit Air next year.

From its internal testing, Google tells me that which input method people use is pretty situational and ends up being natural. At the beginning of my demo, I favored the touchpad, but by the end, I was leaning toward voice commands.

Talking to Intelligent Eyewear uses Gemini Live. Some prompts, like take a picture or play a song, are one-shot “chat” commands in phone parlance, but for the most part, you’re using Gemini’s back-and-forth conversation mode. Google heavily customized the Gemini Live model for glasses to reduce latency, shorten responses, and tightly integrate the camera.

Even compared to my last demo in May, the latency of voice commands has come a long way. That said, I need more time to see if it’s really fast enough to use as a primary interaction method for most commands.

Similarly, Google will blur all faces and filter out bystander voices before it gets uploaded to Gemini in the cloud. This on-device processing occurs on your phone.

Since that visual media isn’t being saved, the external indicator light won’t be active when you’re talking to Gemini. The LED is only on when photos or videos are being saved and can be reviewed later.

That indicator light features anti-tamper detection that prevents recording. Algorithms detect whether the LED is covered by stickers or markers, with this process featuring hardware verification when the glasses are in their charging case.

Google’s “Glasses” companion app, with Samsung having a near-identical version for Galaxy phones, is pretty similar to the version for Pixel Watch. Unlike the bloated “Meta AI” app, it’s just for settings rather than the very definition of a growth hack with a full chatbot. The difference is staggering, and something Google deserves a bit of credit for in this age of throwing AI into every crevice of day-to-day smartphone usage.

Under the hood, the glasses are offloading a lot of processing to your phone. This includes HDR pipelines and video stabilization. The 12MP camera takes pictures in a 4:3 aspect ratio, while you can record up to 90 seconds of video. Images are transferred immediately to your phone over Wi-Fi, while videos wait until the glasses are charging to preserve battery life. I am so excited by point-of-view photography and the instantaneous capture the glasses offer, and early impressions of camera quality are positive.

Another thing I want to test is how Intelligent Eyewear fares as always-available headphones. There are two open-ear speakers and four microphones, and Google is clearly very proud of the noise-cancellation work in place.

As someone who has been waiting for smart glasses since 2012, I cannot overstate my annoyance at how Meta allowed smart glasses to gain a scummy undertone, if not overtone. It should have been more proactive to societal issues. Meanwhile, Meta releasing glasses without a camera, thus removing the primary utility of real-world context, makes me deeply question its belief that this is the future.

Smart glasses that know what you’re interacting with in the world can be extremely useful. That always-available camera is a genuine physical advantage over smartphones. It unlocks new use cases and convenience that are just not possible with something that goes in your pocket.

Google took a long time to get back to glasses. From what I’ve used so far, the wait will be worth it. The hardware and designs are solid, while the software is chock-full of innovative, well-considered ideas.

#10649. Top 6 AI Text to Speech Tools in 2026: Features, Strengths and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Not long ago, a synthetic voice was easy to spot: flat, rushed and strangely stressed. Today, the best AI text to speech tools can read a script with natural pacing, switch accents, whisper or laugh on cue, and even speak in a voice cloned from a short recording. That makes them useful far beyond screen readers. Developers use them for product voice prompts, marketers for video voiceovers, teachers for course narration and writers for listening back to their own drafts.

This guide explains how modern text-to-speech works, what to look for when choosing a tool, and compares six options: OpenArt, ElevenLabs, Murf AI, Speechify, WellSaid Labs and Amazon Polly. Each section covers strengths, limits and the best use case. The details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing, and plans, models and prices change often, so confirm current terms before you commit.

OpenArt’s AI text to speech tool lives inside a wider creative platform. You open the Audio tab, choose Create voice-over, select a model, paste your script, pick a voice, generate, listen and download. It runs in the browser with nothing to install. According to OpenArt’s page, you can switch between models for each script, including Fish Audio Drama 3, Eleven v4 and Eleven v4 Turbo.

The direction system is what makes it practical for storytelling. You add notes in square brackets, such as [whispering], [laughs] or [pause], directly in the script. OpenArt says Fish Audio Drama 3 gives close control over single words and mid-line changes of mood, while Eleven v4 reads the whole script to infer the emotion of each line and keeps the voice steady when you regenerate a line. Preset voices are labeled by accent, gender and age, and you can upload a recording of your own voice to clone it.

Because OpenArt also makes video and background music, you can build the voiceover, visuals and soundtrack in one place. It describes a Director mode for planning stories up to five minutes, and the voiceover can also be added in any video editor.

Pricing: New accounts get 40 free credits. Paid plans add monthly credits and watermark-free downloads, and commercial rights apply on eligible paid plans. Check the pricing page for current plans.

Best for: creators who want expressive voiceovers together with AI video and music.

ElevenLabs is one of the best-known names in AI voice. Its text to speech page describes a large voice library, voice design and both instant and professional voice cloning, with the latest model, Eleven v4, positioned as the most expressive. It also lists Eleven v3, Multilingual v2 and a low-latency Flash and Turbo family aimed at real-time use, and it claims support for more than 90 languages and accents, with the exact count varying by model.

For developers, there is a text to speech API with SDKs, which makes it practical for apps, games, agents and automated pipelines. The free plan includes 10,000 credits a month, and paid plans start with Starter at $6 a month, then Creator at $22 and Pro at $99, according to the vendor page, with introductory offers on the first month.

Pricing: Free plan with 10,000 credits a month. Paid plans from $6 a month as listed on the vendor page.

Best for: expressive narration, multilingual content and apps that need a voice API.

Murf AI positions itself as a studio for voiceovers. Its page lists more than 200 voices, built with permission and partnership from professional voice actors who earn royalties, and support for 35 or more languages for text to speech, with dubbing in 40 or more. The Studio editor offers pitch, speed and intonation controls and a custom pronunciation library, which helps with brand names and technical terms.

Murf also has an API. Its page describes Falcon 2 for conversational use with time-to-first-audio under 100 milliseconds, priced at $0.01 per minute, and Gen 2 for controllable voiceovers. Gen 2’s “Say it My Way” feature lets you record your own rendition so the model reflects your tone and inflections.

Pricing: Free plan with limits. Paid plan prices are on Murf’s pricing page.

Best for: marketing videos, training and presentations where you want to fine-tune delivery.

Speechify is built around reading aloud. You paste text or upload a PDF, DOCX, TXT or EPUB file, pick a voice, adjust playback speed and listen or download the audio. Its page lists more than 1,000 voices across 60 or more languages, including several regional English accents, and the tool works in the browser and in apps for iOS, Android, Mac and Windows, plus Chrome and Edge extensions.

The use cases lean toward productivity and accessibility: studying, going through emails and documents, language learning and support for people with visual impairments, dyslexia or ADHD. A free account saves your documents and syncs them across devices.

Pricing: You can start free. Paid plans are listed on Speechify’s pricing page.

Best for: students, professionals and anyone who wants to listen to documents and articles.

WellSaid Labs focuses on professional voiceover for businesses. Every voice is built from recordings of a real voice actor who consented and is paid when the voice is used, and the voices are licensed for commercial use. Its page describes hundreds of voices across languages, accents, tones and styles.

Pronunciation control is a highlight. You can highlight a word to see suggested pronunciations and save your choice to a team library that applies to every project, and set pacing, emphasis and pronunciation line by line. WellSaid also describes a Producer product that connects AI assistants to WellSaid through MCP and its API.

Pricing: Contact sales for a quote based on your voiceover volume.

Best for: corporate training, product marketing, courses and advertising where licensing and consistency matter.

Amazon Polly is Amazon Web Services’ text to speech service, and it is aimed squarely at developers. According to AWS, it supports more than 40 languages and language variants with over 100 male and female voices, and it works through APIs in your code. It supports SSML, the W3C standard markup for phrasing, emphasis and intonation, and custom lexicons that fix the pronunciation of acronyms and terms. Output formats include MP3 and OGG.

Pricing is usage-based. AWS lists a free tier for new accounts, with usage allowances that depend on the voice engine, so estimate your character volume before you decide. A short example shows how little code a basic request takes:

The sample uses the AWS SDK for JavaScript, calls the neural engine and sends SSML so that the pause and emphasis are explicit. Adjust the region, voice and engine to match what your account supports.

Pricing: Usage-based with a free tier for new accounts. See the AWS pricing page for current rates.

Best for: apps, IVR systems, notifications and any product that needs automated speech at scale.

Treat this table as a starting point. Features and limits change often, so test two or three tools with the same script.

Modern text-to-speech systems turn written words into audio through several stages.

Newer expressive systems go further by reading the whole script to infer emotion and context, and by accepting direction in the text itself, such as a bracketed note that says to whisper. That is why a single script can produce very different reads depending on the model and the instructions.

If you use a tool with SSML support, you can make a spoken code or number far clearer:

The say-as element tells the engine to read the number digit by digit, and the break and emphasis tags shape the rhythm. Supported tags and attributes differ between engines, so check the documentation for yours.

What is the best AI text to speech tool? It depends on the job. OpenArt suits voiceovers with video and music, ElevenLabs suits expressive multilingual audio, Murf AI suits studio-style control, Speechify suits listening, WellSaid Labs suits licensed corporate work and Amazon Polly suits developers.

Is AI text to speech free? Several tools offer free plans or credits, but limits are common. Examples include OpenArt’s free credits for new accounts, ElevenLabs’ free monthly credits and Murf’s limited free plan, which has no commercial licence.

Can I use AI voices commercially? Often yes on paid plans, but the rules differ by tool. Always check the current licence before publishing.

Can AI text to speech clone my voice? Some tools support it, including OpenArt and ElevenLabs, usually from a short recording of your own voice. Only clone voices you have permission to use.

What is SSML? Speech Synthesis Markup Language is a W3C standard that lets you control pauses, emphasis, pronunciation and pitch in synthetic speech. Amazon Polly is one service that supports it.

Which tool is best for developers? Amazon Polly and ElevenLabs both offer APIs, and Murf describes API options as well. Evaluate latency, languages, pricing and SDK support for your use case.

How do I make AI voices sound less robotic? Write conversationally, add pauses and emphasis, correct pronunciations and split long scripts into short sections. Choose a model that is designed for expressive reads.

AI text to speech is now good enough for real projects, but the right tool depends on what you are making. OpenArt is a strong first stop for expressive voiceovers alongside video and music, ElevenLabs offers a deep voice library and API, Murf AI and WellSaid Labs suit polished, controlled narration, Speechify turns documents into audio and Amazon Polly fits straight into developer workflows. Test two or three with your own script, check the licence and always listen to the full result before it goes live.

#10647. Google’s new Android XR glasses work with iOS, Made for iPhone certified

Google today announced Android XR audio glasses called Intelligent Eyewear that notably support iOS with Made for iPhone certification.

Hands-on: Google and Samsung’s vision for Android XR glasses is 20/20

Intelligent Eyewear is made by Samsung with frame designs from Gentle Monster and Warby Parker. There are 30 unique designs in total, and the frames weigh 40-44 grams with a 12-megapixel ultra-wide camera.

Google’s Glasses companion client for iOS handles compute, including HDR processing, and saves images and videos (up to 90 seconds) to Apple Photos. The Made for iPhone certification comes into play for Wi-Fi Aware connectivity that allows for fast media transfers.

Gemini commands that use the camera, like visual search or translation, don’t trigger the light because those photos or videos are not saved anywhere, including Gemini History. Before that media reaches the cloud, Google redacts faces from video feeds and filters out bystander voices. This processing is done by the companion app. Additionally, that media isn’t used to train AI models or target ads.

Other key specifications include four microphones and two open-ear speakers. Besides voice commands with the “Hey Gemini” hotword, there is a touchpad that accepts taps and swipes. A physical on/off switch lets you wear Intelligent Eyewear as regular glasses.

On iOS, Gemini can make calls, send 1:1 messages (including via iMessage), and receive notifications.

Battery life is rated at nine hours on a single charge, while there’s a charging case that stores seven charges. Features include turn-by-turn Google Maps directions and real-time translation. On the streaming front, there’s support for Apple Music, Spotify, YouTube Music, and Audible.

Google’s Android XR glasses require an iPhone 13 Pro or newer.

Warby Parker Intelligent Eyewear is available in three frame designs, starting at $395 for optical and sun styles. Gentle Monster Intelligent Eyewear is available in the US and South Korea from $449.

#10645. Here’s how Google’s Android XR glasses are handling the camera & privacy

It’s a shame, because a camera in smart glasses serves such a functional role, both for the user in just capturing pictures and for the AI assistant built into the experience. I recently talked about how the camera was my favorite part of smart glasses in an issue of our 9to5Google Weekender newsletter, but how it just feels a bit wrong now.

Anyway, with that context in mind, let’s look at how Google is handling this touchy subject.

Importantly, the light on each pair of Android XR glasses is also designed to avoid any tampering. Android XR glasses automatically perform a system check to ensure the light is working on a regular basis – you might catch the light blinking in white and blue while charging which is a part of the system check ensuring the hardware is fully functional. If the light is found not to be working, the camera is disabled.

Continuing on with how the camera interacts with Gemini, Google explained in a conversation with 9to5Google that Gemini is tuned not to use the camera in cases where it’s not obvious that the user wants the camera to be active. An interaction such as “what painting is this” would very clearly imply that the user wants the glasses to view the scene in front of them and identify the art piece, where “how much money is on the table,” Google explained, would ask for a follow-up before using the camera seeing as it’s not obvious that the user expects the camera to be used in that sort of phrase.

Another way in which Android XR glasses will avoid the recording stigma is with their limits. Google is limiting Intelligent Eyewear video recording to 90-second sessions. While that does limit what the user can do, Google said that most recordings fell within that window anyway. Meta, by comparison, offers recordings up to 5 minutes.

Beyond just the glasses app and Gemini, Google says it is making other efforts across its ecosystem to protect against and deter misuse of the camera on Android XR glasses, including:

Technology has changed rapidly over the last few decades. While we've got hardware that's so much more powerful than what came 10 or 20 years ago, there's still a lot of enjoyment to be had in the classics. That's why there's a huge market for retro hardware, from game controllers designed to look like classic gamepads from old consoles, to full re-releases of old machines, with plenty of fun (and well-priced) accessories in between.

If you're buying for a retro aficionado, you're going to want to pick up something that's fun to use and has a serious nostalgic edge. There's a whole host of gear out there for those who prefer an '80s or '90s aesthetic, rather than the products we live with today.

● Check out all these retro PC gift ideas on Amazon

You can relive the glory days of yesteryear today with these retro gift ideas. We've put together some of the best ideas for a wistful techie who can upgrade their setup, with old-school designs ready for today's hardware. Check out the options we've hand-picked for you below.

This miniature recreation of a classic NeoGeo arcade cabinet includes 40 games (the average price of a Neo Geo game in the 1990s was $200, $8000 for all 40 games!) and includes classics like Metal Slug, The King of Fighters and of course, Fatal Fury. The console has scaled versions of the original controls, a 3.5-inch screen, and runs from a USB-C power supply.

Need a bigger screen? You can connect via HDMI. External controllers and NeoGeo Mini Game controllers (available separately) can be used to retro game from the sofa!

This Game Boy-style console is tiny (0.7 x 0.3 x 1.2 inches!) and is powered by the Raspberry Pi Pico's RP2040 microcontroller. Programmable via a web interface, you can make your own games and demos to share with others.

This gorgeous, retro-inspired N Edition Retro Cube 2 wireless speaker from 8BitDo offers stereo sound, with support for 2.4 GHz and Bluetooth connectivity, along with USB for wired use, and includes a wireless charging dock.

A 1:1 scale replica of the gaming handheld that dominated the 1990s. This is a glorious piece of retro tech, preserved in Lego form for your shelves.

Nintendo's Game Boy is an iconic piece of tech, and this Lego model is a replica of the great handheld. It comes with two cartridges, Super Mario Land and The Legend of Zelda: Link's Awakening. The screen can be replaced with a lenticular screen that recreates the original console's pixels.

This retro Hagibis display comes with a 3.5-inch IPS LCD with a native resolution of 960 x 640, although it can scale up to 2,560 x 1,600 if you like punishing your eyes. It also includes several USB-A and USB-C ports with Gen 3.1 speeds, an HDMI port, as well as SD and TF card readers.

This mini PC is retro-inspired, with a shell very reminiscent of the original NES. This is a fully-fledged PC, however, with an AMD Ryzen 7 H 255 CPU and integrated Radeon 780M GPU, along with 16GB RAM and a 1TB SSD.

#10639. Data-Driven Pages That Never Go Stale: A Practical Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every developer has shipped the other kind of page: a lovingly written article or landing page that peaks on launch day and then quietly rots. Screenshots age, statistics expire, rankings slide. Data-driven pages play the opposite game. Built from a living dataset, they republish themselves daily, grow to thousands of URLs, and answer questions nobody had asked when the site launched. This guide breaks down how they work, using a real fan-run stats site as the running example: one public dataset in, more than ten thousand fresh pages out.

Static pages lose ground because the web moves and they do not. Facts expire, competitors publish fresher answers, and search engines reward pages whose information matches today. A page that cannot update itself must be rewritten by hand, so most sites update a few pages and abandon the rest.

Freshness is not one rule for the whole web; it depends on the query. Nobody needs a CSS tutorial rewritten weekly, but statistics, prices, availability and anything with a season attached decays on a schedule. Developers misjudge this constantly, treating every page like documentation when half their catalogue is really a perishable good. Serve a three-month-old stat as if it were current and readers learn the most damaging lesson a site can teach: do not trust the numbers here.

Then there is the maintenance maths. A hundred hand-written pages each needing one review a year is a hundred small debts, and the interest compounds as the catalogue grows. The team updates the flagship pages, promises to return for the rest, and quietly never does. Data-driven architecture is really a staffing decision disguised as a technical one: humans design the system once, and the system does the updating forever.

A data-driven site has three layers: a dataset that refreshes on a schedule, templates that turn records into pages, and URL patterns that map every meaningful combination users might search for. Change the data and every page changes; improve a template and ten thousand pages improve at once.

The running example makes this concrete. A fan-built Overwatch stats tracker republishes Blizzard's public hero data on a daily cycle. From that one feed it generates hero profiles, head-to-head matchup pages and multi-condition tier lists: 10,291 URLs in its sitemap, across six languages, with matchup pages alone accounting for 8,586 of them. Nobody wrote those pages. Somebody designed the system that writes them, which is a very different, and much more scalable, job.

The example runs on Next.js behind a CDN, but the stack is the least important layer of the three. The same pipeline feeding WordPress, Astro or a static site generator would look identical from the reader's side of the glass. Choose boring technology you already know, and spend your originality budget on the dataset and the templates, because that is where readers will feel the difference.

Generated pages stay useful when every URL must earn its existence with data. Set minimum thresholds before a page is published, give each template unique modules its siblings do not have, and noindex combinations with nothing to say. The line between a resource and spam is not automation; it is emptiness.

Quality gates work best when they are public and mechanical. The example site publishes its tier formula openly: 70% of the score from win rate, 30% from pick rate, and a hard floor that excludes heroes picked in under 0.5% of matches from topping the ranking, because a tiny sample cannot prove anything. That single floor is editorial policy expressed as code. Your version might be a minimum number of data points, a minimum price history, or a minimum review count — the number matters less than the fact that the gate exists and applies to every page equally.

Each template also needs modules its siblings cannot copy. A hero profile carries a seven-day trend and its best and worst matchups; a matchup page carries the head-to-head history; a tier list carries the movement since yesterday. Add a short human layer on top — a methodology page explaining the scoring, changelogs narrating what moved — and the catalogue stops reading as output and starts reading as a publication. Google's scaled-content rules target pages manufactured to manipulate rankings with little value; a page that answers a real query with real, current data is the opposite of that. The honest test for any generated URL is simple: if it vanished tonight, would a searcher lose an answer?

Start embarrassingly small: one dataset, one entity type, one template. A football club's results archive, a property portal's sold prices, your own product catalogue — anything structured and refreshed beats a grand plan. Prove one template earns traffic, then add combinations, languages and dashboards in that order.

What is programmatic SEO? Programmatic SEO means generating many pages from a structured dataset using templates, instead of writing each page by hand. Done well, each page answers a distinct search with real data. Done badly, it produces thousands of near-identical pages, which is where its reputation suffers.

Is programmatic SEO against Google's rules? No. Google's spam policies target scaled content created mainly to manipulate rankings, with little or no value for users. Pages generated from real data that answer real queries are publishing. The deciding question is whether a page helps its reader, not how it was produced.

How often should the data behind pages refresh? Match the refresh to how fast the underlying facts change. Sports and game statistics justify daily pulls, prices often need hourly checks, and reference data may only move monthly. Refreshing faster than reality changes wastes crawl budget and makes 'updated' stamps meaningless.

Do generated pages need unique written text? They need unique value, which is not the same thing. Identical paragraphs on ten thousand pages add nothing, but unique data, comparisons and trends are content in their own right. Add short human-written context per template, and let the data carry the differences between pages.

How many pages should a first project launch with? Launch with the smallest set that proves the template: one entity type, a few hundred pages at most. Validate that search engines index them and users engage before adding combinations and languages. Scaling an unproven template just manufactures tomorrow's cleanup job at industrial size.

The sites quietly winning the long game are not publishing more; they are publishing systems. One honest dataset, one well-built template and strict quality gates will outlast a hundred hand-written pages competing on freshness alone. Try it this week: take one dataset you already own, design a single template with four real modules, and ship fifty pages. Watch what the data does next.

Meanwhile, the context that matters most never reaches the model at all. Think about the last time someone said something worth keeping: an idea over coffee, a throwaway comment that unblocks your whole week. What did you do? “Hold on, let me write that down.” Out comes the phone, and by the time you look up, the moment has moved on without you. Or you don’t bother, and it’s gone by the end of the day.

There are many AI recorders already on the market, admittedly with varying levels of quality, so we’ll have to wait and see how Nothing’s differentiates itself.

#10628. China engineers behind J-35 fighter jet pioneer 3D-printed load-bearing structures in military aircraft

China has been at this for over two decades, and they're not slowing down.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Shenyang Aircraft Design and Research Institute, the developers of China’s J-35 fighter jet platform, used metal 3D printing to create large primary load-bearing structures for military aircraft applications. Global Times reports the structure is over 16.4 feet (five meters) in length, using segmented printing and fusion techniques.

Shenyang Aircraft Design and Research Institute, a research organization under the Aviation Industry Corporation of China (AVIC), has been working on 3D-printed aircraft components since 2003. The impressively large 3D-printed components are the culmination of decades of research and development of various metal additive manufacturing techniques.

The engineers are no strangers to large 3D-printed components: in 2005, they achieved secondary load-bearing structures; then, in 2012, large primary load-bearing titanium alloy structures, earning additive manufacturing expert Wang Huaming and his team China’s National Technological Invention Award.

Recently, however, the team created a primary load-bearing structure even larger, at over 16.4 feet. The massive component is not printed in one piece, as printing such a large structure faces warping and cracking issues, as stated in Progress and Challenges of Laser Direct Manufacturing of Large Titanium Structural Components, published in the Chinese Journal of Lasers. To avoid these problems, two techniques were employed: Segmented Additive Manufacturing (SAM) and additive fusion joining. It sounds like fancy words to say it was printed separately and welded, and it technically wouldn’t be wrong — but the key distinction is that it is part of the whole workflow; basically, metal 3D printing into the intentionally designed gaps between the components and creating one whole printed piece.

Additive manufacturing eliminates the need for expensive molds and multi-part construction. Titanium alloys are notoriously difficult to machine, cast components are usually weak, and joining parts using bolts and screws is additional labor — metal 3D printing solves all of this by creating a strong printed part in the desired shape in one large piece, minimizing the need for additional fasteners. By eliminating molds, the team can iterate much faster and create more complex structures that would otherwise not be possible with traditional manufacturing techniques.
