#10624. Running WireGuard for a Development Team: The Hidden Cost of VPN Maintenance

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Setting up WireGuard on a small virtual server is a satisfying infrastructure task. Install the package, generate a key pair, open a UDP port, configure a peer, and connect a laptop. Within an afternoon, your staging environment can sit behind a private tunnel, and your internal dashboard can be restricted to traffic from a known address.

The initial setup is rarely the difficult part.

The real engineering work starts when the team changes. A developer joins, another leaves, someone replaces a laptop, a server needs patching, or a connection suddenly stops working. Each event creates a small maintenance task. Over time, those tasks become an operational responsibility that somebody must own.

This article examines what it takes to operate a self-hosted WireGuard VPN for a development team. We'll look at peer configuration, access management, troubleshooting, monitoring tools, and the engineering time involved. We'll also compare self-hosting with managed business VPN services so teams can make a decision based on their infrastructure needs.

WireGuard is a modern VPN protocol designed to establish encrypted connections between network peers. Its relatively small codebase, modern cryptography, and straightforward configuration make it attractive for developers who want control over their network infrastructure.

A basic WireGuard deployment typically involves:

A server with a reachable public IP address.

A private and public key pair for the server.

A UDP port through which peers communicate.

Peer configuration that defines which addresses are allowed through the tunnel.

A simplified server configuration might look like this:

The corresponding client configuration contains the client's private key and the server's public key:

These examples illustrate the basic relationship between a server and a client. A production configuration will also need appropriate firewall rules, routing, DNS settings, and a plan for distributing configuration securely.

The important distinction is that WireGuard establishes encrypted peer connections. It does not automatically provide a complete employee identity system, onboarding workflow, approval process, or audit dashboard.

Those responsibilities belong to the team operating the infrastructure.

Developers interested in using WireGuard on personal development devices can also explore SitePoint's guide to building a hardened mobile development environment with Termux, Docker, and WireGuard.

A peer is not just an entry in a configuration file. It represents a device that has permission to communicate through your network.

That makes peer management an access-control responsibility.

For a small development team, it is tempting to keep a single configuration file and update it manually whenever someone joins or leaves. This can work initially, but it becomes harder to manage as the number of devices increases.

A useful first step is to maintain a peer inventory:

The inventory should not contain private keys. Private keys must remain on their respective devices and should never be placed in a shared spreadsheet or source repository.

The inventory instead helps administrators answer practical questions:

Which device is associated with this tunnel address?

Has the peer been removed after the device was lost or replaced?

This information becomes especially important when a team uses the VPN to reach staging servers, internal dashboards, databases, or other development resources.

Manual onboarding is manageable when a team has only two or three developers. It becomes unreliable when several people join, leave, or change devices over the year.

A repeatable onboarding process should cover more than generating a key pair.

Confirm that the team member is authorized to access the environment.

Generate a key pair on the user's device.

Add the public key to the server's peer configuration.

Apply the appropriate routing and firewall permissions.

Deliver the client configuration through a secure channel.

Test access to the specific development resources the person needs.

Record the peer owner, device, and approval details.

Offboarding needs an equally clear process:

Identify every peer associated with the departing team member.

Remove the relevant public keys from the server.

Confirm that the configuration change has been applied.

Review any other credentials or allowlists that granted access.

An administrator can inspect the currently configured peers with:

The output helps identify peer public keys, transfer counters, and latest handshake information. It is useful for troubleshooting, but it should not be treated as a complete access audit or proof that a particular person is still authorized.

The broader lesson is to make access changes part of a documented workflow. A peer should not remain active simply because nobody remembers who created it.

One common network-design mistake is assuming that connecting to a VPN should give a developer access to every internal service.

A developer working on a frontend application may need access to a staging web server but not to the production database. A contractor may need access to one test environment without being able to reach internal administration tools.

WireGuard's peer configuration and the surrounding network controls should be designed around those requirements.

Separate tunnel address ranges for different groups.

Firewall rules that restrict traffic to approved services.

Cloud security groups that limit access to specific ports.

Application-level authentication for internal dashboards.

Separate production and staging network boundaries.

The exact implementation depends on the infrastructure. The key point is that VPN membership and application authorization are different controls.

A successful VPN handshake should not be treated as permission to access every resource behind the tunnel.

A VPN can appear simple when it works. Diagnosing it becomes more complicated when a developer is connecting from a home router, a hotel network, or a mobile hotspot.

Incorrect routing or AllowedIPs settings.

A server process or host becoming unavailable.

WireGuard provides useful inspection commands:

These commands help inspect peer information, check the service state, and review recent service logs. The exact systemd service name can differ depending on how the interface was configured.

Administrators should also monitor the underlying server. At minimum, that means checking:

Whether the WireGuard interface is active.

Whether disk or memory usage is approaching a limit.

Whether operating-system and package updates are pending.

A small virtual server may be inexpensive, but it is still infrastructure that needs an owner.

Teams working with alternative tunnel-based development workflows may find SitePoint's tutorial on setting up Cloudflare Tunnels with Docker Compose for persistent webhook debugging useful. It covers a different tunneling use case, but the operational questions around connectivity, service health, and troubleshooting are closely related.

A self-hosted VPN does not need to become a collection of manual tasks. A few established tools can make configuration, monitoring, and debugging more repeatable.

The core tool is, of course, WireGuard. Its command-line utilities allow administrators to inspect peers, manage interfaces, and review connection information. The official documentation is the best starting point for understanding configuration options and platform support.

Ansible can automate repeatable server administration tasks. A team could use playbooks to deploy configuration files, apply system updates, or standardize firewall settings across several VPN hosts.

Sensitive values should be handled with an appropriate secrets-management approach. Private keys should not be committed to a Git repository or stored in plaintext playbooks.

Prometheus is useful for collecting and querying metrics, while Node Exporter exposes Linux host metrics such as CPU, memory, disk, and network statistics.

These tools do not automatically provide a complete WireGuard security audit. They can, however, help identify resource problems and server-health issues before those problems become user-reported outages.

Uptime Kuma provides a self-hosted monitoring dashboard with notifications. It can help teams monitor the availability of a VPN host or related services.

A basic availability check does not prove that every VPN peer can connect successfully. For more meaningful monitoring, combine host checks with an end-to-end test from a separate network.

Wireshark and tcpdump help developers inspect network traffic when diagnosing connectivity problems.

For example, tcpdump can capture UDP traffic on the WireGuard port:

This can help determine whether packets are reaching the server. It does not decrypt WireGuard traffic or replace checks of routing, firewall rules, and peer configuration.

The right toolset depends on the size of the deployment. A team with one VPN server may only need WireGuard's own commands and basic host monitoring. A larger environment may benefit from automation, centralized metrics, and a documented incident process.

A self-hosted VPN can cost very little in direct infrastructure charges. However, the server invoice does not include the engineering time required to operate it.

Consider a hypothetical team with these assumptions:

Two people join and two leave during the year.

The team reviews or rotates device keys quarterly.

Patching and monitoring require about two hours per month.

Six troubleshooting incidents occur annually.

The estimated loaded engineering cost is $60 per hour.

The virtual server costs approximately $6 per month.

The following estimate illustrates how quickly maintenance time can exceed the infrastructure bill.

At $60 per hour, 46 hours represent approximately $2,760 in engineering time. The server adds around $72 per year, bringing the estimated total to $2,832.

These figures are illustrative, not a universal cost benchmark. A team with infrastructure automation and established monitoring may spend considerably less time. A team with frequent staff changes or strict audit requirements may spend more.

The useful exercise is to measure the work your own team performs. Track onboarding, access reviews, patching, incidents, and recovery time for a few months. That gives you a more realistic comparison than looking at hosting prices alone.

Self-hosting is not automatically the best option just because a team has developers who can configure servers.

A managed business VPN may be worth considering when the team needs centralized member management, simpler device administration, or a stable network exit address without maintaining the underlying VPN infrastructure.

Depending on the provider and plan, managed services may offer:

A central dashboard for adding and removing users.

Client applications for common operating systems.

Multi-factor authentication or identity integrations.

For example, PureVPN for Teams is one service in this category. Teams evaluating it—or any other provider—should check the current feature set, pricing, logging practices, access controls, and service terms before making a decision.

A managed service does not remove every security responsibility. The team still needs to decide who receives access, review permissions, protect user accounts, and understand how traffic and metadata are handled.

It also introduces vendor dependency and a recurring subscription cost. Those trade-offs should be considered alongside the engineering time saved.

There are situations where running WireGuard yourself is the more practical option.

You already operate infrastructure. If your team routinely patches servers, manages secrets, monitors services, and responds to incidents, maintaining a VPN may add relatively little work.

You need custom routing. A deployment that connects several private cloud networks, on-premises environments, or specialized subnets may require network controls that are easier to implement directly.

You have strict data-control requirements. Some organizations have policies that limit which third parties can handle network traffic or related metadata. Those requirements may favor infrastructure under the organization's own control.

You have a dedicated platform or security team. If someone is explicitly responsible for access management, monitoring, and incident response, self-hosting can fit naturally into existing operations.

You can automate the lifecycle. Infrastructure-as-code, configuration management, documented peer inventories, and tested recovery procedures can reduce repetitive work and make a self-hosted deployment easier to maintain.

The important distinction is between a deliberately operated service and a one-off server that nobody owns.

Before choosing an approach, ask the following questions:

Who is responsible for the VPN six or twelve months after deployment?

How many people and devices are likely to join or leave?

How are public keys and peer configurations tracked?

How quickly can access be revoked when a device is lost?

Which internal services should each developer be able to reach?

Do you need a fixed egress IP for external allowlists?

What monitoring and recovery process exists if the server fails?

Do your customers or internal policies require access logs or formal reviews?

Does your team have the time and expertise to maintain the infrastructure?

Would a managed service cost less than the engineering time and operational risk it replaces?

If your team already has mature infrastructure practices and needs custom network control, self-hosting may be a sensible choice.

If the team is small, access changes frequently, and nobody has time to own another server, a managed service may be more practical.

WireGuard is an excellent protocol, and deploying it can be a straightforward engineering task. But operating a VPN for a development team involves more than establishing an encrypted connection.

Peer management, onboarding, offboarding, network permissions, monitoring, patching, and troubleshooting all require ongoing attention.

The right choice depends on the team's infrastructure experience, security requirements, size, and available engineering time. Self-hosting offers control and flexibility; a managed service can reduce operational work. Neither option removes the need for sound access-control practices.

Before deciding, calculate the maintenance effort, identify who will own the service, and make sure the design can be maintained as the team changes.

The cost example uses illustrative assumptions rather than current vendor pricing. Managed VPN features and prices can change, so verify provider details before purchasing.

#10619. 403 hyperscale US data centers used staggering 82 terawatts of electricity in a year, more than 80% of Michigan’s entire consumption

Powering them put out an estimated 25.7 million metric tons of CO2, 2–3x a 2018 estimate.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A Harvard-led study estimates that 403 hyperscale U.S. data center sites consumed about 82 TWh of electricity in a year, 1.8% of the country’s total, with more than half coming from gas and coal sources, Wired reports. This compares to the entire state of Michigan selling 99 TWh in 2024. The data centers’ pull also put out an estimated 25.7 million metric tons (Mt) of CO2, which is two to three times a 2018 estimate.

Hyperscale data centers, in this case, refer to sites run by a hyperscale cloud or content provider, or a colocation operator who serves one, with a capacity in the tens of megawatts. The 675 candidate sites came from data vendor Baxtel, with 403 confirmed with satellite imagery and building-footprint maps, at an average capacity of 39.9 MW and a median of 36 MW.

Nobody officially counts what the data centers actually draw from the grid. Instead, the electricity is estimated by multiplying each site’s maximum capacity by the hours in a year and an estimate of how hard the data centers run. For CO2 and the source mix, each site is placed in its grid region, of 23 total in the study, with a regional average mix from the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA). These are then multiplied and added up for the total number. Such estimation methods may reduce the strength of arguments against data center buildouts.

The power is also not evenly laid out, with 142 of the sites located in Virginia alone, taking 21.4 TWh, or about a quarter. PJM, a grid operator for 13 states, including Virginia, and D.C., or America’s largest grid, covers 32.8 TWh, or about 40%, more than any other region. Notably for Virginia, its power draw is actually just a bit below the 22.85 TWh estimate for all U.S. hyperscale sites from 2018. See the table for more details.

Not all power is equal — renewables, for example, can be perceived more favorably — and 53.9% of the estimated draw comes from fossil fuels, 38.2% of the total from gas and 15.2% from coal. Renewables cover 25.3% while nuclear handles the remaining 20.9%. Estimates put this in the 21–31 Mt range for CO2 output, versus the 10.5 Mt estimate from 2018.

Estimated carbon intensity, though, is lower than the average, at 314 g of CO2 per kWh versus 348 for the entire U.S. grid, or about 10% lower. Admittedly, almost a quarter of the load is in regions dirtier than the U.S. average. The likely reason it’s lower is that the biggest loads lie in PJM territory, which is nuclear-heavy, while Oregon, for its part, is hydro-heavy. The estimates, in this case, use 2023 plant data, and the method does not trace which plant feeds which site and also leaves out clean-power contracts and credits.

The study’s results also lag reality as the window ended in April 2025 and, with how much development there has been for AI, among other things, the footprint “could already be substantially larger now in 2026,” lead author Gianluca Guidi said in a statement. Another issue is that the median site capacity in the study is relatively small compared to upcoming buildouts, with many campuses now planned in the hundreds or thousands of megawatts. On-site power, such as gas plants, also may not show up in grid data.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Official numbers may be forthcoming, though, as the Energy Information Administration’s pilot surveys of data center energy use are complete, and the agency is using them to design a survey of the sector, a spokesperson told Wired. The first pilots, which launched in March, relied on web surveys and in-person interviews, with 196 companies asked about energy sources, electricity use, plus servers and cooling, although not covering all states. Gauging the direct human impact is next in the chain, with Francesca Dominici, the study’s corresponding author, saying that quantifying health effects is an important next step.

#10615. Galaxy SmartTag 3 at $20 makes a compelling case to skip the Google Pixel Tag

#10613. The Design Workflow Is Broken. Here Is How Vitalii Khomenko Thinks It Should Be Fixed

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

With 250+ client engagements across both agency and embedded in-house roles, Vitalii Khomenko has seen product teams from the inside in a way few designers have. Here he makes the case for a new kind of design workflow built for the AI era, and explains why the window to adapt is narrower than most founders think.

For the last decade, every startup conversation about speed eventually became a conversation about engineering. Hiring more developers, cutting scope, choosing a simpler stack, it was almost always a dev-side problem. Vitalii Khomenko, Principal Designer at Orizon Design and founder of Fluente LLC, argues that's no longer true. And he has a specific view on what teams should do instead.

What makes Khomenko's perspective worth taking seriously isn't just seniority. It's the specific shape of his experience. Over a career spanning 250+ client engagements, he has worked both as an external specialist brought in to deliver and as an embedded designer who became the single connective thread between product, brand, and execution. That dual vantage point is rare. Most designers live on one side of that line. Khomenko has operated extensively on both, which gives him an unusually clear read on where friction actually originates inside product teams, and where it doesn't.

His work spans early-stage startups finding their first visual language and established brands managing complex, multi-disciplinary design systems. Across that range, certain patterns repeat. And the pattern that started becoming impossible to ignore this year pointed him toward a conclusion that is reshaping how he thinks a modern design workflow should actually be structured.

The bottleneck didn't disappear. It moved. And the workflow needs to move with it.

When shipping code was slow, the existing design workflow made sense. Multiple rounds of revision, user testing, iteration, all of that fit naturally into the window while engineering caught up. That window is now gone, and most design workflows were never updated to reflect it.

"Founders are asking design teams to move at the same speed as an AI-assisted dev pipeline, and design doesn't compress the same way code does," Khomenko says. The result is a workflow that was built around a constraint that no longer exists, applied to a pipeline that has fundamentally changed around it.

The forced choice between polish and speed that product teams are now navigating isn't new as a concept. What's new is how stark it has become, and how directly it traces back to a workflow problem rather than a people problem. "Because development is cheap and fast now, there's constant pressure to ship the next thing immediately," he explains. "But the products that actually retain users are still the ones where the details were considered carefully. Fewer revision rounds isn't a process improvement if it means shipping avoidable friction."

Khomenko has watched this play out across enough teams, at enough stages, to be specific about where the existing workflow breaks down. The issue isn't design judgment. It's where that judgment sits in the process, and what format it's expected to produce at the end.

To understand Khomenko's proposed fix, it helps to understand exactly where the current workflow stalls. For most product teams, the end of the design process looks like this: a designer produces polished, dev-ready screens inside Figma, and hands that file to engineering. Engineering then interprets those screens, translates the visual decisions into code, and builds from there.

That translation step, Figma file to working code, is where the pipeline now breaks down. AI-assisted engineering has made the building part dramatically faster. But the translation step hasn't changed. An engineer still has to read a Figma file, interpret spacing, extract assets, make judgment calls about component structure, and write the implementation from scratch. That process takes the same amount of time it always did.

"The manual workflow inside Figma simply isn't keeping up with AI-boosted engineering," Khomenko says. "The design handoff is quietly becoming the thing everyone is waiting on."

The result is a pipeline where everything around the handoff has accelerated, and the handoff itself has become the single point of drag. Figma, in other words, was built for a world where engineering was the slow part. It wasn't designed to feed a pipeline where engineering moves at AI speed.

This is the core of what Khomenko is proposing, and it's a more significant structural shift than it might first appear.

In his workflow, Figma doesn't disappear. It moves. Rather than serving as both the exploration environment and the final delivery artifact, Figma becomes the visual exploration layer exclusively: the place where brand identity is established, layout logic is worked through, and design direction is validated. It remains the right tool for figuring out what something should look and feel like.

But the final delivery, the artifact that actually goes to engineering, is code. Specifically, components built into prepared libraries that engineering can work from and scale directly, without any translation step in between.

The difference in implementation speed is the point. When a designer delivers a coded component library rather than a Figma file, engineering doesn't need to interpret anything. The decisions are already expressed in the language they're building in. What previously required an engineer to read, interpret, and re-create from a visual reference now arrives ready to use. In a pipeline running on AI-assisted development, that difference compounds fast.

"Figma is still the right place to explore what something should look and feel like," Khomenko says. "But handing over a Figma file and asking engineering to implement it is now the slow path. Delivering in code is the fast path. That's the shift."

Executing this workflow requires a different kind of designer, and Khomenko is direct about that. The UI/UX designer becoming a Design Engineer is, in his assessment, not a distant possibility. It is a matter of time, and the teams treating it as a future consideration rather than a present reality are the ones most likely to find themselves stuck at the handoff.

But Khomenko is equally clear about what this shift doesn't mean. Delivering in code is one part of the role, not the whole of it. The full design cycle isn't going anywhere. Applying branding to imagery, producing animations, building marketing materials, these remain essential creative tasks that require a broad and varied toolkit well beyond code. "The designer of the near future isn't just generating code," he says. "They're moving across the full cycle, brand, motion, marketing, product, with design engineering as an important and growing part of that stack, not a replacement for it."

What changes is that code-ready delivery becomes a core expectation within that stack. The designer who can move fluidly from visual exploration in Figma to component delivery in code, while also carrying the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience, is the profile that the new workflow demands.

Khomenko is candid that no single version of this workflow has fully standardized yet. Every team is figuring it out differently. The tools are evolving, the handoff expectations are shifting, and the processes teams are building around all of it vary considerably. There is no off-the-shelf playbook.

But this is precisely where his 250+ engagements become more than a credential. They're a dataset. Having watched enough teams, at enough stages, through enough cycles of tooling change, Khomenko can see through the noise of individual variation and identify what's directionally true even when the specifics are still in flux. "Each team's workflow is really custom at this stage," he says. "But some general patterns of how this is going to look in the near future are already visible."

Those patterns consistently point in the same direction: design judgment moving earlier, final delivery moving into code, and the designer's role expanding in technical range while staying broad in creative scope. The exact tools and sequences will keep shifting. The underlying logic of the workflow Khomenko is describing is already taking shape in the teams paying closest attention.

For founders and product leads, the workflow Khomenko is describing has direct implications for how teams are staffed, budgeted, and structured.

Front-load design judgment. The biggest leverage in the new workflow isn't faster execution, it's better problem definition before generation starts. Bring senior design thinking in earlier, when it can shape what gets built rather than polish what's already done.

Separate exploration from delivery. Figma and the visual exploration phase serve a distinct purpose from the final delivery layer. Treat them as separate stages with separate expectations, rather than collapsing them into a single step expected to produce everything.

Shift the delivery expectation to code. Whether that means hiring for Design Engineer range, developing it internally, or restructuring handoffs, the end of the design pipeline needs to arrive in a form engineering can work from directly. The translation step is where time goes to die, and it's now the most avoidable part of the process.

Protect the full creative stack. Design engineering matters more than it ever has. It is not, however, the whole job. Teams that reduce design to code generation will lose the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience.

Stay flexible. The workflow is still forming. Teams that build rigidity into their process now will find themselves redoing that work within a year. The teams getting this right are the ones building enough adaptability into their structures to absorb what's still coming.

"The Figma handoff made sense when engineering was the slow part," Khomenko says. "Engineering isn't the slow part anymore. Delivering in code isn't the future of design workflow. For the teams paying attention, it's already the present."

I write about AI and software development from the angle that usually gets skipped: what a spec sheet or benchmark actually implies once you try to build with it. Find me on LinkedIn.

#10612. What My Son Is Learning From Artificial Intelligence

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

My 10-year-old son came to me recently with a question about black holes. Not in the way children usually do, restless and easily redirected, but with the focused intensity of someone who had already been thinking for a while.

He had been talking to an artificial intelligence—specifically, a large language model called Claude.

I looked at their conversation. It had been good, I discovered. The explanations were patient and well-calibrated. He had followed up, pushed back, and received answers that adjusted to his level. By the time he found me, he was genuinely engaged, asking the kind of questions that suggest a mind that is curious and actively at work.

But I have also been watching something else. It is not that he looks things up. Children with questions about black holes have always turned outward, to books or to a particularly knowledgable parent or teacher. What has changed is the speed. When the uncertainty is of a sort that once had to be lived with for a while, my son reaches for an answer sooner than he used to. The question is no longer something to sit with. It is something to resolve. And I find myself wondering what this means about his relationship to sitting with not knowing.

That shift, subtle as it is, may point to something worth taking seriously.

I am a psychoanalyst, and I spend my professional life attending to what develops in the space between people, including what develops when that space is too quickly filled.

Childhood has always involved productive friction. Not knowing how something works, waiting for an answer, trying to figure something out without immediate resolution. These experiences are not incidental to development, but are part of how the mind learns to tolerate uncertainty, to hold a question open, and to struggle toward understanding. The capacity to sit with not-knowing is itself a developmental achievement that underlies creative thinking, emotional regulation, and the ability to navigate a world that rarely resolves cleanly.

I watched this in miniature not long ago. My son was waiting for a package—a computer part he had saved for—and the delivery had been delayed by weather. In an earlier childhood, mine perhaps, this would have been a long afternoon of checking the window. Instead he opened a chat to resolve the uncertainty: how long do weather delays usually last, what determines them, how do you read the tracking data from the delivery company to get the most accurate picture. The conversation was detailed and as far as I could tell the information was reliable. He came away with a small expertise in logistics, but with an estimate that turned out, in the end, to have no relation to anything. The package arrived that evening, on none of the schedules he had so carefully worked out with the help of his AI tool.

The point is not that the AI was wrong, because predicting a delayed truck is difficult. The point is instead that waiting, the old childhood experience of wanting something that has not yet come, was converted into a research problem that could be managed instead of endured. I admired his resourcefulness tremendously and am grateful he had the opportunity to think through a complex problem. I also found myself wondering what the afternoon would have asked of him without it.

AI reduces precisely this sort of friction. It does so efficiently and often helpfully. But consistently removing the experience of not-knowing from a developing mind is not a neutral act. It shapes what the mind comes to expect of itself.

The research on what this does to thinking is new, and the majority of it is about adults. Despite this, it points in a consistent direction. In one widely discussed study from the MIT Media Lab, not yet peer-reviewed, adults who wrote essays with ChatGPT showed weaker and less distributed patterns of brain connectivity than those who wrote without its assistance, and many struggled after writing to quote sentences they had just produced. The researchers called the pattern “cognitive debt.”

Another study, a survey of 666 adults, found that heavier reliance on AI tools was correlated with weaker critical thinking, most pronounced in its youngest participants. The author suggested this might result from the offloading of mental effort itself. None of this is certain but is, instead, a suggestive pattern of early evidence. And the transfer to children is not straightforward. An adult who offloads mental effort has already built the capacity being offloaded; a child is still building it. Does that make the effect smaller in children or larger and more durable? We do not know.

This is different from what smartphones have done. The concern with smartphones has been largely about attention and social life: what children see, how they spend time, how they relate to each other. These are real and serious concerns, but AI introduces a different kind of change. It is not just another screen competing for a child’s focus. It is something closer to an interactive presence that responds, explains, adjusts, and, crucially, never gets tired.

For a developing child, this matters in a way we have not yet fully come to terms with.

Children learn who they are through relationships with other minds. This process is not primarily cognitive but unfolds through the experience of being known and misunderstood, challenged, surprised, and pushed back against. Through encounters like these, children discover that other people have their own inner lives, including their own perspectives and their own limits. They learn how to navigate the gap between what they expect and what they receive from others. Over time, these negotiations, internal and external, form the scaffolding of a self.

What is distinctive about AI, from a developmental standpoint, is not that it is wrong, or distorting, or even necessarily harmful. It is that it is extraordinarily responsive without having genuine subjectivity. It simulates the experience of being understood without the reality of another mind behind it.

For an adult, this distinction is available, if sometimes quite easy to forget. For a child, whose capacity to distinguish between a mind that truly has an inner life and one that simulates having one is still forming, the distinction might not yet be fully available at all.

Researchers have begun to observe how children try to think this problem through in real time. In one University of Edinburgh study of families with smart speakers, primary-school children were observed probing whether the device in the kitchen had a mind of its own, settling, provisionally, on conclusions like the one that gave the study its title: “Alexa doesn’t have that many feelings.” That, however, was a machine that talks in highly scripted phrases, in contrast to the systems children now converse with which are fluent and personal, attuned to the shape of a particular child’s interactions. Working out what this means has become correspondingly harder.

This creates something new in the developmental landscape. When a child turns to AI in a moment of confusion or distress and receives a fluent, patient, seemingly attuned response, the attachment system is engaged. The child feels, in some functional sense, heard. And that feeling, whatever its source, is real in its effects.

I have not seen my son turn to AI for emotional support, and I am glad for that, because here the questions are harder still. These systems shape themselves to their user. They adopt your vocabulary, match your register, remember what concerns you, and are available at three in the morning without complaint. Nothing in the design pushes back, for the most part, or asks anything of you. It would be difficult to engineer an arrangement better suited to engaging the attachment system of a child who is lonely or lacking in the structure needed for sound development.

The early numbers suggest that many children have already found their way there. In a national survey released by Common Sense Media in 2025, nearly three in four American teenagers had used an AI companion, and about a third had chosen to discuss something serious with an AI instead of with a person. A similar share described their AI conversations as at least as satisfying as conversations with friends.

In the United Kingdom, the nonprofit Internet Matters found that roughly one in eight children who use chatbots do so because they have no one else to talk to. The American Psychological Association was concerned enough to issue a health advisory in 2025, noting that adolescents are less likely than adults to question the accuracy or the intent of what a machine tells them, and warning that simulated companionship may begin to displace the relationships in which development actually happens.

For a child without reliable support, the relief such a system offers may be real, and so I am reluctant to dismiss it. But relief is not the substance of attachment. A full attachment relationship requires another person who can be truly changed by you, and with you—someone who is affected, who has limits, who gets things wrong and can be held to account. An AI can extend the experience of being emotionally met indefinitely. What it cannot do is be moved.

For the child who is largely or completely unattended to, this may still be better than nothing at all. Soothed in the early hours of the morning, a child whose sadness before bed is registered somewhere may be kept afloat by something real, and I would not wish to take it from her. But we should be honest about such an arrangement, which I expect will become increasingly common. It is a patch and not a structural solution. When a machine becomes the most reliable attention in a child’s life, the pertinent question is not about the machine but about the deprivation that made it necessary. And, crucially, a culture that responds to unattended children with software is treating the symptom while leaving the underlying structure intact.

There is also a quieter concern about authority and accountability.

Children have always learned to navigate between competing sources of knowledge, including parents, teachers, peers, and books, each with their own perspective, their own limits, and their own accountability. This navigation is itself developmental. The child who discovers that a parent doesn’t know something, or that a teacher can be wrong, or that a friend sees things differently, is developing a more differentiated relationship to knowledge.

AI, in the form a child is most likely to reach on their own, enters this landscape as something that appears to know a great deal, that expresses itself with fluency and confidence, and that has no stable point of view and no accountability for what it says. When a child asks why a friendship went wrong, or whether they were right to feel hurt, the answer they receive may shape the story they tell themselves about what happened. And unlike a parent or teacher, the system may not challenge the child’s assumptions so much as elaborate them. None of this is inherent to the technology under discussion. A system can be built to hold a position and to answer for it, but the ones children use are mostly not built that way.

Children appear to be taking up the offer. The same Internet Matters report found that nearly two-thirds of British children between nine and 17 had used an AI chatbot; among those who had, roughly a quarter had asked one for advice—on schoolwork, but also on more personal matters—and four in 10 expressed no reservations about following the advice they received.

None of this means that AI in children’s lives is a mistake. The benefits my son has experienced are genuine—wider curiosity, more sophisticated questions, greater confidence in exploring unfamiliar terrain. These are real, and they matter.

Tom Wooldridge is the author of the new book End the Food Fight: Replacing Control with Connection to Help Your Child Heal from an Eating Disorder (Guilford Press, 2026, 140 pages).

Some of what he brings home delights me. He asked recently whether you could power a sailboat by blowing into the sail through a straw. The answer he received was better than the one I would have managed: blowing at your own sail from on board mostly cancels itself out, since you push the air forward and the air pushes you back. Mostly, but not entirely—if the sail catches the stream and turns it around, a little net thrust survives, and if you skip the sail altogether and aim the straw backward, you have built yourself a very small jet engine. He spent the rest of the day on propulsion: rockets, squid, the reason a fan bolted to a sailboat is an old physics puzzle with a surprising answer.

But development is not only about access to knowledge. It is also about the conditions under which a self forms—conditions that include, crucially, the experience of not knowing, of waiting, of struggling toward understanding without guarantee of arrival. These are not inefficiencies to be optimized away. They are the medium through which a mind learns what it is capable of. A child who has never had to sit with a question—who has always had access to a patient, fluent, endlessly available presence to resolve it—may be acquiring knowledge without acquiring the tolerance for uncertainty that makes knowledge genuinely usable.

This is the thing I find myself returning to when I watch my son. He is, in many ways, more intellectually alive than he might otherwise be. He explores further and asks better questions. At the same time, when uncertainty arises, he reaches outward more quickly than he used to—to a presence that is always available, always patient, always responsive. What I cannot yet know is what he is quietly learning to expect: from knowledge, from relationship, and from himself.

We are not conducting a study. We are raising children. And the conditions of childhood have changed again, in ways whose full significance has not yet come into view.

Tom Wooldridge, Psy.D., is a psychoanalyst, founding dean of the School of Psychology at Golden Gate University, and director of the Human Systems Lab, which explores the roles of AI and other emergent technologies in human systems.

#10610. I’d rather use Android XR glasses over Meta Ray-Bans for the app alone

In trying out Google’s Intelligent Eyewear Android XR glasses for the first time, one of my main takeaways is that it’s the app, not necessarily the glasses themselves that would push me into using them over the mainstay Meta Ray-Bans.

One of the inherent advantages that Android XR glasses were always going to have over third-party options is native integration. It’s something we see frequently, and perhaps most well-known on smartwatches.

The “Glasses” app on Android/Pixel, iOS, and Samsung Galaxy devices is dead simple. While we can’t show you the full app just yet as the one we were able to try was not completely final, it’s a very simple experience.

The app opens to an overview of your glasses to show their connection and battery status, with settings below for Gemini, apps, the camera, and more. You’ll also be able to see things such as what the various lights on the glasses mean, tutorials, and more. There’s no bloat and no messing around – it’s a utility to just make adjustments or control the glasses as needed.

You’ll probably be using this app mostly to view the photos and videos captured on Android XR glasses. Google has this set up to auto-import photos in nearly real-time. It only takes about 4 seconds to import and the process starts immediately for photos. Videos, on the other hand, take longer and may need to be manually imported (for the sake of battery in part, Google mentioned).

Across the three main platforms, there are some differences. The iPhone app doesn’t import photos quite as fast, and Samsung has its own app to fit in with One UI’s design language for Galaxy users, but the core premise is the same across all three.

While we’ll have to see how this works in real life, I was blown away by how simple it all was, though that might be because the bar is below the floor.

Meta’s Ray-Bans (and newer Meta Glasses) dominate the smart glasses conversation, and I think that the app is one of the biggest problems. It’s certainly one of the biggest reasons I’ve barely touched my Wayfarers in recent months. That’s because, in early 2025, Meta replaced its dedicated “View” app for its glasses with a Meta AI app, which is just invasive and distasteful in my book. Meta is no stranger to shoving AI down the throat of its existing users. Another well-known move was converting Facebook and Instagram search into AI search boxes with creepy levels of context and invasive actions – it makes Google’s AI Overviews look tame by comparison. But I’d argue the most aggressive was this move with its glasses. Something as simple as tweaking a setting or importing a photo now forces you to dodge an AI chatbot.

Truly, Meta’s app is the worst, and I applaud Google for building a very simple utility for Android XR glasses instead.

#10609. This Quest 3 golf club actually improved my scores, but it comes at a cost

The AMVR GA2 is a fresh take on a Quest 3 golf club accessory, and I was pleasantly surprised with what it did for my scores.

In the past decade of covering VR devices and accessories, I’ve used a lot of golf clubs. Short clubs. Long clubs. Adjustable length clubs. Clubs with realistic weight. Even a club with a magnet inside that makes a convincing “thwack!” sound every time you swing it. But the AMVR GA2’s inclusion of a proper club head at the bottom does something fascinating for its physics, and I immediately noticed the difference at the end of the first course I played with this Quest 3 golf club.

Most VR golf club accessories, including the first-gen AMVR GA1, stick the controller at the bottom of the shaft where the head usually goes. This makes sense since you don’t hit a real ball with a VR golf club accessory; it’s all virtual. But, ironically, putting a proper golf club head on the end (and the controller in the middle of the shaft) apparently does make a difference. This one works with both the Meta Quest 3 and Quest 3S, but not the older Meta Quest 2 or Quest Pro.

I’m not going to delve into the physics of why this works, but it’s likely that shifting the fulcrum point improves it. I didn’t expect this at all. When AMVR told me about their new club, I accepted because the company typically makes top-notch accessories. I really didn’t need another club in my extensive Quest 3 golf club collection. But I’m glad I did, because I’ve done much better than usual in my weekly Walkabout Mini Golf rounds with friends since I got it.

The mechanism for securing the controller onto the club is identical to previous AMVR controllers; only the location of the mount is different. Slide the controller (with wrist strap attached) into the holster. Click the rubber stopper in so the controller doesn’t move, then loop the wrist strap around the controller as a backup in case you didn’t secure the rubber stopper properly. It’s quite simple.

All major VR golf games allow custom spacing for Quest 3 golf clubs, so it doesn’t take more than 30 seconds to get comfortable with the club in your game of choice.

The only problem I’ve found with this club is its price. Most Quest 3 golf club accessories cost between $30 and $50, depending on the capabilities. This one is normally double that, but AMVR was kind enough to pass along a 25% off coupon for 9to5Google readers, making it more palatable if you’re looking to improve your VR golf scores. Just apply the coupon code 1FD4JYT5 at checkout on Amazon for an extra 25% off the AMVR GA2 golf club.

If you’re serious about your scores in Golf+, Walkabout Mini Golf, or another favorite of yours, this might just be the ultimate enthusiast Quest 3 golf club accessory.

#10603. Proton Pass is up to 60% off for Black Friday—and it does way more than just store your passwords

There’s a familiar interruption that comes with shopping online: you’ve found what you want, you’re ready to check out, and the store asks you to sign in. Was this the account with your usual password? Did you change it last time? Is it easier to reset it again?

Proton Pass is designed to take that friction out of your day. It creates strong, unique passwords, remembers them for you, and fills them in when you log in. But it also handles something a password alone can’t: keeping your real email address out of another retailer’s database.

For Black Friday, Proton Pass Plus and Pass Family are both 60% off their regular monthly pricing with a one-year subscription, while Proton Unlimited is 50% off. Pass Plus starts at $1.99 per month, billed annually, making this a good opportunity to get your passwords — and a few other important parts of your digital life — organized.

Take a look at Proton’s Black Friday offers, starting at $1.99 per month

The problem with strong passwords isn’t understanding why you need them; it’s keeping track of a different one for every account.

Proton Pass does that work for you. When you sign up for a website, its password generator can create a strong password that’s completely unique. Save it in your vault, and Proton Pass can fill in your credentials when you return.

That’s useful whether you’re creating an account for a single purchase or signing into a service you use every day.

Unique passwords also help limit the fallout from a data breach. If a retailer exposes your login credentials, using a different password elsewhere means those same credentials won’t unlock your other accounts.

With unlimited logins and encrypted vaults that sync across devices, Proton Pass lets you keep those details together as you move between your phone and computer.

Proton Pass is available for practically every platform, from Chrome and Firefox on the web to native macOS, Windows, and Linux apps to, of course, iOS and Android. This makes it trivially easy to access your passwords, passkeys, and API credentials wherever you find yourself.

Black Friday shopping often leaves something behind long after the packages arrive: a steady stream of promotional emails from stores you barely remember visiting.

Proton Pass includes unlimited hide-my-email aliases, giving you an alternative to handing out your real address every time you create an account.

An alias is an extra email address that forwards messages to your normal inbox. You can give it to a shopping website and still receive your order confirmations and delivery updates, without sharing the underlying address you use for everything else.

If that alias starts receiving spam, you can switch it off. Your real email address stays the same, and you don’t have to update all your other accounts.

It’s a practical way to put some distance between your primary inbox and the websites you’re trying for the first time. A separate alias for each service also gives you more control: you can stop messages arriving through one address without disrupting the others.

Passwords are only part of what’s worth keeping in a secure, accessible place.

Proton Pass also stores notes, credit card details, and other confidential information in end-to-end encrypted vaults. That makes it useful for the small, everyday moments when you need something sensitive and don’t want to go looking for it.

When you’re booking a flight, saved passport details let you look up the information you need without digging through a drawer halfway through the reservation.

When you’re shopping online, saved card details mean you don’t have to leave the checkout page to find your wallet.

When you’re traveling, keeping a digital copy of an important document within reach gives you a convenient reference while you’re away from home. It doesn’t replace an original document where one is required, but it can save you from searching through scattered files and messages.

Pass Plus also includes a built-in two-factor authenticator, Dark Web Monitoring, and secure link and vault sharing. Those features give you more ways to manage access to your accounts and share sensitive information when you need to.

Pass Plus is the straightforward choice for one person who wants password generation, autofill, unlimited aliases, and secure storage.

Pass Family includes six Pass Plus accounts and a family admin panel. If several people in your household need a password manager, it’s worth considering rather than setting everyone up with separate subscriptions.

Proton Unlimited bundles Pass with Proton VPN, Proton Mail, Proton Drive, and more, including 500 GB of cloud storage. It’s the option to consider if you also want a VPN, encrypted email, and cloud storage alongside your password manager.

A password manager isn’t the most conspicuous Black Friday purchase, but it’s one you can put to work immediately: the next account you create, the next password you forget, or the next time a checkout asks for details you don’t have handy.

Proton’s offers are available to new users and include a 30-day money-back guarantee.

#10602. When Should You Remove NAT from an On-Prem Kubernetes Network?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

In an on-premises Kubernetes environment, removing NAT can make the network simpler to inspect, reduce translation overhead, and let the data center route directly to workloads. But direct routing is not automatically the right choice.

The decision depends on what the organization controls and what it needs from the network. A team that owns the physical fabric, has enough address space, and is comfortable operating BGP may benefit from making pod networks directly routable. A smaller environment, a team without control of the underlay, or a cluster that benefits from network abstraction may be better served by NAT or an overlay.

Kubernetes is also changing the ground under these decisions: kube-proxy is set to drop IPVS support starting with version 1.43, with nftables as the replacement. For on-prem teams already reviewing their service data path, it is a good moment to ask whether NAT still belongs in it.

So the useful question is not how to eliminate NAT, but when the operational benefits of doing so justify the additional routing responsibility.

NAT solves a real problem. It allows an internal network to use its own address space without requiring the surrounding infrastructure to know how to reach every endpoint inside it. This is especially useful when you don’t control the underlying network.

However, an on-premises data center is different. When you control both the Kubernetes cluster and the surrounding physical network, you can make Kubernetes networks directly routable.

In our case, removing NAT had practical and architectural benefits. It improved network performance, reduced CPU load on the nodes, and removed the risk of conntrack tables filling up on Kubernetes hosts. A directly routed L3 model also works well for traffic balancing when a node has two uplinks.

This does not mean eliminating NAT from the data center entirely. NAT can remain at the boundary of the on-premises network, for example on hardware firewalls. Inside the data center, however, Kubernetes networks can be directly routable, allowing the physical network to reach workloads using the same routing mechanisms it already uses elsewhere.

In our implementation, BGP connects the Kubernetes network to the data center fabric: BIRD runs on the nodes and advertises pod CIDRs and selected service addresses, while kube-router manages Kubernetes networking. We described this implementation in more detail in our earlier article on building a routable on-prem

Kubernetes cluster with BGP and ECMP. The important point for this decision is not the particular stack, but the requirement it creates: the surrounding network must be able to accept and route Kubernetes prefixes safely. If the organization cannot or does not want to make the cluster part of its routing domain, keeping NAT or an overlay is usually the simpler choice.

Removing NAT alone does not make a network resilient. If every node still depends on one switch, one NIC, or one uplink, direct routing simply exposes the same physical single point of failure more clearly.

The resilience benefit appears when the physical topology supports multiple independent paths. In our setup, nodes connect to independent data center switches and ECMP can use equivalent routes across those paths. If one path disappears, traffic can continue over the other.

That distinction matters when deciding whether direct routing is worth the effort. Organizations with redundant uplinks and switches can integrate Kubernetes into an already resilient routed fabric. If the underlay itself has no path redundancy, removing NAT should not be presented as a high-availability improvement: the physical failure domain still dominates the design.

Our stack is one example of how direct routing can be implemented, not a default recommendation for clusters that remove NAT. We chose kube-router because it supports a minimal installation as a binary and systemd service and covers the networking functions we need for this L3 design. BIRD handles route advertisements to the surrounding network.

Other environments may reasonably choose Calico for conventional L3 routing or Cilium when an eBPF-based dataplane and its additional capabilities are useful. The choice of CNI and routing daemon is secondary to the architectural decision: first decide whether exposing Kubernetes prefixes to the physical network makes operational sense, then choose the implementation that fits the team’s requirements.

A directly routed Kubernetes network is not automatically better than an overlay or NAT-based architecture. Each model solves a different operational problem.

NAT is a reasonable choice when the cluster needs to remain isolated from the surrounding address plan, when pod addresses cannot be routed through the data center, or when the infrastructure team does not control the upstream network. It also keeps Kubernetes-specific prefixes out of the physical routing domain, which can be valuable for smaller environments where direct visibility into every pod is not important.

An overlay is useful when portability and abstraction matter more than exposing the physical packet path. It lets Kubernetes networking remain largely independent of the underlay and can simplify deployment across heterogeneous infrastructure. The tradeoff is another layer to inspect during troubleshooting and, depending on the implementation, encapsulation overhead.

Direct routing makes the most sense when the organization owns the physical network, has address space available for pod CIDRs, and has operators comfortable with BGP and route filtering. It becomes more attractive when preserving source addresses, using standard network troubleshooting tools, predictable performance, or integrating the cluster with redundant data center paths are important. At a larger scale, those operational benefits can justify the extra coordination between Kubernetes and network teams.

The broader lesson is not that every Kubernetes cluster needs BGP or that NAT should disappear everywhere. It is that Kubernetes has matured enough that we should stop automatically treating its network as a black box.

As more production and AI workloads move onto Kubernetes, network architecture becomes part of application reliability. In an on-premises data center, BGP and ECMP give us the option to use the resilience already available in the physical network rather than building another isolated networking layer on top of it.

Sometimes the best way to make Kubernetes networking more robust is not to add another abstraction, but to remove one. But only when the surrounding infrastructure and the team operating it are ready to take responsibility for the routing that abstraction previously hid.

If most of those answers are yes, direct routing is worth serious consideration. If not, NAT or an overlay may be the more resilient operational choice—not because it is technically more advanced, but because it better matches the environment.

#10590. 7 Best Accounts Receivable (AR) Software Platforms for Automating Invoice-to-Cash

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The best accounts receivable software for your company is the layer that closes the stage where your cash leaks. If buyers settle from an emailed link and outreach starts late, a reminders tool covers most of it. If customers prefer to pay online by card or ACH, add a payments network with a customer portal. If clients require invoice uploads to Coupa or Ariba and clear multiple invoices with one blank-memo wire, you need an AR automation layer on top of the ERP. Every platform listed here serves B2B businesses invoicing other companies, excluding consumer payment apps and collection agencies.

Our source material is each vendor's public product, integration, security and pricing pages, plus G2 reviews, as they stood in November 2026.

About three in five US suppliers let business clients pay within 30 days, per Atradius's 2026 US payment practices survey (September 2026). Most overdue invoices settle within a month past due, so funds arrive roughly two months post-billing. The same survey notes US enterprises often lose 1% to 2% of B2B invoices to bad debt. Software that shortens this window performs four main functions:

What invoice-to-cash software does not handle is core billing operations. Billing schedules, revenue recognition, and GL accounting remain within the ERP. Contract-first platforms initiate workflows from signed agreements and map customer billing prior to collection efforts; invoice-first applications pick up after ERP invoice generation.

Most finance stacks have four layers: the ERP that creates the invoice and holds the books, a payments network, a reminders tool, and an AR automation layer working the steps in between.

The ERP covers the last row, and a reminders tool covers timing. The rows in between depend on reading what a customer or a portal sent, which is AR automation work. That share keeps growing. Paper checks fell to 26% of B2B payments in 2025, per the AFP 2025 Digital Payments Survey as reported by Nacha, and FedNow settled about 2.5 times as many payments in Q3 2026 as a year earlier, per Federal Reserve FedNow statistics. Heading into 2027, more money arrives over electronic rails while its remittance detail lands somewhere else.

Layer: AI-native AR automation, for Series A to Fortune 500 finance teams

Published pricing: Pricing scales with business volume and isn't a percentage of collections

Leak it closes: Unapplied cash first, then stalled replies and portal rejections.

Layer: AR automation with built-in payments, sold as Invoiced by Flywire

Leak it closes: Cash application, where CashMatch AI handles wires that pay several invoices at once and payments that arrive without remittance.

Leak it closes: Invoice delivery into buyer portals, through 260+ AP portal integrations run from one interface.

Layer: AR automation layer with AI agents, on the ERP

Leak it closes: Customer replies that carry a promise-to-pay date or a dispute.

Layer: Payments network with customer portal

Leak it closes: Payment acceptance, through a customer portal for paying and disputing invoices online.

Layer: Reminders tool with AI reply drafts, on Xero and QuickBooks

Published pricing: Essentials $69 a month; Pro $99 to $799 a month; Custom from $999 a month

Leak it closes: Replies that sit unanswered at small and midsize companies.

Published pricing: US $259, $779 or $1,169 a month by annual revenue band; annual billing saves 10%

Leak it closes: Follow-up timing, with reminders sent from your own email address on a schedule that escalates.

On Xero or QuickBooks with customers paying from the emailed link, the leak tends to be timing, and a reminders tool covers it. Once W-9 requests and payment questions fill your team's week, the replies need a different layer.

Portal rejections and lump-sum wires put the leak in the table's middle rows, where an AR automation layer on the ERP fits. In a demo, we'd ask each vendor to show how fast its software flagged a rejected portal invoice, then to apply a multi-invoice wire with a blank memo live.

If your team still keys card numbers or chases ACH details, start with the payments layer. A payments network with a customer portal gives buyers stored methods and autopay. Unless the vendor says otherwise, it won't read replies or file into buyer portals, so plan an AR layer for those stages.

It's like closing the corporate barn door after the horse has bolted.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Less than a week after Jensen Huang resigned from the company, Nvidia's board has created its own National Security and Export Control Oversight Committee. According to its announcement, the new standing committee will provide high-level oversight of Nvidia’s “national security policies, export control compliance, and global trade risk management,” with its National Security Team and Global Trade Compliance Team reporting directly to the group and to interim CEO Colette Kress.

“Nvidia recognizes that advanced silicon technologies and the emergence of artificial and superintelligence present unprecedented challenges for national security,” Nvidia chairperson Harvey Jones said. “As the world’s leading advanced computing company, Nvidia has both a responsibility and an opportunity to lead the industry in addressing these challenges. We are committed to working constructively with government and industry partners to help establish and advance a robust framework that uses every tool at our disposal to safeguard national security while supporting continued technological innovation.”

The rumors of Jensen Huang’s affair with Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese spy, have thrown the company into turmoil, especially after his admission and subsequent apology. People have begun digging into details about Ms. Huang, revealing that she was at the center of a tangled web of companies and was reportedly instrumental in importing around $7.5 billion of banned Nvidia chips into China. A deeper investigation also revealed that her husband, Kang Wei, was behind her rise to prominence and seemingly accepted her relationship with the billionaire. It remains unclear if the couple made these shrewd business moves purely for profit or if they were under the control of a hidden third party, but it has certainly exposed the company to additional scrutiny.

The chairpersons of the House committees on China, Foreign Affairs, and Intelligence have since invited Jensen to a closed-door briefing to discuss Nvidia’s “security protocols against foreign adversary espionage,” while President Donald Trump mentioned that the FBI is looking into Jensen Huang’s relationship with Ms. Huang. The U.S. first applied export controls to Nvidia’s most advanced chips, the A100 and H100, in 2022, forcing the company to create the China-market A800 and H800 GPUs that complied with Washington’s performance limitations.

However, the White House lowered the bar once more in 2023, which resulted in the even more watered-down H20, which, nonetheless, remained in high demand. Even though the Trump administration did not implement Biden’s AI diffusion rule, which threatened to outright ban several countries from buying Nvidia AI chips, the president eventually banned the export of H20 chips to China from April to June 2025.

Even before Trump’s total Nvidia AI GPU ban, there have already been multiple reports of smuggling, with one semiconductor analysis firm tracking more than 1.5 gigawatts of banned AI chips exported to China and estimating that up to 1.5 million Nvidia GPUs were smuggled in recent years. Despite that, the former Nvidia chief has always denied the allegations, saying that “There’s no evidence of any AI chip diversion.” Whether the allegations are true or not, it seems that the company is taking the situation seriously and taking steps to plug any leaks. As for Jensen Huang, he may be out of the company, but he still has to face Congress and all ongoing investigations.
