#10580. Samsung, SK hynix, and Micron form unlikely alliance to standardize robot charging as chipmakers push toward unmanned fabs

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Semiconductor manufacturers are increasing automation to boost the productivity of existing fabs

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Samsung Electronics, SK hynix, and Micron are reportedly working together to develop common charging standards for mobile robots used in semiconductor fabrication plants, according to a new report. Representatives from the three companies have reportedly formed an early-stage consultative body to lead standardization efforts, alongside major industry stakeholders such as the Semiconductor Equipment and Materials International (SEMI) industry association.

According to a report by Seoul Economic Daily, the initiative aims to establish common technical specifications that would allow robots from different manufacturers to share charging equipment, reducing the cost and complexity of deploying large robotic fleets across semiconductor manufacturing facilities. The broader goal is to accelerate factory automation, particularly in older fabs, while laying the groundwork for fully autonomous semiconductor manufacturing.

So why are three of the world's biggest memory manufacturers, who are also competitors, coming together to establish standards for the robotics industry? What exactly does the collaboration involve? And what are the implications of something as seemingly minor as robot charging for the broader semiconductor and robotics industries?

Soaring demand for semiconductor memory chips used in AI infrastructure has driven manufacturers to automate as many production processes as possible to optimize manufacturing speed and capacity in existing facilities to increase output, while they build additional fabs. Modern semiconductor fabs already have extensive automation systems. However, even these facilities still require human workers for certain operations, such as moving materials between floors and unpacking products, that existing automation systems cannot perform. The issue is even more pronounced in older fabs, which generally have fewer automated material-handling systems and still rely on workers to move wafer containers using manual carts.

One potential solution is to hand these remaining manual operations over to mobile robots, allowing manufacturers to automate tasks without undertaking extensive renovations or installing complex fixed transport systems. These could include autonomous mobile robots (AMRs) that transport materials around the factory, mobile manipulators that combine robotic arms with moving platforms, and humanoid robots capable of performing more complicated handling tasks.

The problem, however, is that these robots come in different builds and configurations, often with their own batteries and accompanying charging infrastructure. A semiconductor fab deploying multiple robot models from different manufacturers could consequently end up requiring separate charging stations for each model. In addition to increasing equipment and maintenance costs, these installations consume valuable factory floor space.

The proposed initiative seeks to address this problem by establishing common charging specifications that different robot manufacturers can adopt. Standardized charging infrastructure would allow multiple types of robots to use compatible charging stations, reducing the need for separate installations and making it easier for semiconductor manufacturers to expand their robotic fleets without adding operational inefficiencies.

The alliance is considering standards covering battery specifications for different mobile robots, charging-station equipment and communication protocols, charging connectors, and battery management systems (BMS). Together, these specifications would establish common requirements for the physical and electronic interfaces involved in charging robots inside semiconductor fabs.

The initial objective is to achieve charging compatibility across different robot models. In addition to letting a fab deploy robots from several manufacturers without having to install a separate charging system for every model, this would also give manufacturers greater flexibility when purchasing new robots, since their choice of supplier would no longer be as heavily influenced by the charging infrastructure already installed in the facility.

A subsequent step would involve integrating robot charging data with the fab's manufacturing execution system (MES), the software that coordinates and monitors manufacturing operations. With access to information such as battery charge levels, robot availability, and production schedules, the system could coordinate when individual robots operate and when they return for charging. For example, robots could be scheduled to recharge during periods of lower demand or while other robots handle critical material transfers, minimizing interruptions to production. This integration would become increasingly important as manufacturers deploy larger fleets of robots across the hundreds of individual processes involved in semiconductor fabrication.

Interestingly, the three chipmakers leading the standardization efforts are primarily customers of industrial robotics manufacturers, not manufacturers of the robots themselves, except for Samsung, which has an established robotics business. The objective is to develop specifications that robotics manufacturers and charging-equipment suppliers can incorporate into their products.

On the other hand, the three companies are not independently creating specifications and dictating them to suppliers. The initiative would likely follow an industry standardization process in which robotics manufacturers, equipment suppliers, and other stakeholders contribute to developing and validating the technical requirements. For now, the project remains in its early stages, with participants still determining which components and interfaces should be standardized and consulting robotics and smart-factory specialists.

According to DigiTimes, semiconductor industry participants first explored establishing common robotics standards roughly a decade ago, but discussions made little progress until recently. The renewed momentum is largely attributed to the ongoing semiconductor boom, which has coincided with rapid advancements in industrial robotics.

Unprecedented demand for AI-related memory products, along with record financial performance, has given semiconductor manufacturers both the incentive and the financial resources to invest more aggressively in factory automation. While manufacturers are expanding production capacity to meet demand, new fabs take years to construct and bring online. As a result, they are simultaneously looking to squeeze out as much capacity as possible from existing facilities by increasing automation and improving productivity.

As for why Samsung, SK hynix, and Micron are collaborating despite their intense rivalry, the answer likely comes down to shared commercial interests. Incompatible robot charging infrastructure is an industry-wide problem, and all three companies stand to benefit from a common solution. The AI boom has already encouraged numerous partnerships across the semiconductor supply chain, including between companies that are considered fierce rivals, as manufacturers look for ways to overcome common production and infrastructure constraints.

The involvement of these particular companies also gives the initiative considerable weight. Together, Samsung, SK hynix, and Micron control almost the entire global DRAM market. They also operate extensive semiconductor manufacturing networks and represent a substantial potential market for fab-specific robotic equipment. A common set of specifications backed by all three would therefore provide robotics manufacturers with a strong commercial incentive to develop compatible products, potentially shortening development cycles and accelerating adoption.

Although the current collaboration focuses on a relatively small aspect of factory automation, it is a significant step for broader standardization across the semiconductor manufacturing and industrial robotics industries. Common charging infrastructure would make it easier to deploy and manage mixed robotic fleets, while future integration with production management systems could bring fabs closer to operating with minimal human intervention. This longer-term ambition of completely autonomous facilities is already taking shape. Both SK hynix and Samsung have outlined plans to develop an autonomous semiconductor fab by 2030.

#10575. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves to You

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

The Fugaku supercomputer, operated by the national research institute Riken, has been breached, with users' data, including names, emails, and more, stolen.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Fugaku, Japan's most powerful supercomputer and the ninth-fastest on the planet, has suffered a suspected data breach. The news was announced by its operator, Riken, which is the country's national research institute, with the outfit also confirming that personal information relating to more than 4,000 users of Fugaku was potentially taken during the breach. Further, the information of 375 users of Riken's Rikyu AI supercomputer was also possibly taken. The personal information includes names, email addresses, research files, and even the authentication information that was used to access the supercomputers and related services. The Japan Times reports that the operators of Fugaku first became aware of suspicious communications on November 12, with those suspicions then triggering an investigation.

By November 14, the investigation had confirmed that unauthorized access to Fugaku had taken place, with access also blocked at that time. The investigation identified that hackers had used the supercomputer's externally accessible operational status dashboard as the springboard for the hack between November 10 and November 13. Now taken offline while security concerns are addressed, the dashboard allowed the hackers to gain access to the system's internal network, but beyond that, details are scarce. Riken does not yet know — or at least, it isn't saying — the full story of how the hackers gained access to Fugaku as a whole. But it has confirmed that some servers had unauthorized software installed.

Following the discovery of the breach, Riken reported the incident to Japan's Ministry of Education, Culture, Sports, Science and Technology (MEXT). Chief Cabinet Secretary Minoru Kihara has since confirmed that MEXT and Riken were working together to investigate what happened and ascertain the full scope of the breach itself. “Fugaku plays a vital role in our country’s research and development infrastructure, so it is important that they prioritize security while limiting the impact on research activities,” Kihara is reported to have said.

Located at Riken’s Center for Computational Science in Kobe, Fugaku officially came online in March of 2021 after being developed in collaboration with Fujitsu for a cost of around ¥130 billion (approximately $817 million in 2026). It became the world's fastest supercomputer before being overtaken by Frontier, an exascale supercomputer housed at the Department of Energy's Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility (OLCF) in Tennessee.

The fastest supercomputer currently in operation is China's LineShine. It recently retained its crown as the fastest on the planet amid a move away from traditional supercomputers to AI data centers.

#10564. What is Model Routing in AI?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

"Do you know the closest ATM?" and "I was charged twice, the refund never arrived, and now my card is blocked" are both customer messages. Most AI apps send both to the same large model and pay large-model prices for each one.

Model routing fixes that. A router looks at each request and sends it to the cheapest model that can handle it, and keeps the expensive model for the requests that need it.

In this guide we'll cover what model routing is, how routers decide, and what it saves. Then we'll build a small router in about 60 lines of JavaScript and run it over 200 customer messages. At one setting it matched a large model's accuracy for about a third of the cost. At another it came within a point and a half for 89% less.

Model routing in AI is the practice of sending each request to the model best suited to it, instead of sending every request to one model. A router sits between your app and a pool of models, reads each request, and picks a model based on what you care about: cost, speed, or quality.

You'll also see it called AI model routing, LLM routing, or LLM model routing. They all mean the same thing. The pool might be a small and a large model from one provider, or models from several providers behind one API.

The idea rests on one observation: most requests are easy. A small model answers them as well as a large one, in a fraction of the time and for a fraction of the price. The router's whole job is finding the hard ones.

The diagram below shows a typical AI model routing architecture.

Model routing works in three steps: the router reads the request, decides which model should answer it, and forwards the request to that model. The middle step is where routers differ. Routing tools decide which model to use in one of four ways.

Rule-based routing uses rules you write by hand. Requests from free-plan users go to a small model. Anything tagged as code goes to a coding model. Prompts over a certain length go to a model with a bigger context window. It's simple, predictable, and free to run, but the rules only know what you thought of in advance.

Intelligent model routing uses a model to make the decision. A small, fast classifier reads each request and predicts which model in the pool will answer it well. You'll also see it called smart or dynamic model routing.

Two more terms come up here. Task-aware routing picks a model by the kind of job, like summarising, coding, or answering a support question. Context-aware routing also looks at what surrounds the request, like the conversation so far, the user, or the tools available.

The big platforms work this way. Microsoft describes its Foundry model router as "a trained language model" that picks a model for each prompt in real time. Google says Gemini Notebook uses its new Decisions API as a real-time router, serving everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency. I've tested the Google Decisions API against Jev on the same messages we'll use below.

Cascade routing tries the cheapest model first and only moves up when the answer isn't good enough. The usual test is confidence: if the cheap model is sure, keep its answer. If it isn't, send the request to a stronger model. It's also called confidence-based routing, and it's what we'll build below.

A cascade needs no separate classifier, because the first model's own confidence does the routing. The trade-off is that escalated requests pay for two models and wait for both.

Fallback routing is about staying up, not saving money. If a model times out, hits a rate limit, or its provider has an outage, the router sends the request to the next model on a list. Most production routers combine fallbacks with one of the three methods above.

The main benefits of model routing in AI systems are lower cost, faster responses, and fewer outages, without giving up quality on the hard requests.

We'll build a cascade router for a job every support tool has: sorting customer messages. Each message gets one of ten labels, like atm support or request refund.

The test set is 200 messages from Banking77, a public set of banking support messages: ten labels, 20 messages each. They're the same 200 messages from my zero shot classification test, which is where the single-model numbers below come from.

Put both keys in a .env file and run the examples with node --env-file=.env. It's the same setup as in my guide to getting a YouTube API key.

Create router.mjs. The first function sends the message to Jev with one multiple-choice question, and returns the label Jev picked along with its confidence:

Confidence runs from 0 to 1. When nearly all the probability sits on one label, it's close to 1. When Jev is torn between two labels, it drops.

The second function asks Claude Opus 5.5 the same question. The output_config block limits the answer to one of our labels, so there's no free text to parse. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema explains how that works.

The router itself is about a dozen lines:

If Jev is at least 80% confident, its answer goes straight back. If not, Claude answers instead. And if Claude fails, the router falls back to Jev's answer, because a shaky answer beats an error page.

Try it on an easy message and a harder one:

Jev was sure about the lost card. On the ATM question it leaned towards card acceptance with a confidence of 0.64, so the router asked Claude, which got it right.

Because cheap, strong, and threshold are options, the same function routes between any two models.

Model routing reduces AI costs by keeping easy requests on a cheap model and paying for the expensive one only when it's needed. Here's what that looked like on our 200 messages, starting with each model on its own:

Opus gets six more messages right out of 200, and costs more than 120 times as much.

Now the router, at four confidence thresholds:

Speed improved too. At the 0.8 threshold, the messages Jev kept came back in a median of 227 ms. The ones that escalated took about 2.1 seconds, because they waited for both models.

A note on method: I ran every message through both models once and saved the answers, then replayed the router over them at each threshold. That's how you compare thresholds without paying for the same test four times.

Then I ran the router live to check. At 0.95 it scored the same 95.0% and sent 61 messages to Opus. At 0.8 it scored 93.5% and sent 20, a point under the replay. Two answers moved: one of Jev's confidence scores landed at 0.82 instead of 0.78, just over the line, and Opus changed its answer on one message. Scores near the threshold shift a little between runs, so leave yourself a margin.

The choice of cheap model matters less than you'd think. With Google's Gemini Decision 1 in first place instead of Jev, a 0.7 threshold reached the same 95% for US$0.28 per 1,000 messages.

Model routing can cost a little quality, and the threshold decides how much. In our test the gap to Opus ranged from nothing at 0.95 to two points at 0.7.

The loss comes from one place: answers the cheap model got wrong while feeling sure. At the 0.8 threshold, Jev kept five wrong answers because its confidence was above the line. Opus got four of those five wrong as well, so only one was a real loss: "Do all ATMs take this card?", which Jev filed under card acceptance with a confidence of 0.91.

Escalating isn't risk-free either. On one message, Jev had the right label with a confidence of 0.44, and Opus overruled it with the wrong one.

To optimize costs using AI model routing, measure first, route second, and keep measuring. These six steps cover LLM model routing cost optimization for any pair of models:

The return is easy to estimate once you know the split. If nine in ten requests stay on a model that costs 1% as much, the bill drops by about 89%, which is what we measured.

Several inference providers have multi-model routing built in, and most AI gateway platforms support model routing too, so you don't have to write your own. As of November 2026, these are the top model routing solutions for AI apps. Each name links to the tool's model routing docs:

Hosted model routing platforms for large language models decide for you, which is the fastest way to start. A hand-built router like ours takes more work, but you choose the signal and you can see why every request went where it did.

An LLM router is the component that does model routing for large language models. It can be a function in your own code, like the one in this guide, a hosted model such as Microsoft Foundry's model router, or a feature of an AI gateway.

Smart model routing is another name for intelligent model routing: a model, not a hand-written rule, decides where each request goes. Dynamic model routing means the same thing.

The ROI of AI model routing depends on how much of your traffic a cheaper model can handle. In our test, a 0.8 threshold kept 89.5% of messages on the cheap model and cut the cost per 1,000 messages from US$1.96 to US$0.22, for about a point of accuracy. The build was about 60 lines of code.

OpenRouter's auto model routing works through its Auto Router. You set the model to openrouter/auto, and a decision model picks a model and a reasoning effort for each request. You pay the standard rate of whichever model it selects, with no extra fee.

OpenClaw model routing is a primary model plus an ordered list of fallbacks. In the OpenClaw model routing configuration you set agents.defaults.model.primary and agents.defaults.model.fallbacks, and an agent can override the fallback list. When a model fails, OpenClaw first rotates auth profiles on the same provider, then moves to the next model on the list, as its model failover docs describe. That makes it fallback routing, not cost-based routing.

An AI gateway simplifies model routing by putting every model behind one API, one set of keys, and one place for logs. Your app calls the gateway, and the routing rules live in config instead of code, so changing a model doesn't need a deploy.

Mid-stream failover is when a router switches models after a streamed response has already started, because the first model failed partway through. It's the hardest kind of failover, because the user has already seen part of an answer. Many routers only fail over before the first token arrives and return an error after that, so check what your gateway does.

Our enterprise and data center SSD reviews show how a drive performs in real-world service, not just on its best day.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After a hiatus of several years, Tom's Hardware is expanding its in-depth testing and analysis to enterprise and data center SSDs. Consumer SSDs and conventional hard drives lack the performance and endurance for extensive AI workloads. This has increased demand for enterprise SSDs. Form factors like U.2, U.3, E3.S, and E1.S, once rare, are now common in high-end workstations and home labs. These enterprise storage solutions provide higher capacities, better performance, and greater reliability than consumer drives.

Comprehensive reviews of enterprise and data center SSDs are therefore more important than ever. Buying a drive of this caliber is a major decision and a significant investment for organizations of all sizes. With this latest venture, Tom's Hardware will continue to help buyers make informed decisions with comprehensive reviews and performance analyses.

Reviewing enterprise and data center SSDs required us to build a workstation that could truly challenge these drives. This project would not have been possible without the support of several component makers, including Asus, Corsair, G.Skill, Icy Dock, and Noctua, who made the workstation a reality, a challenging prospect in the current market.

The Xeon 658X, a 24-core, 48-thread chip from the Granite Rapids family with a 4.9 GHz boost clock speed, powers the testing workstation. Single-core performance and total core count matter just as much when reviewing storage. Clock speed determines how many IOPS one core can push and how fast it answers a single request, while 24 cores let us spread a drive's load across eight fio jobs and later test several drives at once. The Xeon 658X ticks all those boxes.

We've entrusted the Xeon 658X's cooling to Noctua's NH-U14S DX-4677 air cooler, which supports both LGA4677 and LGA4710 sockets. The single-tower CPU air cooler delivers high-performance cooling at very low noise levels thanks to its dual NF-A15 140mm cooling fans.

With its server-grade components and robust enterprise features, the Asus Pro WS W890E-Sage SE is the ideal motherboard to pair with the Xeon 658X. Its extensive feature set includes an onboard MCIO x8 connection, split into x4/x4, that allows for evaluating two U.2 or U.3 drives. The board also provides seven PCIe 5.0 expansion slots (six at x16 and one at x8) to fully leverage the Xeon 658X’s 128 PCIe 5.0 lanes, so the SSDs and graphics cards never hit bandwidth limits.

We're still in need of support for an RTX Pro graphics card, which would be an invaluable asset for enterprise SSD reviews for real-world AI workloads, but our current test platform allows us to test the most important aspects of performance.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Despite the memory and storage shortage, G.Skill and Corsair have stepped up to provide crucial components for our enterprise SSD test bench. G.Skill sent a G5 DDR5-6400 C32 64GB (4x16GB) R-DIMM memory kit, which runs at the stock DDR5-5600 speed. The decision to keep it at the JEDEC baseline has nothing to do with the memory kit: Memory frequency does not affect performance in enterprise SSD tests.

Meanwhile, Corsair provided the speedy MP700 Pro XT 2TB, one of the best SSDs, as the test bench's primary storage. The PCIe 5.0 drive delivers sequential read and write speeds up to 14,900 MB/s and 14,500 MB/s, respectively, and keeps the workstation snappy, especially when it hammers enterprise drives all day long.

No power supply is better suited to the job than the Corsair HX1500i. It offers a massive 1,500W capacity with plenty of headroom for future upgrades, and it runs quietly and stably, two qualities that matter when you keep the test system running for countless hours. It is an 80 Plus Platinum power supply with at least 89% efficiency at full load.

Testing enterprise drives in a standard PCIe expansion slot is more convenient. For that purpose, Icy Dock has provided us with the ExpressSlot Elite MB221E5P-B and MB303E5P-B to accept one and two E1.S SSDs. Unlike other generic designs on the market, these adapters feature tool-less installation mechanisms that don't require removing them from the PCIe expansion slot, and they include 50mm fans for active cooling.

The list also included the ToughArmor MB218E5P-B, a 5.25-inch drive bay that supports up to eight E1.S SSDs for future projects exploring RAID arrays. To test E3.S drives, we use a JMT ADT-R27C adapter strapped to a Noctua NF-F12 industrialPPC-24V-3000 IP67 PWM cooling fan, which can run at 12V and still provide substantial airflow to actively cool the SSDs under evaluation.

A TGDGamer E-ATX test bench houses the hardware for the enterprise SSD system. It may not look flashy, but it does the job, and that is what matters. As an open-air test bench, it is easy to install and remove hardware, and it eliminates variables when it comes to heat generation and buildup. The test bench also supports motherboard form factors up to SSI-EEB, so the Asus Pro WS W890E-Sage SE feels right at home.

A spec sheet only tells you what a drive can do on its best day. The real world is not as pretty. The drive is mostly full, garbage collection is constantly at work, and multiple workloads are hitting it simultaneously. Our enterprise benchmark suite is built to measure the drive in service: at steady state, under load, through write bursts, and with other jobs running. Every drive gets the same automated run on the same test bench. Every result is stored with the exact parameters that produced it, so we can trace how we got there.

This can take some time. A full run takes most of a day on a 7.68TB PCIe 5.0 drive, and it writes the drive end-to-end many times over. To make life a bit easier on us, the benchmark can be run unattended, and it’s crash-resumable from the last stage. A shorter express mode handles bench checks and quick triage. When a result looks odd, express runs and variant runs, which repeat just the tests in question or change one setting, help us find the cause before we take it to the vendor.

We run the bench on a custom Debian 13 image that we build and maintain ourselves, with the suite copied in at build time. This image gets installed on the bench’s OS drive, and the kernel is pinned so every drive has the same software stack. Right now that’s Linux 7.2.6 from Debian backports, because Debian 13’s stock 6.12 kernel predates support for the Asus Pro WS W890E-Sage SE’s Intel E610 10GbE controller. The suite is also designed to be run remotely if needed, but we need a tester at the bench to switch the drives, of course.

Debian is our pick over Ubuntu for a few reasons: it has fewer background services, no Snap, and no unattended upgrades by default. We hold the kernel so nothing changes between runs. We remove AppArmor and turn off scheduled jobs that could fire mid-run, like the weekly TRIM timer and the drive-health service that polls every drive, and the suite records any that are still running. The suite also turns off NVMe autonomous power-state transitions (APST) at the start of every run and checks that the drive reports it off, so no drive drops into a low-power state during an idle gap and adds wake-up latency to the next I/O.

Every test runs in fio 3.42, using io_uring and direct I/O against the raw block device. This means no filesystem and no page cache sitting between fio and the drive; the bench’s 64GB of RAM never caches a byte of test data. We compile fio from source because Debian’s packaged version lacks SPRandom, which we’ll get to in short order.

Some enterprise storage reviews run application profiles in Oracle’s Vdbench, but we decided to go full fio. This makes for a more consistent suite with one set of latency accounting across every chart. And fio is open source and actively maintained, while Vdbench hasn’t had a new release since 2018. Vdbench profiles are built from the same parts fio uses, such as read/write ratio, block-size mix, and random or sequential access, and our mixed-workload sweeps cover the common read/write ratios at 4K and 8K, though not mixed-block-size profiles. Running Vdbench ourselves wouldn’t make our results comparable to other outlets’ anyway, since they use different hardware, preconditioning, and profile definitions.

A key element of benchmarking SSDs is making sure the test drive is at steady state, with the performance it can sustain rather than the peak you can see after a fresh erase. Our first stage is a secure erase using nvme format. If a drive refuses, the suite falls back to a plain format, then a full-drive discard, and records which one it used.

Next comes preconditioning. SPRandom, SanDisk’s open-source contribution to fio, is a faster alternative to filling the drive over and over for hours. It takes user capacity and over-provisioning (OP) as inputs, which SanDisk says lets it reach a steady-state layout in just one pass. Overprovisioning (OP) is inferred from the capacity point or given by the tester. NVMe drives don’t report their raw flash, so a 7.68TB drive like our first one, assumed to sit on 8TiB of actual NAND, works out to having about 14.5% OP.

We don’t take SPRandom’s result on faith. A steady-state gate follows, running 4K random writes at queue depth 256 (QD256) until the drive’s write speed stops moving. It uses the two tests from the Storage Networking Industry Association’s (SNIA) steady-state rule, with limits ten times tighter, applied to the last 30 minutes: split into five six-minute rounds, the round averages must sit within 2% of one another, and the trend line across the window can’t move more than 1%.

That takes a while, but that’s the point: an earlier, looser version of the gate passed our first drive while its write speed was still climbing. The whole settling curve is saved with every run, and a drive that doesn’t settle within three hours is marked as not reaching steady state, though the run continues. As a backstop, the suite repeats the four-corner 4K write after the random sweep, hours later, and flags the run if the two results differ by more than 3%.

The suite also checks its own results as it goes. If a test’s slowest moment falls below 70% of its average, that test is run again on the spot. If a point in the random sweep lands more than 10% off the curve set by its neighbors, it’s measured again before the drive is erased for the sequential tests. Both results are kept, and any re-test is noted in the review.

Following the random tests, the tool erases the drive again and writes it end to end once, with 128K sequential writes at QD64. We run it before the sequential and AI tests, so the drive is conditioned with the kind of I/O we’re about to measure. It matters because sequential writes can swing wildly on a randomly written drive: on our first test drive, the fastest result was more than three times the slowest. After a sequential fill, they held flat from QD2 through QD1024, at both 128K and 1MB.

Drives are formatted to 512-byte logical blocks when they support it, so our block size sweep can go down to 512-byte transfers, which a 4K-native namespace can’t issue. SNIA’s enterprise IOPS test uses 512-byte transfers too, and Kioxia pitches 512-byte access for its GP Series, which is aimed at GPU-initiated storage. Drives without a 512-byte format are tested as shipped, and their sub-4K results are marked unsupported. The switch happens in the same format command as the secure erase, so it adds no time.

A fast enough SSD will happily benchmark your host, or your tools, instead, and our early runs turned up three ways that could happen. The first is CPU idle states, or C-states: waking our Xeon 658X cores from their deepest idle state can take up to 170 microseconds, and at QD1 that directly impacts the results. Holding the cores awake for the whole run cut the first drive’s average 4K QD1 read latency by about a sixth.

The second is the CPU itself: a single fio job can saturate its core well short of what a PCIe 5.0 drive can manage. Split across eight jobs, the same test runs several times faster, so random tests now spread their queue depth across up to eight fio jobs. That’s also why fast cores, and enough of them, matter for this test bench.

The third is fio itself. By default, fio seeds its random generator the same way every run, so it replays the same sequence of random offsets, and each random write test rewrites the previous test’s blocks in the same order. On our first drive, that was the main reason 4K random writes came in at more than twice the sustained figure. Every random test now runs with a fresh random seed.

What we don’t change matters, too. Completions arrive by interrupt rather than polling, and the IOMMU stays in its default mode, because that’s how most applications use a drive. On our first drive, polling and IOMMU passthrough would each have shaved some latency off 4K QD1 writes.

Interrupts also set a ceiling at the other end of the scale: this bench tops out near 2.8 million 4K random read IOPS, and adding fio jobs doesn’t lift it, though polling does. So on the fastest drives, our 4K random read result can sit below the spec sheet’s, and that’s our bench’s limit, not the drive’s. Our low queue depth latencies, writes especially, run higher than a tuned host would show, but they’re closer to what you’d actually see. To show the drive’s limit at both ends, the suite repeats three tests with polled I/O at the end of the random stage: the 4K random read peak, plus 4K random reads and writes at QD1. Those results get their own charts and never mix into the main ones.

4K random read and write at QD256; 128K and 1MB sequential read and write at QD64

The headline numbers, measured on a preconditioned drive instead of fresh out of the box

4K and 8K, QD1 to QD4096, eight read/write mixes from 100% read to 100% write

Servers rarely run flat out; QD1 is what latency-bound software feels, and 8K is a common database page size

4K random read at QD1024 across eight jobs, plus 4K random read and write at QD1, all with polled I/O

What the drive can do with the host’s interrupt overhead out of the way; the results to set against a spec sheet

128K and 1MB sequential reads and writes, QD1 to QD1024

How much parallelism a drive needs to reach full speed; low queue depths are what a single restore or scan gets

Random reads and writes from 512 bytes to 1MB at QD32

Writes smaller than a drive’s mapping unit can pay a read-modify-write penalty; large blocks show its bandwidth cap

4K and 8K reads and writes at QD1, QD4, and QD16, from the median (P50) to the 99.999th percentile (P99.999)

Averages hide the stalls applications notice; at a million IOPS, the slowest 0.001% is still ten requests a second

4K 70/30 at 13 queue depths from QD1 to QD4096, plotted as a share of the drive’s own peak

Finds the load where latency starts to climb

Whether sustained writes hold a flat line or sawtooth as garbage collection struggles

4K QD1 reads for four minutes right after a heavy write burst

How far reads drop after heavy writes, and how fast they recover

Write bursts after idle gaps of 0 to 300 seconds

How much write speed a quiet spell buys back

4K QD1 log writes with a sync after each, a random 8K 70/30 mix at QD16, and a 256K sequential read scan

Log-write latency, page I/O, and analytics scans, without benchmarking a database’s RAM cache

Checkpoint-sized saves and restores modeled on 8B- and 70B-class models (105GiB over eight writers, 912GiB over 64)

Time until the slowest writer finishes, which is when a real checkpoint would be done

A fixed 4K read load, alone and during a checkpoint save

What a checkpoint does to serving traffic on the same drive

Drive temperature through the hour-long sustained write test

Measured against the drive’s own warning and critical thresholds

Some things are out of scope by design. We review drives; we don’t certify them. That rules out dual-port failover, power-loss validation, and single-root I/O virtualization (SR-IOV), which belong in a qualification lab. We’ll add tests when they prove themselves on the bench. Many of the tests in the table are old standbys and still earn their place. Others are newer and require further explanation.

For one, we chart latency against load: the 70/30 4K mix from the random sweep, at 13 queue depths from QD1 to QD4096, with throughput shown as a share of the drive’s own peak. Tail latency climbs as the load grows, and the point where it turns sharply upward (on our first drive, that was write latency) represents the practical operating ceiling, or the load you can plan a server around without giving up low latency.

We also test write-to-read recovery, which measures how a drive’s reads hold up after a heavy write burst. A few minutes of 4K random writes at QD256 leave the drive with cleanup to do, and QD1 reads are then timed over the next four minutes. If a drive is still busy collecting garbage, it will read slower before climbing back; the extent and duration of the drop are the result.

Idle recovery measures what downtime buys back. After a minute of sustained writes, the drive sits idle for anywhere from zero to five minutes, then a 10-second write burst, logged second by second, shows whether background cleanup freed enough blocks to run faster.

Checkpoint interference shows what an AI checkpoint does to anything else sharing the drive. A light 4K read load, which stands in for inference or database queries, runs alone, and then again during a checkpoint save. Each read is timed from when it was due, not when it was sent, so reads stuck waiting behind a stalled one count too.

Lastly, we test thermals. Drive temperature is tracked through the hour-long sustained write test and charted against the drive's own warning and critical thresholds.

The full process means every drive is treated the same and compared fairly, with a method built to give the same results run after run. This allows us to provide a level playing field built on accurate and repeatable performance characterization.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

#10559. Google Pixel rolls out new ‘Family Hub’ feature with useful controls [Gallery]

First appearing earlier this month, Google Pixel phones are rolling out a handy new Family feature in the Settings menu. Here’s what it can do.

Rolling out now via what appears to be a server-side update (tied to Google Play Services), Google Pixel phones are adding a “Family” section to the Settings menu. The feature is appearing for us on a Pixel 11 Pro XL running the October 2026 update (not Android 17 QPR2), while a Pixel 10 Pro has also just surfaced the new UI.

This new section, which Google has previously referred to as “Family Hub,” gives users a quick place to see their family members and settings in one central location. It shows a list of your family members – child accounts and anyone attached to your Google One family plan – with controls over Google One storage, Google Play Family Library, shared payment methods, and “Family events” in Google Calendar.

You can also see the live location of any family members you are sharing your location with or start sharing your location with another family member. You can also easily remove or add members to your family plan via this new menu. Each family member gets their own page where you can see benefits shared, location, and also if they are a “parent” in the family – with “remove” and “transfer” options available.

I've had it for a few weeks, and while it's surely something useful for those with a "family setup", it's IMHO a major annoyance for those who don't (who, I suspect, are the vast majority).

Couldn't they have it show up only for those "in a family"?

It's there, at the top, doing absolutely nothing useful for the vast majority of users, and pushing down all other options.

While this won’t be helpful for all users, it’s certainly full of useful shortcuts and tools for those at the center of a family plan and looking to share benefits with their household.

As far as we can tell, this is widely rolled out to Pixel owners at this point. As mentioned, we’re seeing it across multiple devices, and 9to5Google readers mentioned they’re also seeing it live even prior to installing the latest system update. The folks at Droid-Life also mention now seeing it on their Pixels.

#10557. Pixel Tag is a brilliant Band-Aid on a Find Hub network Google can’t easily fix

The Google Pixel Tag is here and, at least so far, it’s really good. But fresh hardware and some new features can’t fix the core problem. Google’s Find Hub network has been restricted from the very start, and it’s not something Google can easily fix.

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

The good news on Pixel Tag: this is a well-built tracker that, unlike others, is the clear product of the hardware having direct access to the software. Little touches like being able to adjust ringer volume on the fly combine nicely with UWB – the latter something we rarely see on third-party devices largely due to Apple – all things that elevate the Find Hub tracker to what we called the new “gold standard.”

The bad news: this is still a Find Hub tracker.

Google’s Find Hub network has improved dramatically over the two years since its launch. Where Google originally had to admit that tracking performance was lacking (a nice way of putting it, frankly), improvements started arriving in 2025. We noticed that the network seemed to be working much better a few months after its wide rollout, and Google later confirmed speed improvements.

But, ultimately, the Find Hub network is still reeling from how it debuted.

Again, it’s gotten better since then, but it’s still not as good as it should be. Samsung’s network, limited solely to Galaxy phones, shouldn’t outperform Google’s. Find Hub trackers should be just as good as AirTags and the third-party trackers that work with Apple’s network. They’re just not, and I truly do believe it all boils down to this initial choice. One of the reasons this is getting better is that Google has been pushing users to the “all areas” setting during the setup of a new device – I’ve noticed this on some recent devices I set up, but it’s not been completely consistent just yet. That’s a good step forward but, even if it is the new default, that still requires millions of users to upgrade their devices, and we’ve never been in a better time to not upgrade your phone.

All of that is to say, the Pixel Tag isn’t fixing this problem. Surely, it’ll give Google some needed fire to fix the problem, but new hardware isn’t the solution. That’s something I was discussing with 9to5Google readers on our review. UWB is a tentpole feature of the Pixel Tag, but it doesn’t change the network problems, it just improves precision finding. We needed a first-party tracker like this regardless of the state of the network, something that really takes advantage of the platform and pushes it forward, but it’s just a quick fix, a Band-Aid.

What do you think? Have you found the Find Hub network reliable enough, or is there still room for improvement? Sound off in the comments below!

A São Paulo businessman has been arrested by police on suspicion of sending a game console stuffed with explosives to the electronics store of a rival in an attempt to kill him. The device then exploded. On Monday, Military Police executed a warrant against Everton Yoshizumi Lima, who is suspected of perpetrating the outrageous attack, G1 reports.

On October 23, 2026, at around 5:30 pm, a delivery person dropped off a video game console at an electronics repair shop, with the cover that it was being left for modifications requested by the customer. Police say the device had an explosive planted inside that was programmed to detonate upon receipt. "Unaware of its contents, the store owner put the video game console in a drawer and continued working," the report states. The device ignited and exploded, causing severe damage and injuring two people: the owner of the store and a friend who was at the scene. The whole thing was captured on video, which you can see by expanding the tweet below:

Empresário é preso suspeito de enviar videogame com explosivos que detonaram em loja de eletrônicos de Santo André, Grande SP https://t.co/EqXSlkM1J9 #g1 https://t.co/H3naAgNzlVNovember 16, 2026

The device was found to have two explosive charges of the same magnitude inside, but only one of the devices went off, possibly limiting the capacity for damage and injury. The report notes that investigators believe the intent of the device was to kill the victim, given the amount of explosives. The console was delivered to the store by an unsuspecting motorcyclist who was working for a delivery app company and had no idea they were carrying a bomb.

The request appears to have been traced back to Lima, who is the main suspect in the attack. Lima purportedly owns other electronics stores in the area, including two on the same street where this store was recently opened, labeling the motive as "business competition." "The new store had been advertised through a social media campaign and the distribution of flyers," the report states. "Annoyed by the competition, the suspect allegedly decided to eliminate the competitor, according to the line of investigation."

According to the report, Lima was arrested during the course of an investigation into a possible motorcycle theft. It appears that Lima was the owner of the target vehicle, and that police visited in advance, suspecting someone might try to steal it. While checking his data, it was found that Lima had an outstanding arrest warrant for his suspected involvement in the attack.

#10552. How Do You Raise Kids to Be Emotionally Mature?

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Raising a teen is one of the last chapters in parenting. During these years, it’s easy to prioritize your child’s grades, sports, and other accolades, especially as they inch toward leaving the nest.

While achievement is one marker of success, there’s another (often overlooked) skill that shapes your teen’s well-being and development: emotional maturity.

According to clinical psychologist Lindsay Gibson, emotional maturity develops through deep emotional connections, beginning with a child’s parents and primary caretakers. Research shows emotional maturity is the ability to accept and express care, temper big feelings, and make wise choices. Without it, teens are more likely to feel lonely and less satisfied with life, one study found.

In her latest book, How to Raise an Emotionally Mature Child, Gibson points out that “the quality of humanity’s future depends on the emotional maturity of coming generations.”

Juli Fraga: In your book, you mention that humanity’s future will depend on emotional maturity. Why does this matter so much right now?

Lindsay Gibson: Emotional maturity is critical to our well-being as individuals and to the smooth, productive, and innovative aspects of society. When you’re emotionally mature, you can think and anticipate consequences. You can also look back and learn from what you’ve done before. So, you can rely on your thinking to help you deal with reality. Emotional maturity also means tuning into your inner world, including your emotions, imagination, hopes, dreams, connections to other people, and sense of belonging.

JF: Our society places a lot of emphasis on achievement, and less importance on emotional intelligence. How can parents help their teens balance the two?

LG: There’s a real cultural pull in society to prize intelligence and achievement. But when parents get too fixated on these qualities, a child may feel like performance matters more than their thoughts, feelings, and sense of safety.

Kids need to be safely held and supported through the growing-up process. It helps them feel resilient, secure, and confident—qualities they need to take out into the world to function independently. If you get too fixated on the intelligence and achievement parts, you’ve got someone who’s very powerful in one area, but whose emotional side may be underdeveloped. It’s the balance between the two that matters.

JF: In your book, you mention why it’s so important for parents to help their kids develop a sense of self. Can you explain why this matters, especially during the teen years when kids are individuating?

LG: One way teens find out who they are is by discovering who they’re not. Teens might push a limit or break a rule, and it’s all par for the course. What’s important for parents is to be able to recognize and imagine that their child has a separate heart and mind from theirs—a process called “mentalization.”

A self is that inner spark that speaks to us. It says “don’t do that” or “now’s the time to go for it.” It guides you toward what’s most compatible with your individuality and who you are meant to be.

Our sense of self comes from our relationships with people we love and who love us. To feel secure, kids need solid, dependable, connected, and deeply resonant relationships with their parents and caretakers. They need parents who are curious and reflect something back that’s meaningful about who the young person is. When they have that foundation, they have a sense of being a real person inside.

JF: Parents are so busy these days, and overloaded—mentally and emotionally. Raising emotionally mature kids may feel like another big to-do on an already long list. What advice do you have?

LG: Taking time to pause and take your child seriously as a real person will work wonders for their self-confidence and future resilience.

Slowing down takes practice because by the time you’re old enough to have a family, you’ve probably been very goal-directed; parents can be pretty hard on themselves because our culture is so fixated on achievement.

But pulling yourself away from your goal for even a second to tend to your child is something worth practicing because that’s what children’s needs are all about. Kids also develop confidence when they know they matter just as much as anybody else.

JF: What are some behaviors that are normal for teens but that parents might misinterpret as pushback or misbehavior?

LG: Sometimes teens experiment with saying or doing things that clash with their socialization up to that point, such as the language they use or the clothes they wear. These are things that the parent can feel like, “Whoa, I’m uncomfortable with this…”

Here are two meaningful ways to navigate these moments.

Ask your teen questions about what they’re into, such as the styles they like or what they admire about someone they idolize. Try showing interest, not intrusive interest, but just interest in what they like, or why they are experimenting with different things.

Keep in mind that when your teen snaps at you, is in a bad mood, or slams the door, their emotional centers are activated in a way that will probably never be repeated in their life because their brains prioritize emotional development first during this stage.

JF: Can you share more about why a teen’s development follows this trajectory?

LG: Yeah, I know it’s easy to ask, “Why in the world would nature do it that way?” It’s because that’s how important the emotional self and relationships are to our real success as independent adults.

As adults, we have to know ourselves inside and out and use our feelings to guide us. Teens are still trying to work this out. Learning how to handle these emotions takes a lot of practice. They’re finding out what it’s like to feel rage, or what it’s like to be in love, or to feel disappointed. And they’re feeling them in the most intense way they ever will, because the brain is taking on the job of developing them into a really deep, complete human being.

JF: What advice do you have for parents when their teen’s emotions push their own buttons?

LG: Keep in mind that when your teen responds in an adamant, emotional, defiant way, or calls you names, they’re not really in command of those emotions yet.

Instead of coming down hard with words that suggest they’re not a good person, try to respond with: “I’m really interested in how you feel about this. I want to hear your arguments, but when you call me a name, it makes it impossible for me to be available to you. It just doesn’t work. We don’t call each other names in this family. That won’t work when you’re an adult, either.”

The interaction then becomes an education about how people respond to different kinds of approaches. Harshness that stops a teen cold shuts down their impulse to communicate their feelings to other people, which isn’t good for resilience or honest relationships.

JF: What are two things that parents do to foster emotional maturity that can actually backfire?

LG: One is the importance of achievement, of having a good resume. Of course, parents know how hard life is as an adult. We feel it’s our duty to convey these realizations to our kids. But when it becomes something that feels like everything else will fall into place if you do these things first or achieve X, we are not helping our kids with the emotional or interpersonal side of life. We’re creating a one-dimensional kind of person.

Sometimes parents also think that if they don’t give their child severe consequences or punishments, the child won’t learn and mature. When you try to convey information through punishment, you actually teach a child to defer to other people’s anger and control. Of course, we want them to be aware of consequences, but sometimes we can go too far. You can’t have a full, right life if you’re always anticipating that somebody might be mad at you.

Juli Fraga, Psy.D., is an AEDP-trained psychologist in San Francisco. She supports parents with kids of all ages. She is also the co-author of the new book, Parents Have Feelings, Too.

#10551. Phison Pascari X201P 15.36TB SSD Review: Fast PCIe 5.0 write performance for AI and ingest servers

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Phison’s Pascari X201P meets its rated specs and pairs 12 GB/s sequential writes with a steady 600K random write IOPS, making short work of AI checkpoints. It gives ground at low and moderate queue depths, where QD1 writes and mixed workloads lag, and it ran warm on our bench.

Mixed-workload performance trails at middle QDs

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Phison is serious about enterprise storage, and the Pascari X201P was not released lightly; it came in the night, under NDA, alongside this week’s Supercomputing 2026 event. The drive is part of the Performance X-Series, which handles intensive workloads where consistency is the name of the game. We were nevertheless pleasantly surprised by its write performance, with a steady 12 GB/s for sequential and over 600,000 random write IOPS, considering it’s marketed at the 1 drive write per day (DWPD) endurance point.

Part of that surprise comes from the fact that this is Phison’s read-intensive PCIe 5.0 data center drive, a successor to the X200P. It comes in the U.2 form factor — which can be more convenient in many cases — from 1.92TB to 30.72TB, and an E3.S version from 3.84TB, in both single- and dual-port models. It stands next to its mixed-use sibling, the X201E, which is the one rated for 3 DWPD. What makes the X201P exciting, in part, is its own 16-channel X2 controller and the flash that’s inside.

This drive is aimed at AI, analytics, and database servers, with its case made in the 4K 70/30 mix at higher queue depths. It passed Phison’s one-million-IOPS mark at QD128 in our testing of the 15.36TB model, and the drive met all of its rated specs, though its random read rating required polled I/O to reach on our bench. It faced challenges with QD1 writes, log commits, and the middle range of our mixed sweeps. Given its blend of performance, capacity, and endurance, we consider it a very competent entry from the company that was perhaps better known in the consumer SSD market until recently.

The drive met or beat every rated performance figure that we can test, which should be the target of any enterprise SSD released today. If there’s an exception, it’s due to how we test; Phison lists a ceiling of 3,250K IOPS for 4K random read, which is higher than our bench allows with our interrupt-driven I/O, which we use rather than a polled approach. We also run a polled test on every drive for cases like this, and our result was 3,283K IOPS at the same queue depth Phison specifies, which shows the drive does exceed its specification.

Datacenter NVMe SSD v2.0, with v2.6 telemetry features

PCIe 5.0 x4, single port (2x2 dual-port models offered)

ISE, SED (TCG Opal 2.02) or non-SED; AES-XTS 256-bit

512-byte and 4,096-byte, each with optional metadata

Six: 25 W, 22 W, 20 W, 18 W, 15 W and 12 W maximum

Host-controlled thermal management supported

Our sample is in the U.2 form factor, 15mm thick. We have the single-port SKU, ISE model XP208H0815T3P3216T310, with firmware X2WM6026. Some data we previously saw from the drive was reportedly on X2WM6025, but we confirmed X2WM6026 for all relevant results. We mounted the drive on a PCIe 5.0 x4 add-in card adapter for U.2, standing upright in the slot.

Phison did not provide firm hardware details, but we were able to discover some things on our own. The flash was probably the easiest, as the company claimed it was a 321-layer part. That can only be SK hynix’s V9 TLC, which is not flash we’ve tested before in any product. It’s exceptionally fast and uses a three-deck design, which is relatively new for TLC flash. The flash could be credited with some of the exceptional performance measurements we see on the drive.

As for the proprietary controller, we’ve heard that it’s using a combination of the standard Arm Cortex-R5 — these would be the main cores — with AndesCore RISC-V on the side. We’ve seen the latter before on the Phison E28 consumer SSD controller, which did have some enterprise-like aspects to it. The E28 is the newer design, built on a 6nm process with Andes’ newer N45 cores alongside the N25F, while the X2 is a 12nm part that, as far as we can tell, sticks with N25F-class co-processors.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

In this case, the X2 has twice the flash channels, which enables a superior level of performance out of TLC flash, as in enterprise you do not rely on pSLC. That mix of cores sets the X2 apart from other 16-channel PCIe 5.0 enterprise controllers: Marvell’s Bravera SC5 and Silicon Motion’s SM8366 rely on Arm cores throughout, whereas Phison pairs its Cortex-R5 cores with small RISC-V co-processors.

#10548. Google Maps rolling out travel itineraries & list search for Ask Maps

Ahead of the holiday season, Google Maps is launching a handy travel itineraries feature alongside a slew of other new capabilities for Ask Maps.

Ask Maps can now build travel itineraries that feature numbered map pins for each stop. It supports everything from shopping excursions to multi-day road trips with several stops. When used for the latter, Google Maps will generate a day-by-day schedule with tips and the numbered pins that aid visualization. Plans can be shared with others and exported to Google Docs on desktop.

Once on the trip, Ask Maps will show directions and real-time details, including live traffic conditions, whether a place is open, and “even what the vibe is at your next stop.” You can conversationally have Google add more stops to the itinerary.

Meanwhile, Ask Maps can now tap into your saved lists to find bookmarked places and take action. This lets you ask: “Which spots saved in my lists have availability for five people in the next 30 minutes?”

Before you start directions, you’ll see familiar “Know Before You Go” tips that surface information about your route, like parking locations, car charging options, weather, potential delays, and tolls.

New agentic capabilities let Ask Maps find online beauty, pet care, auto, and home repair appointments, as well as call local businesses on your behalf. For example, “Find me a 4 p.m. nail appointment near me” returns a list of open slots.

Finally, Ask Maps is expanding to 30 more countries across Europe, including Spain, Germany, and the UK.

These updates are rolling out starting today on Google Maps for Android and iOS. Travel itineraries and list search are available in all 200+ Ask Maps countries. Know Before You Go in directions and appointment booking are coming first to the US.

#10546. How to Migrate an Elementor Site to the WordPress Block Editor

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Elementor made your site easy to build. It may also be making it slow. Here's how to move a site onto the Block Editor without breaking URLs, losing content or dropping out of Google.

Page builders solved a real problem. Before the Block Editor matured, they were the easiest way to give clients drag-and-drop layouts. Many of those sites are still running today, and a lot of them are slow on phones.

The data backs that up. According to the HTTP Archive Core Web Vitals Technology Report for June 2026, 36.8% of Elementor sites pass Core Web Vitals on mobile. Sites built with the Block Editor pass 53.0% of the time. That's correlation, not proof, since hosting and setup matter too. But in my experience, it matches what happens when you remove the builder from a slow site.

My reason for migrating sites off Elementor is simple: **page builders make websites heavy. **Having said that, Elementor has improved a lot. Its performance settings, Flexbox Containers, and asset-loading options cut much of the bloat, and a carefully tuned Elementor site can pass Core Web Vitals. But tuning only goes so far. You’re still shipping the builder’s framework, wrapper markup and scripts, and every plugin or Elementor update is a chance for the speed to slip again.

For small business sites that don’t need drag-and-drop editing, rebuilding the same design on the WordPress Block Editor (Gutenberg) removes that weight at the source, without changing what the client sees.

This guide follows the order I use. It assumes you're comfortable with WordPress admin, WP-CLI and a staging site. If you need to set those up first, SitePoint's guide to the ultimate WordPress development environment covers the tooling.

Not every Elementor site needs to move. Migration makes sense when:

It's harder to justify when the site leans on Elementor Pro features you'd need to replace, such as complex popups, dynamic loops or WooCommerce templates. Those can all be rebuilt, but the job gets bigger fast.

You can't prove a migration worked without "before" numbers. Capture these on the live site first.

Crawl the site with a tool such as Screaming Frog, and export every indexable URL with its title, meta description, H1 and canonical tag. You can also pull published pages and posts with WP-CLI:

Run Google PageSpeed Insights, which uses Lighthouse for its lab scores, on the homepage and two or three key templates. Save the mobile lab scores and, if the site has enough traffic, the field data from real users.

Export the last three months of pages and queries from Google Search Console. That's what you'll compare against after launch.

Elementor stores each page's layout as JSON in the elementordata post meta field, and marks builder pages with elementoredit_mode. This command lists every page that uses the builder:

Then list everything else Elementor controls, which is easy to miss:

On the small business sites I've migrated, the inventory usually turns up more than the owner expects. The pages are the easy part. The surprises are saved templates nobody remembers creating, a popup still running on every page, add-on widgets from plugins that were installed once and forgotten, and contact forms that live inside Elementor Pro rather than a form plugin. Finding those before the rebuild saves a broken launch.

Work on a staging copy. Install a block theme, such as Twenty Twenty-Five or a lightweight starter theme, and move the Elementor global styles into theme.json. That way colors, fonts and spacing are defined once, instead of in every widget.

Copy the hex values and font names from Elementor's Site Settings so the new site matches the old one. Rebuild the header and footer as template parts in the Site Editor. If you'd rather write a theme yourself, SitePoint's series on building a WordPress theme from scratch covers the basics.

Most Elementor sites repeat the same few sections: a hero, a services grid, testimonials and a call to action. Build each one once as a block pattern, then reuse it on every page. Patterns are plain block markup, so you don't need React to write them, whatever you may have heard about React and WordPress development.

In a block theme, any PHP file in the /patterns folder with a header comment is registered automatically:

For page content, there's a useful shortcut. Elementor saves a plain HTML copy of each page into post_content. Open a page in the Block Editor, and the old content usually appears in a Classic block. Choose Convert to blocks, then delete the leftovers and rebuild the layout with your patterns. It keeps the copy and images, so you only redo the design.

The difference shows up in the page source. Here's typical front-end markup for a single heading on an Elementor page, using the older section and column layout:

Newer Elementor versions with Flexbox containers and the optimized markup settings trim some of those wrappers, but most older sites still carry all of them. Multiply that by every element on every page, and the weight adds up.

To speed up this step, I built a free Elementor to Gutenberg converter. Paste in a page's elementordata JSON from Step 2, and it returns block markup you can paste into the Block Editor's code editor. It handles headings, text, images, buttons, spacers, dividers and column layouts, and lists anything it can't convert, such as forms and popups, for you to rebuild by hand. It runs entirely in your browser, so the page data never leaves your computer.

The code is rarely what holds a migration up. Content is. Once clients see their site being rebuilt, many decide it's time to rewrite their pages too, and the project ends up waiting on copywriters and approvals. My fix is to separate the two: migrate the existing content as it is, launch, then update the copy afterwards. That keeps the speed and SEO gains on schedule, and it means any ranking change can be traced to either the migration or the new copy, not both at once.

This is the step that decides whether rankings hold.

To spot-check a redirect or a status code from the command line:

When staging matches the live site, deploy it. Then:

Leave the old elementordata meta in place for a few weeks, in case you need to roll back. Once you're confident, take a backup and remove it to shrink the database.

Run PageSpeed Insights again straight away for lab scores. Field data takes longer: the Chrome UX Report uses a rolling 28-day window, so give it a month before judging real-user results. Compare Search Console clicks for the same period before and after launch.

In my migrations, the biggest change is usually page weight and loading time, because the builder's CSS, JavaScript and wrapper markup are gone. What doesn't always improve is server response time. If the site is on slow shared hosting, the first byte still arrives late, and that needs a hosting fix, not a rebuild. Search traffic should hold steady if the URLs and metadata were protected. If it drops, the Step 5 crawl comparison is the first place to look.

Moving off Elementor isn't a one-click job, but it isn't a full redesign either. Record a baseline, inventory what the builder controls, rebuild with patterns, and protect every URL. Most of the speed gain comes from removing code the site never needed. For what to tackle after that, see these practical web performance techniques. Most of the risk comes from rushing the SEO checks. Done carefully, the client gets the same site, a faster one, and an editor that comes with WordPress itself.

***Author Bio: ***Fahad Raza is a WordPress developer and SEO consultant based in Sydney. He has built websites since 2005, worked in SEO for more than 18 years, and spent eight and a half years on the IKEA Australia and New Zealand website.

#10545. Save serious money with RedPocket Mobile’s $5 Black Friday plans

You’re not going to believe this, but there’s one part of your life that’s not actually getting more expensive. If you switch to RedPocket Mobile during its Black Friday sale, you can ditch your costly carrier and get a plan that starts at $5 per month. It’s not too good to be true: RedPocket has been around for 20 years, and unlike most MVNOs, it’s independently owned and operated. That gives it the ability to offer you real network choice when you sign up for a new plan (we’ll explain more later), and keeps your costs low. Really low.

But you’re reading this on 9to5Google, so how does it impact you as an Android user? That’s a great question.

RedPocket is offering $150 rebates on Android devices like the Pixel 11, Galaxy S26, Galaxy Z Flip 7, Moto G Play, and more. (You can also get $100 rebates for iPhones if you’re into that sort of thing.)

These offers run from November 9 through the 30th at redpocket.com, so don’t wait too long to save big.

It’s OK if you haven’t heard of RedPocket Mobile, because it has been quietly working for 20 years to improve the way people use their phones. Founded in 2006, the company works with all three major US carriers — AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon — and because it doesn’t own any of its towers, it can pass along significant savings to its customers. Here’s what the company says on its website:

RedPocket was built for price-conscious consumers who want real value and freedom from rigid, high-cost contracts. As a no-contract carrier, we’ve stayed focused on making reliable, transparent, and flexible phone service for everyone. Our plans are designed to fit real lifestyles, whether you’re a heavy data user, a frequent traveler, or just looking to cut your monthly bill.

Most MVNOs are owned by the Big Three, which means you don’t really have a choice of network when you sign up. But RedPocket works with all three major carriers and lets you choose which one works best for your location and usage patterns.

RedPocket offers a tool called CoverageGenius that helps you decide which network is right for you. While you can’t switch between networks once your line is set up, it’s a great way to figure out where to start.

I teased that $5/month cost at the beginning of the article, but here’s the real breakdown: you can get a full year of service for $60 if you’re a light data user. RedPocket’s Annual Essentials and Annual Plus plans get you unlimited calling and texting in the US, along with calling and data roaming in dozens of countries around the world. The main difference is how much data you get: 3GB for Essentials and 20GB for Plus.

Annual Premium ups the ante to unlimited data, plus calling and data roaming in even more countries.

Across all three plans, you’re saving between 33% and 50% off the regular price when you sign up between November 9th and 30th.

RedPocket understands that it may be hard to commit to a full year of service even if it’s super cheap. That’s why it offers monthly plans, too, that can serve as a trial for any of the networks. It costs just $10 for the first month to try any of the three plans, with Monthly Plus rising to $20/month after that and Premium $30/month thereafter (Essentials stays at $10/month).

The Justice and Commerce Departments have announced an investigation into the company previously run by the woman officials have accused of being a Chinese spy while having an affair with Jensen Huang while he was Nvidia's CEO.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A criminal investigation into Megaspeed has been confirmed by the United States Justice and Commerce Departments, with a joint statement saying that they will “bring the full extent of the law against Megaspeed for any criminal conduct that may have occurred.” Authorities believe that Megaspeed is one of a raft of companies that may have circumvented export rules designed to limit the sale of advanced AI chips, like those built by Nvidia, to approved companies and countries. The company is at the heart of the recent Jensen Huang scandal, which saw him resign from the company last week.

Megaspeed was added to Nvidia's no-sell list in June 2025, the statement reveals, but a New York Times report claims that a Malaysian Megaspeed subsidiary continued to receive large shipments of Nvidia chips for a full eight months after that date. It's believed that Nvidia's export-controlled Blackwell and H200 chips were shipped to Speedmatrix, either via third parties or directly from Nvidia itself, before being handed off to China. Nvidia has argued that it was given Commerce Department permission to ship the components to Megaspeed. Notably, Nvidia continues to list Megaspeed as a cloud partner.

The new criminal investigation follows a Commerce Department investigation into Megaspeed that first kicked off in 2024 over possible export violations. By May 2025, it had requested Nvidia's data relating to sales it made to Megaspeed, and by June, officials visited Megaspeed and even interviewed Alice Huang, the now-former executive who is allegedly a Chinese spy. Ms. Huang is also the woman that then-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang had an affair with before resigning his position earlier this month. Following that visit, the Justice Department took the lead in the investigation into Megaspeed.

Joint Statement from Department of Commerce and Department of Justice on Megaspeed Investigation. https://t.co/aAdtN9aDfFNovember 17, 2026

Megaspeed has long been accused of being a front for an AI component smuggling operation. In October 2025, an investigation claimed that it had helped move around $2 billion worth of Nvidia chips into China by importing them and then re-exporting them to the country. Megaspeed says that it complies with all laws and that the woman at the center of the affair scandal, Ms. Huang, is no longer at the company. The Chinese government has also dismissed claims that she was a state spy.

The joint Commerce and Justice Departments' statement pointed to its strong track record when prosecuting chip smugglers to date. 17 defendants across a total of six investigations have been charged so far, with more likely to follow. A Californian man was recently arrested after being accused of smuggling more than $300 million worth of export-restricted Nvidia chips to China. He now faces up to 20 years in prison.
